## Why SWAP?

## Background

Creating and maintaining a SWAP is optional unless a state or territory wants to receive federal State Wildlife Grant (SWG) conservation funding. 
1
 Every U.S. state and territory has a SWAP that is developed, maintained, implemented and updated by a state fish and wildlife agency or other state natural resources agency with fish and wildlife conservation responsibilities. US Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) Office of Conservation Investment is responsible for SWG delivery and SWAP review and approval. All SWAPs must contain at least the Eight Required Elements (Figure 
1
). 2

## Figure 1. SWAP Eight Required Elements

States may organize this content in any way and expand it to communicate state-specific conservation values, needs, and actions. SWAPs can connect conservation priorities across political boundaries, include mechanisms to be nimble in the face of difficult or rapidly emerging conservation questions, illustrate conservation planning best practices
foot_2
 and current state of conservation, and foster partnerships and funding for implementation.
The Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (AFWA) maintains extensive online resources about the SWAP program, its importance and success across the nation, progress reports, states' plans, best practices, and a SWAP Learning Series for coordinators and partners to share lessons learned and good ideas. 
4
 Using these resources, experiences from the SWAP community of practice, and extensive natural resources expertise in Washington state, the Washington SWAP has communicated fish, wildlife, and habitats conservation needs for more than 20 years.
2025 is a milestone year for SWAPs and the conservation network that engages with these plans. This is the 20 th anniversary of the first SWAPs and the 25 th anniversary of the State and Tribal Wildlife Grants programs. In September of this year, the Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies Wildlife Diversity Conservation and Funding Committee submitted a resolution that was passed by the AFWA State Directors reaffirming the value of this third generation of SWAPs and the importance of secure and dedicated funding for implementation. 
5
 Washington Department of Fish and Wildlife (WDFW) has created two previous SWAPs (2005 and 2015) 
6
 ; this is the third edition.

## Purpose

For humans, species and their habitats provide food and forage, cultural and spiritual touchstones, creative inspiration and well-being, clean air and water, recreation and livelihoods. Conserving biodiversity -the full range of life in all its forms, including habitats and systems -is important to all of us. 7 Conservation ensures that plants, fish, wildlife, and the habitats on which they depend can continue to support and inspire humans and be enjoyed by future generations, in whatever way they may legally interact with those resources. The Washington SWAP's purposes are to provide well-supported information to improve biodiversity conservation, guide limited conservation funds, support the WDFW 25-Year Strategic Plan, and improve conservation communication.
The following financial paragraph will be updated when WDFW receives Federal Fiscal Year 2026 information, or when the plan goes to the Regional Review Team, whichever comes first.
In federal fiscal years 2024 and 2025, the total amount of SWG was just over $72 million for all states and territories. Washington received approximately 
$1.3 million in 2024. 11
 To put that amount in perspective for WDFW's conservation needs, WDFW requested $47.6 million in biodiversity conservation funding in the 2023-25 operating budget for partial SWAP implementation and other key biodiversity actions. The agency received partial funding 12 ($24 million for the biennium) to increase the agency's and partners' capacity to recover at-risk species and their habitats, develop efforts to evaluate and manage SGCN, and engage in conservation-crucial public education and outreach. 
13
 Unfortunately, a tough state budget cycle in 2025 and a reduced state revenue forecast permanently reduced WDFW's funding for that work 
14
 . The state fiscal near-term outlook is not positive for restoring the biodiversity funding, but this continues to be a need that WDFW will pursue.
Through implementation and partnerships that have jelled around SWAP conservation actions, these plans have grown into well-accepted guides to good conservation action, acknowledged by and invested in by other state, federal, and private funding sources and partnerships. Most states, including Washington, cobble together many funding sources to implement a fraction of their SWAP. Conservation needs are outpacing the current level of funding and conservation partners' abilities to respond to emerging issues, gain traction on tough issues, and maintain progress.
Nationally, a broad coalition supports increased and sustained conservation funding for diverse fish and wildlife and their habitats, using SWAPs as good blueprints to deliver conservation (AFWA 2016). Having a SWAP is more important now than ever.

## Implement and Support the WDFW Strategic Plan

The SWAP contains many conservation recommendations, some of which are led and owned by other agencies, organizations, Tribes, and communities. The SWAP is a key plan that informs WDFW work, however the SWAP cannot make a commitment to action in a timeframe -there are not enough resources for full SWAP implementation. The SWAP alone is not work-planning or resources allocation for the Department. This plan rings a bell for many important issues and the conservation recommendations in the SWAP speak to those, even if they are not WDFW's to lead or if WDFW cannot begin the work right now. This plan supports the conservation community at large of which WDFW is a part. WDFW has identified the SWAP as a tool to implement a portion of the agency's 25-Year Strategic Plan. 
15
 Related to the Strategic Plan, the SWAP provides
• information for internal agency conservation work-planning and decision-making, • a platform to communicate with interested stakeholders, governments, and partners, and • a framework to report progress and success.

## Improve Conservation Communication

Our best chance of long-term, effective, and durable conservation progress is to connect conservation opportunities across people who can participate in and affect conservation outcomes. This version of the SWAP will look and feel different from the 2015 SWAP to provide easier-to-find information and make meaningful connections among people who want to use the SWAP and the ecologists, biologists, and communities that recognize and define conservation needs.
Despite the name, the SWAP isn't just limited to "wildlife." The Washington SWAP highlights information and conservation actions for wildlife, fish, plants, habitats, ecosystems and landscapes in which people live, work, and play. We are all connected by our lands and waters, a sense of place that feeds us literally and culturally, traditionally and in new ways every day. We eat, breathe, drink, and play, benefitting from all that Washington's natural resources offer. Much of our economy is resourcesbased in agriculture, forestry, fisheries, technology, green energy, open spaces and outdoor recreation (Washington Department of Commerce 2024). All of this is dependent on beneficial and wellcommunicated connections among people and biodiversity.

## Conservation Compass Points

Reviewers' note: this section was provided in Spring 2025 for external reviews Effective conservation is founded on scientific integrity, data, and information. Effective conservation is also influenced by values and big picture themes that overlay more localized issues, human engagement and behavior, and the relationships among humans and the natural environment. These conservation themes and values are introduced in this section as Compass Points, and they guide conservation actions throughout SWAP.
Conservation Compass Points are conservation principles to improve the pace, scale, and effectiveness of conservation. These are not conservation actions or prescriptions by themselves, but they inform and shape the species, habitat, and system conservation actions in the geographic sections of the Plan.
Like a compass heading, these provide direction along the way to our destination: resilient ecosystems and habitats with sustained plant, fish, and wildlife populations.
Ten SWAP Compass Points are described in this section:
• increase the pace and scale of biodiversity conservation
• start 'upstream' to identify and address issues
• acknowledge conservation complexity and 'gnarly' problems
• document and report progress as an important part of success
• promote scientific integrity and transparency
• expand and continue engagement
• prioritize actions that address resilience, connectivity, and OneHealth

## • facilitate effective voluntary conservation action

• value inter-governmental conservation opportunities at all scales, and • monitor, learn, adapt and communicate.

## Increase the pace and scale of biodiversity conservation

People in Washington are connected by our lands and waters, and by a sense of place that feeds us literally and culturally, traditionally and in new ways every day. Beneficial connections among people, fish and wildlife, plants and habitats, air, water, and soils create a natural collaboration that supports us when we eat, breathe, drink, work and play in all that Washington offers. Washington State has recognized strong connections among biodiversity and our healthy and vibrant Washington communities, economy, and recreation opportunities. 
16
 In 2002, the Legislature directed the State to "develop a comprehensive framework to safeguard Washington's rich biodiversity heritage for the benefit of current and future generations." The resulting Biodiversity Conservation Strategy 17 paired well with Washington's Comprehensive Wildlife Conservation Strategy (our state's first SWAP, 2005).
Since SWAP 2015, the conservation, natural resources management, and development communities have better tools (e.g. remote sensing, cloud-based computing) to detect and understand the pace and scale of changes in temperature, water availability, ecosystem shifts and connectivity, invasive species and diseases, and the distribution of development associated with human populations and industry. These are key factors affecting species and habitat sustainability. NatureServe reports that 34% of plant species and 40% of animal species in the United States are at risk of extinction and that 41% of ecosystems are at risk of range-wide collapse. In the same report, more than 18% of assessed ecosystems in Washington state are at risk. 
18
 Losses in habitat availability, usability, and connectivity influence species' ability to adapt and/or recover. Those threats have not changed much in the last 25 years although community awareness, public support, partnerships, and opportunities have improved. 
19
 Recommendations from the 2007 BCS continue to be relevant today:
• include landscape approaches to conservation,
• enhance incentives for voluntary conservation by private landowners, and
• engage citizens in monitoring and conservation action.
We aren't necessarily doing the wrong things for conservation; we're just not doing enough of the right things at a scale and pace to stay ahead of habitat loss, system degradation, and species loss.
In 2023, the conservation community and the state Legislature agreed that new solutions are needed to conserve biodiversity, improve conditions for resilient and sustainable habitats in a cumulative way, better preserve opportunities to stabilize and recover declining fish and wildlife populations, and conserve ecological functions that support us all. 
20
 To exceed current efforts to protect and restore ecosystems across the state, we need to identify options to galvanize, incentivize, or in some circumstances require projects to increase biodiversity and/or resilience in affected ecosystems.
This SWAP presents biodiversity conservation strategies at statewide and ecoregional scales to expand single-species or single-resource protection programs to conservation support at many scales (sites to landscapes) of conservation work, much of which can also improve ecosystem services valued by people (e.g., timber production, flood control, water quality and quantity, recreation, creativity).
Non-habitat interventions (e.g. hatcheries, predator management, reintroductions, captive rearing) may be needed to provide critical support to species on the brink of losing genetic diversity or that are not yet completely supported in natural habitats. These interventions may also help preserve human rights, needs, and cultural heritage. These interventions must be paired with systems-level restoration, habitat enhancement, and natural process protections and improvements. Our ability to stem the tide of 
18

https://www.natureserve.org/bif
19 Dietsch et. al. 2018
. State report for Washington from the research project titled "America's Wildlife Values." 20 WDFW. 2025. Ecological Improvement Framework Proviso Report. 
https://wdfw.wa.gov/publications/02632
 natural system and species loss and improve habitat and species sustainability relies on the collaboration among conservationists and communities where that work needs to occur. Collaboration is necessary to increase the pace and scale of conservation: do the best we can together, more quickly, in the right places.

## Start 'upstream' to identify and address issues

To create long-term and sustainable improvement in habitats and systems that support species and people, conservation challenges must be addressed as far 'up-stream' as possible: identify root causes of existing issues and think about early warning signs for potential problems, rather than just reacting or responding to manage 'down-stream' symptoms and consequences.
Getting to the headwaters of an issue requires that the project developers keep asking questions to understand what could create a durable solution, name what is creating a barrier to progress, define who is affected and can help. Identify the parts of the problem that are technical (more information or data could solve the problem) and which are adaptive (cultural, social, or system influences). It could be different information, people, or places to solve each of those parts.
Example. Northern Leopard Frogs (NLF) have all but disappeared from their historic range in Washington state. A site-based solution would be to only reintroduce frogs where they used to occur. An 'upstream' solution applies the site-based solution and asks questions about why they are in trouble in that site. The 'upstream' solution improves the context of the reintroduction for more durable outcomes: improves habitat sustainability, removes invasive species, and works with land and water managers to ensure long-term stability in the habitats required by NLF. In the NLF recovery work, the reintroduction is paired with upstream solutions to improve progress and the effectiveness of the reintroduction (Figure 
2
). Conservation actions are the most important parts of the SWAP: a collection of informed opportunities to improve the status and sustainability of species, habitats and systems. Actions included in the SWAP may be in progress now, need support for next steps or better decisions, or don't have a clear starting block and need framing to take the first steps. Some actions are harder, riskier, more urgent, or complex: these are 'gnarly' problems -challenging or expansive situations in which a site-based technical solution (e.g. breed and release more, plant more habitat, move them out of the way) isn't going to be wholly effective in the long-term. Solutions may be more difficult because the causes 'upstream' are unclear, it's difficult to identify which first actions would be most helpful (decisionsupport needed), or the issue or proposed solution reveals conflict.
Often, 'gnarly' problems are influenced by values and tradeoffs, need a system-level approach, and require social and cultural support before ever getting to the ecological or technical work. These tougher solutions often have more risk: failure is possible, testing and learning will need to be part of the project plan, available information may be insufficient, and new information may come from nontraditional sources, documentation and communicating progress are necessary and take more time, and decisionmaking transparency is essential. Knowledge, shared priorities and direction, optimism and grace are helpful but sometimes conflicts are deeply embedded in culture, economy, or social values that project biologists and working groups are not well-equipped to address. In addressing 'gnarly problems,' the conservation actions could be to develop a diverse community-based working group, apply structured decision-making, resolve conflict, or build a coalition before defining new or further action on the ground.

## Promote scientific integrity and transparency

"a river can't be known, … the river is not a thing, in the way a Saturn V rocket engine is a thing. (The river) is an expression of biological life, in dynamic relation to everything around it -the salmon within, the violet-green swallow swooping its surface, alder twigs floating in its current, a mountain lion sipping its bank water, the configurations of basalt that break its flow … this suggests a horizon rather than a boundary for knowing, …." -Barry Lopez (2001) Sustainable fish and wildlife rely on habitat and ecosystem health, some of which we can see, study, and understand and some of which will always be a mystery … a horizon.
And yet, conservation action must be based on what we can know even if that knowledge is incomplete so that we act before habitats and the species that depend on them are unrecoverable or lost. The best information we have is supported by scientific integrity, well-applied science -policy interface, guidance from communities of practice, monitoring and adaptation, and documentation.
Science provides critical insights for conservation decision-making, offering data, models, and projections that help assess risk, evaluate options and inform conservation. Actions (decisions) in response to scientific findings ultimately depend on the ability to apply those findings and may include assessments of risk tolerance, opportunity, policy, and cultural values. Science informs but does not prescribe action; data, information, models, and reports state what we think we know, can help us evaluate and propose next steps, and identify uncertainty. That information can also be used to test potential consequences of action or inaction. A final decision may also depend on weighing potential benefits and trade-offs, and it's important that the decision-making process and outcomes are transparent.
WDFW is committed to implementing a robust science-policy interface. The science-policy interface (SPI) includes the social processes between scientists and decision-makers (defined broadly), and is intended to facilitate productive exchanges, joint construction of knowledge, and transparent and inclusive natural resources decision-making (van den Hove 2007).
The most important attributes of an effective SPI include saliency, credibility, and legitimacy (IUCN 2010):
• Salience (or relevance) reflects the ability of the SPI to be responsive to issues on which decision-makers focus and over which they have some control.
• Credibility reflects the believability of jointly constructed knowledge to interested parties, that is, the extent to which affected participants perceive SPI products (facts, theories, ideas, models, and scenarios) as valid.
• Legitimacy reflects perceived fairness, as measured by how well the SPI has considered stakeholders' divergent values and beliefs, and the degree to which the SPI is unbiased in its conduct and fair in its treatment of opposing views and interests.
Further support for a purposeful science-policy interface is articulated in the WDFW Fish and Wildlife Commission's Science Integrity Policy (adopted March 2025) 
21
 . The policy describes expectations of Commissioners and agency staff when conducting, managing, applying, and communicating scientific findings and activities, and describes behaviors that can ensure scientific process and findings that inform decisions are credible, transparent, and unbiased. The policy states:
As a model of behavior, scientific integrity means that individual scientists, institutions, and policymakers uphold these principles in how they gather, analyze, and present information, while fostering an environment where trust in science is built through ethical conduct.
The SWAP provides well-vetted information from expertise, references, resources, plans, reports, and data to identify, scope, inspire, and implement conservation actions. These actions are crafted by a network of ecologists, biologists, peers, and communities. Being transparent about the information considered and why helps maintain trust and accountability. When conservation action decision-makers clearly communicate the scientific basis for actions, uncertainties they face, and influences and constraints on applying the information, this can demonstrate that management decisions are scientifically robust and aligned with policy priorities.

## Expand and continue engagement with purpose

Conservation doesn't happen without people. Durable and effective conservation solutions need people's support and participation. 
22
 Gaining social support begins with an effort and willingness to understand a place including its politics, culture, patterns of human use, and local values, not just the ecological foundations of what we need to do for species, habitats, and systems. Engaging people effectively in conservation can
• identify multiple perspectives, potential partners, and affected stakeholders,
• reveal information, opportunities, barriers, and potential sources of conflict,
• improve equitable access to resources (sites, staff, funds) and outcomes,
• create shared objectives, acquire consent, and continue communication,
• empower stakeholders to identify and be responsible for the outcomes,
• share the success and accomplishment,
• improve accountability for decisions and actions, and
• influence longer-term funding to maintain progress and apply lessons learned.
The most durable conservation solutions include human social and cultural values alongside ecological function and species/habitat restoration and conservation. A broad spectrum of western scientific, indigenous, and local ecological knowledges can be helpful to define a future vision and achieve desired outcomes.
The SWAP lays out conservation actions that depend on people to make good choices: where ecological knowledge meets peers, experts, governments, organizations, and regional and local communities at the table. The perfect recipe for conservation success does not exist in science and information alone. 
23
 It is important for people to be able to see the relationship of proposed conservation to their work, lives, and values for better conservation outcomes.
Champions are important catalysts in conservation action but cannot sustain an effort alone. People all over Washington need to believe in the work and support it, explore participation based on their existing circumstances and experiences-what interests them, what and who they know, who is working on it now, what information is available and vetted, who are the potential partners, and how could social or cultural issues they're already interested in be connected to the conservation problem or action? Exploring connections among people is a fundamental part of planning and implementing a conservation action and can lead to more effective campaigns, products, partnerships, and outcomes. Project planners can ask, "Who can help? Who needs to know? Who might be affected and what do they care about? What other questions should I ask? Who makes the decisions? What will we do if there is conflict?" And, because conservation success takes time and the people change across the lifetime of a project, it's important to document partnerships, strategies, ideas, and progress from the beginning.
Washington is well-positioned to work at all scales: natural resources managers promote the importance of landscape and system conservation actions for resilient and sustainable species and habitats; communities are engaged in local and regional planning that includes care for the natural world and/or locally relevant conservation solutions; and Washington has a strong network of active conservation allies -Tribes, land trusts, hunters and anglers, resource agencies, wildlife watchers, outdoor enthusiasts, conservation organizations, community organizers, and more. Several initiatives such as the Washington Shrubsteppe Restoration and Resiliency Initiative, Pacific Northwest Coast Landscape Conservation Design, and Yakima Basin Integrated Plan are leading the way with multiple partners and at many scales, only possible because of the engagement of people. The potential to share interests, access information and resources, and scale conservation action is unprecedented.
Many of Washington's natural resources-related agencies -local, state, and federal -have missions and/or goals that are supportive of species and habitat conservation and human enjoyment and use of natural resources. When humans' alteration of the system could put a species or habitat at risk, action should be taken to avoid, minimize, or compensate for the harm. Humans are part of the ecosystem, and ecosystem-based management approaches seeks to ensure those impacts are not grossly exploitative or detrimental to the ability of the system to sustain life and meet the needs of its human and non-human inhabitants. Care should be taken that historically underrepresented or marginalized communities do not have inequitable access to the benefits or inequitable share of the burden of environmental harms.
This SWAP revision's engagement through presentations, events, webinars, comment opportunities and surveys is captured in Appendix E. Through more engagement, WDFW received more participation in the Plan's scoping and development and wants to carry that interest beyond revision (Implementation, Reporting Progress, and Update Opportunities section). Engagement with and influence of many conservation partners and groups is evident throughout the conservation actions presented in the SWAP, not just in the outreach events conducted during the revision. See also the last Compass Point: Monitor, Learn, Adapt, and Communicate.

## Prioritize actions that foster resilience, connectivity, and OneHealth

While there are many actions and approaches that are important in biodiversity conservation, three key conservation themes are woven throughout the actions proposed in the SWAP in support of system, habitat and species conservation: resilience, connectivity, and OneHealth. These foundations can support the life-sustaining systems and habitats that enable species' adaption and recovery.

## Resilience

Resilience refers to human communities' and the natural environment's abilities to prevent, withstand, respond to, and recover from a disruption. Washington ecosystems are experiencing the effects of more and different disruptions than were understood just ten or twenty years ago: intense and frequent droughts; earlier, hotter, or longer-lasting summer heat; less of snowpack and earlier snow melt; bigger storms and more destructive flooding and erosion; more frequent and/or hotter wildfires; and significant shoreline changes. 
24
 All of these are reshaping natural resource management in Washington.
The structured way natural resources managers have historically understood and responded to habitat, fish, and wildlife management are now subject to accelerated and intensified change in stressors, patterns, and processes. The conservation and natural resources management community needs to move beyond historical baselines and approach conservation with systems, adaptation, resilience, and long-term planning in mind, for all project scales.
Species' physiological limits are tested as shifting temperatures, altered hydrology, and changing seasonal cues affect survival, reproduction, and migration. Habitat structure, function, and availability are shifting, some becoming more fragmented and/or unsuitable to the species they have historically supported. These disruptions impact food timing and availability, intensity and frequency of disturbances (wildfire and drought), and can favor invasive species. Conservation now requires a framework that considers these interconnected changes at several scales to support strategies that are adaptive and forward-looking.
Understanding why species and ecosystems are vulnerable or potentially less resilient is a critical first step in developing effective conservation strategies. This vulnerability is based on sensitivity, exposure, and the ability to adapt. Some species are highly sensitive to temperature shifts or struggle with repeated exposure to habitat loss due to altered hydrological cycles, increased fire frequency, or sea level rise. Species with low adaptive capacity such as those with specialized habitat need or limited dispersal ability face higher vulnerability to these changes. Practitioners need to identify underlying cause(s) of this vulnerability enables to better target management efforts where they are most needed: protect refugia, enhance habitat connectivity, and implement restoration actions that support resilience.
WDFW incorporated vulnerability assessments into Chapter 5 of the 2015 SWAP considering sensitivity, exposure, and adaptive capacity across most SGCN and related habitats. 
25
 These assessments highlighted species at greatest risk due to factors such as physiological limitations, habitat specialization, and low dispersal ability. From this work, WDFW developed resilience fact sheets for many habitat types (e.g. shrubsteppe, riparian, eastside and westside forests) and species; identified resilience risks and opportunities in agency work 
26
 ; collaborated with Washington Department of Ecology on a state-level resilience strategy 27 ; and continues to grow a responsive and useful resilience body of work (e.g. culvert designs, stream suitability modeling, wildfire response and post-fire restoration information). Since 2019, Washington DNR has conducted and published several land and species resilience resources. 
28
 This and a great deal of new information is considered by Subject Matter Experts (SMEs) and the conservation community in the 2025 SWAP to identify SGCN, important habitats, landscape-level conservation needs, and actions for their conservation.
These resilience challenges influence the things we know about species, habitats, and systems and the things we know how to do (restoration, reintroductions, habitat and species management). Adaptation and resiliency information -how best to respond, what we are learning about impacts and responsesis rapidly evolving, and it gives us more information and tools to consider in our conservation actions. This requires conservation project managers to more deliberately build in monitoring and a nimble process to understand and adapt to new information.
Building resilience into our conservation projects requires rethinking tools and strategies that were built on the assumption of stable conditions and systems, or some historical understanding of "normal." In many cases, conservation must shift from static, preservation-focused approaches towards more dynamic, process-based approaches that anticipate and accommodate change.

## Connectivity among habitats and systems

Ecological connectivity is a broad term for the movement of species and flow/function of natural processes without barriers, and it has profound impacts on biodiversity (Arkilanian et. al. 2020). In Washington, the conservation community usually thinks of habitat -lands and waters -connectivity as the element we can most readily see, influence, and measure. We often think of connectivity as being most important for species that move long distances; it's also important for less mobile species.
Habitat connectivity provides fish and wildlife the opportunity to move throughout their range for many necessary reasons. Often, we think of connectivity for seasonal movement, like elk moving from winter to summer range or salmon moving between ocean and river habitats. Habitat connectivity is also 
25
 WDFW 2015, EcoAdapt 2015 
26 Shirk et. al. 2021
 27 WDFW and Washington Department of Ecology 2024 
28
 WA DNR 
https://dnr.wa.gov/data-dashboards-business-and-reports/wildfire-response-forest-restoration-andcommunity-resilience-account/climate-resilience-washington
 and 
https://dnr.wa.gov/natural-heritageprogram/nhp-climate-change
 including rare plant species-specific vulnerability analyses important for daily movement, following foods, colonizing new areas, finding mates, and being able to move in response to changes such as urban expansion or ecosystem shifts due to wildfire, drought, or sea level rise. Landscape to local connectivity is also essential for plant communities, habitats, and system natural functions: plant gene flow, pollination, and seed dispersal; disease resistance; natural restoration in response to disturbance; surface and groundwater functions, quality, and quantity; and riparian and floodplain functions, to name a few. Connectivity also sustains species important to cultural traditions like hunting and gathering. 
29
 Washington's conservation communities have worked on habitat connectivity issues, information gathering, analyses and modeling, and best practices for decades at species, ecoregion, state and transboundary scales; in response to large-scale habitat loss, disease transmission, and wildfire; and to inform land use, restoration, and conservation priorities. Examples include Washington Wildlife Habitat Connectivity Working Group 2007 -present, Arid Lands Initiative 2009 -present, Washington State Department of Transportation 2023, WDFW 2024, WDFW and WA DoT (Michalak et. al. 2025). Many products have provided good foundations for actions. Because the conservation recommendations cross many jurisdictions, include land and water management types, and are influenced by local support, progress relies heavily on collaborative, voluntary action.

## One Health

The term "One Health" emerged around 2009 and gained more recognition in Washington state between 2016 to 2019 with a re-energized One Health Collaborative: Washington Department of Health, Washington State Department of Agriculture, Washington State Department of Fish and Wildlife, and the U.S. Department of Agriculture -Animal and Plant Inspection Service (APHIS). One Health is a cooperative effort across disciplines working locally, nationally and globally to improve the health of our shared ecosystems: humans, animals, plants, and our environment (Figure 
3
). 30 Using a One Health framework can establish an early warning system and apply proactive actions to protect the health and resiliency of people and wildlife when we don't yet understand cumulative effects and enable people to more readily respond to horizon issues. Such a framework includes prevention, preparedness, surveillance, and coordinated outbreak investigation(s), response(s), and recovery efforts. Including this framework in the lays the groundwork for a One Health approach to help ensure healthy, resilient, future-ready wildlife populations in Washington.
Within this One Health Framework, a Determinants of Health model can be adapted to fish and wildlife health, with the goal of robust species' populations with the capacity to satisfy daily needs of survival and adapt to or cope with a changing environment -in other words, resiliency (Wittrock et al. 2019). This approach recognizes the array of factors that influence healthy populations and can be used to highlight key actions to address future health risks.
While often thought of as a management framework for diseases, three components of OneHealth are most relevant in plant, fish, and wildlife conservation: ecosystem services (Romanazzi et. al. 2023), diseases that affect wild species, and invasive species. The OneHealth approach recognizes the interconnection among people, animals, plants, and their shared environment. This section is only a brief discussion of the topic and then current issues will be related to species and habitats in relevant geographies.

## Nature's contributions to people

Making the connection between conservation action and benefits to people is a value that can engage more people in conservation action, beyond those directly interested in a particular species or habitat. These connections among fish and wildlife sustainability, environmental health, natural systems function, and human well-being are known as ecosystem services. Using this framework in conservation project development and reporting helps people assess and communicate how natural resources deliver social, cultural, and economic value. 
31
 It is important to understand that this valuation is not to define only an economic value of nature in our lives, but rather helps us describe and measure the degree to which humans are dependent on nature for their health and wellbeing. 
32
 Care should also be taken to consult directly with communities, especially those considered overburdened, to consider their perspectives and acknowledge their needs and contributions on conservation projects, policies, and decisions affecting ecosystem services. In project development, implementation, and reporting, note the scope of community consultation. 
33
 Considering ecosystem services early in conservation project planning helps identify which activities may provide direct and meaningful benefits to communities and engage people in the work. The Millennium Ecosystem Assessment 34 and other literature considered in Morgan et. al. (2025) provide four commonly accepted ecosystem services categories:
Provisioning Services: products that ecosystems provide such as food, timber, fresh water, supporting subsistence, recreational, and commercial human activities.
Regulating Services: Natural processes that help regulate the environment to benefit people, including flood control, erosion prevention, filtering and purifying water, storing carbon and producing oxygen, and mitigating natural hazards like storm surge, landslides, or wildfire.
Cultural Services: Non-material benefits people derive from ecosystems, including recreation, aesthetic enjoyment, cultural identity, spiritual significance, and educational experiences.
Supporting Services: Core ecological functions that maintain biodiversity, ecosystem resilience, and habitat productivity, such as habitat provision, soil formation, nutrient cycling, and pollination. Supporting services support all other service. Intact habitats allow species to thrive and populations to remain stable over time; healthy biodiversity is key to resilience; and processes like decomposition, nutrient cycling, soil formation, and root connectivity are essential for storing water, maintaining productive landscapes and supporting vegetation.
Wildlife conservation directly and indirectly supports and is related to all four of these benefit categories. 
35
 Documenting that in specific conservation action planning, implementation, monitoring, and reporting can strengthen the ties between people and their environments. 
31 Morgan et. al. 2025
https://wdfw.wa.gov/sites/default/files/publications/02628/wdfw02628.pdf
 See also Appendix A in that publication for examples and more detail on beneficial ecosystem services that also crosswalk to WDFW foundational conservation work. 
32

## Plant, fish, and wildlife diseases

The role of zoonotic disease in wildlife, human and ecosystem health cannot be ignored. A real-world example is the ongoing H5N1 highly pathogenic avian influenza virus outbreak in wildlife not only here in Washington, but nationally and internationally where avian influenza has spread from wild birds to wild mammals and to poultry, cattle, and humans. 
36
 In concert with environmental stressors such as drought, fire, flooding, extreme temperatures, habitat disconnection, and increased global mobility of people and animals and animal products, diseases are likely to increase in prevalence posing new and unknow threats to Washington's wildlife, humans, and ecosystems.
Fish and wildlife diseases are known in Washington: white-nose syndrome in bats; avian influenza or 'bird flu'; chronic wasting disease of deer, elk, or moose; elk hoof disease; snake fungal disease; and wild fish diseases. 
37
 With new and emerging diseases on our horizon, conservation includes tracking disease pathways and movement outside of our borders, monitoring to detect disease emergence in Washington quickly, preparedness in the event we have a detection, scoping research and being ready to respond, and including public action in our responses. It is increasingly important to call attention to disease horizon issues that could likely threaten ecosystems in the future.

## Non-native invasive species

Non-native species are defined as invasive when they cause harm to the environment, economy, or human health. They generally require management by people to be prepared and prevent, detect, monitor, eradicate, or manage the invasive species. These species often compete with or disrupt native species and can also alter a habitat or ecosystem and hinder its ability to recover from disturbance. 
38
 This can also cause disproportionate harm to other members of an ecological community. A good example of this is the pervasive introduced cheat grass in eastern Washington which not only can replace native grasses in disturbed areas but can also amplify ignition and effects of wildfire, burning hotter and more destructively.
Like fish and wildlife diseases, conservation actions include tracking and surveillance, detection and monitoring, preparedness planning, and response that include public action. Actions that preserve habitat function and support habitat protection and recovery are a first line of defense in uninvaded systems. When faced with invasive species, we may find that bolstering the underlying ecosystem is not sufficient for species and habitat protection, so one-time or ongoing well-monitored intervention could be needed to support a system's ability to recover. For example, noxious weed control with mechanical or chemical means may be an important near-term or ongoing intervention with appropriately timed seed and rootstock restoration and reintroductions while native plant and animal populations recover.
36 WA DoH 2025 37 WDFW 2025, USGS 2025
Early detection and response are key to avoiding, minimizing, or containing an ongoing managementdependent effort.

## Facilitate effective voluntary conservation action

The SWAP is not regulatory. It can be informative for management, guidance, policies, and regulations, but the plan relies on people taking the information and recommendations from the SWAP and choosing to apply them. The actions in the SWAP will point to vetted resources and information to support the conservation decisions of formal working groups, agencies, conservation partners, government jurisdictions including Tribes, interested communities, and inspired landowners and land trusts. The SWAP provides ideas for engagement and outreach, partnerships, and private lands stewardship to influence and contribute to habitat connectivity, climate resilience, species and habitat health, sustainability and recovery. Voluntary action is powerful because conservation solutions are often more durable when people are committed to the desired outcomes, and in some circumstances incorporating conservation action into requirements and regulations can help ensure we avoid unintended consequences and tradeoffs for species and their habitats.
To support this, the SWAP makes connection with WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species
foot_10
 which provides accepted science and tools for land use planning and proactive conservation considerations in project development. Additionally, non-governmental organizations in Washington state are wellsupported in their service territories by folks who care about conservation in their communities. They are informed and motivated to engage private landowners and community partners in strategic land and water conservation. Over 140 non-governmental conservation organizations led and supported by people who live and work in Washington state were invited to participate in the SWAP revision (Appendix E). They bring crucial local and regional knowledge and scalable networking to conservation in Washington state, and the SWAP can highlight and deliver information for these networks through conservation actions in specific ecoregions.

## Document and report progress as an important part of success

All conservation actions are constrained. Human capacity, funding, technical knowledge, social or cultural understanding or support, tools or practices, and time are a few examples of limiting resources that require project teams to pause and/or adapt. The complex solutions we need for long-term conservation durability span years (e.g., time representing several generations of the target species or established and stable habitats) and results do not often come in available or consecutive funding cycles. Governments include a public service duty and non-government/community partners are accountable to their supporters to prudently use those resources. Documenting and communicating progress, effectiveness, failure, and change are important steps in that responsibility to make good choices that address constraints and demonstrate commitment to a successful result. Local governments develop comprehensive plans and regulations to manage growth, protect critical environmental features and areas, and designate urban growth areas to achieve the 15 planning goals outlined under the Growth Management Act (RCW 36.70A.020). Natural resources conservation information like that in this plan is related to and can influence regional and local specific objectives and regulations created and managed through county and city planning and implementation. WDFW is a contributor to local land use planning and provides conservation information through the Priority Habitats and Species program, technical guidance on conservation actions including restoration, many conservation plans, and data, models, tools, and information on species and habitats.

## Value inter-governmental conservation opportunities at all scales

Washington shares borders, waters, and natural resources with our international neighbor Canada and state neighbors Idaho and Oregon. The Pacific Ocean and shorelines require conservation collaboration across local governments, states (California, Oregon, Washington, Idaho, and Alaska), nations (Tribes, Canada, US federal agencies), and more broadly through the Western and Central Pacific Fisheries Commission of which the US is one of 26 member countries (NOAA 2025).
Another important example of cross-jurisdiction conservation and management is the Columbia River.
Fed by watersheds originating in Nevada, Wyoming, Idaho, Montana, Oregon, Washington, British Columbia and Alberta (Canada), the Columbia River (Figure 
4
) 42 is of state, Tribal, national, and international commercial, recreational, and natural importance as the largest river flowing into the Pacific Ocean from North America. 
43
 The Columbia River Gorge is the largest national scenic area in the United States. 
44
 Washington stewards important habitats for migratory species -birds, whales, fish -that travel to and from central and south America, along western states' coasts, Canada, and Alaska. By nature of our location, relationships, and the species that rely on Washington for part of their lifecycle, Washington state is a contributor or party to international fish and wildlife conservation agreements, formal More specific examples tied to conservation initiatives will be in the statewide/transboundary and ecoregion sections of the SWAP.

## Monitor, Learn, Adapt, and Communicate

Conservation implementation is guided by monitoring and being able to make changes in light of new information, changes that are beneficial to project effectiveness and progress toward a consistent stated/desired conservation outcome. Conservation progress takes time and adjustments may be needed before seeing success.
A cycle of "plan, do, check, and adjust" outlines the need and value to purposefully track the progress and results of actions and adjust assumptions and actions based on this learning. The SWAP promotes 42 American Rivers 2025
43 USBoR 2021 using an effectiveness framework, either the one developed specifically for SWAPs
foot_11
 or a system-specific one that documents progress and provides clear information a project team can use. Additionally, projects that implement SWAP conservation actions need to include reporting, outreach, and datasharing with communities of practice. This enables partners to consider and adapt to new and documented information, and the success of our collective actions over time. Which also contributes to the first Compass Point: "increase the pace and scale of biodiversity conservation."
See also Reporting Progress section where we propose a catalogue for project tracking to inform conservation practices and the next revision.

SWAP 2015 (the last major revision of this plan) was organized to support USFWS review and approval and remains a very good resource for species and vegetation community information. SWAP 2015 is a key SWAP 2025 reference. Documenting changes from SWAP 2015 is a requirement for revised plan approval by USFWS. 
46
 This section is an overview of the changes to the Eight Required Elements content and other changes. Element changes are also tabulated for the USFWS Regional Review Team in Appendix B: Regional Review Team (RRT) Roadmap, using the USFWS-provided template.

## Organizing and presenting information

These SWAP 2025 changes are responsive to many of the concerns expressed in the Triangle Associates pre-revision report (2023) to make the plan easier to use by more people interested in Washington's biodiversity:
• While SWAP 2025 is supported by vetted technical information, this plan uses less technical language for readability and points to more technical information for readers who want a deeper dive. Appendix H provides a Glossary for more technical terms.
• SWAP 2025 content for species, habitats, systems, threats and actions is organized by the nine ecoregions of Washington state; most of the content in those chapters can stand alone. SWAP 2015 (Table 
4
-4) used ecoregions as a description feature for Ecological Systems of Concern and some, not all, of the SGCN distributions (species fact sheets, Appendix A in SWAP 2015); ecoregions were not an organizing structure.
• SWAP 2025 has a statewide and transboundary chapter that relates issues to conservation recommendations. SWAP 2015 also provided a state overview however "conservation problems" and "conservation strategies" were separated.
• The body of SWAP 2025 is synthesized information with a focus on conservation practices and actions with most of the technical information in Appendices.

## Adding web-enabled content

SWAP 2015 and SWAP 2025 are PDF formats, readable and posted online. SWAP 2025 provides more linked and cited resources -references, plans, working groups, products, reports, data, and maps, many of which are regularly updated. Without changing the core elements of SWAP 2025 that USFWS defines as a "revision," we point to information that may evolve and stay current for project development and prioritization. This way, the SWAP retains more relevancy between revisions.
Reviewers' note: Appendices and large tables that present a lot of information will be available online at the WDFW SWAP website during draft review, then updated and reposted online when the plan is approved by the RRT. While this is not web-enabled content like a map or data slicer tool, Microsoft Excel sheets will provide the users more ability to search and filter information than they had in previous SWAP versions. SWAP data will be made available from WDFW when the plan is approved by the RRT.

## SWAP database

WDFW created a SWAP 2025 data stewardship structure for SGCN information: status, range, habitat association, threats, and actions. This structure is useful to collect consistent information on all SGCN, sort and organize that information for the plan, create easier workflows for status and plan updates, better align information with WDFW Priority Habitats and Species, and, in the future, could provide a public-facing portal for searchable SWAP information. Because we chose structures that align with many other SWAPs nationally, the SWAP database can more easily feed external public-facing web-based SWAP dashboards and databases that are promoted for all SWAPs. 47

## Changes to Eight Required Elements
