## Conservation Actions

Putting it all together for Conservation Targets Conservation actions are the most important part of the SWAP and address the issues of our time that affect SGCN and HGCN sustainability.
Appendix C5 describes the vocabulary and process we used and Appendix D7 provides SGCN issue and action tables, meeting Required Elements 3 and 4.
What we understand about species and their needs, habitats, systems, and impacts to sustainability is constantly evolving. The conservation targets and actions in the SWAP are recommendations at multiple scales based on decades of research and expertise in Washington. Recovery and restoration take timesometimes generations of species to see positive effects. Those actions need to monitor and document progress and consider if or how to adapt to that and emerging information.
The SWAP makes conservation recommendations to support efforts that have positive momentum, well-structured decision-making, and/or urgency to prevent the need to list species and to recover those already in the conservation "emergency room." The SWAP is not a catalogue of all issues that have adversely impacted habitats and species from past to present. The SWAP identifies current and emerging issues that can be acted on now and in the foreseeable future, based on what we know now and can reasonably and confidently model.
Issues are identified in a way that helps conservation practitioners understand what we know about an issues, and conservation actions that directly benefit SWAP systems, habitats and species are identified to address those issues to get started on some or all of these questions: what needs to be done (first and ongoing), who can help, when is it needed (how urgent), and where (ecoregions, watersheds).
Example. "Residential and Commercial Development" is an issue often stated as a contributor to loss and degradation of species and habitats. That impact that has already occurred. Where it cannot be ameliorated is no longer relevant to focus on now. We must do what we can where we can. Where an issue can be specifically attributed as a direct impact to the species or its habitat, and actions can be taken to improve the status of native species and the habitats and systems they need, then identifying this issue is appropriate. Actions could be like these:
• Provide technical guidance to land use planners to mesh PHS shrubsteppe management recommendations and WSRRI strategies for system-level benefits;
• Use urban land use planning and restoration, public lands stewardship, and effective voluntary conservation to connect targeted riparian corridors for high priority fish passage and habitat improvements, floodplain conservation, and/or migratory bird stopovers;
• Reduce pollutant inputs to HGCN to benefit salmon, Oregon spotted frog, other specific aquatic species in a particular watershed or ecoregion;
• Create regionally specific seed mixes for remediation, restoration, and education about westside prairies and pollinators with targeted outreach for wildland-adjacent suburban and exurban development (e.g., homeowners, landowners, infrastructure planners and construction companies) to reduce non-native species invasion into wildlands from these areas;
• Support the DNR Forest Action Plan and work with landowners and utility companies in strategic wildland-urban interfaces to reduce wildfire risk to infrastructure and commercial forests AND retain system benefits for targeted HGCN and SGCN.

## Issue and Action Vocabulary

In the 2025 SWAP, direct issues and actions for SGCN, vulnerable species aggregations, and HGCN are identified using a consistent framework -the Conservation Standards 2016/2020 Threats and Actions vocabulary (Threats Classification v 2.0, Actions Classification v 2.0), with a few amendments for Washington state (Appendix C5). The Washington SWAP will use the Level 2 Issues (Threats) and Level 2 Actions from the vocabulary applied to conservation targets. The issues-to-actions relationship could be one-to-one or one-to-many, meaning that there may be more than one action per threat.
The SWAP identifies direct threats that limit population, habitat, or system sustainability, resiliency, redundancy, and/or representation. 
49
 In the explanation of the threat for a particular target, indirect threats and stressors may also be included to better explain issues. 
50
 Direct threats. Direct threats may be considered sources of stress: human actions that immediately affect/degrade a conservation target; natural phenomena altered by human activities that has an impact on a conservation target (e.g., increase in flooding or water temperature caused by natural system modification that makes a habitat unsuitable for an instream critter).
Indirect Threat. A driver of or influence on direct threats that may help us understand the direct threat and where we can apply solutions. Sometimes called a root cause or underlying cause.
Example. Clear-cutting or unsustainable logging may be the direct threat to a forest habitat or forest-dependent species, whereas logging policies or rules may be the indirect threat (a different layer or further 'upstream' of the direct impact where policy solutions can be applied).
Stress. The impact of the direct threat on the conservation target; attributes of a conservation target's ecology that are impaired directly or indirectly.
Examples. Pygmy Rabbit (SGCN) -if direct threat is increased frequency, intensity, and extent of wildfire in shrubsteppe systems, then stresses to the rabbits may be significantly reduced forage and poor body condition leading to lower reproductive potential, isolated populations, reduced/limited population size, and increased predation from opportunistic native predators that take advantage of rabbit exposure.
Shrubsteppe (HGCN) -if direct threat is increased frequency, intensity, and extent of wildfire in shrubsteppe systems, then stresses to the habitat may be significantly reduced seed bank and soil moisture for natural recovery, reduced natural soil crust, and bare-soil exposure benefitting non-native cheatgrass invasion.

## Considerations

In addition to the vocabulary, the SWAP puts some sideboards on which issues and actions need to be in this plan. These considerations support prioritization -actions that can do the most good with available resources, capacity, and information, in places where those actions will be durable to address the greatest risks to SGCN and HGCN sustainability.
"Threats" in the vocabulary are not inherently "bad." These activities become issues when they adversely affect the status or sustainability of a species or habitat, and people need to resolve that issue through conservation action. Good examples: fire can be applied as a tool in restoration, recreation can create engagement, volunteer stewardship opportunities, and connections that inspire beneficial behaviors.
Conservation actions are how practitioners guide intervention to support a specific desired future condition or positive-progress strategy to address an issue. Conservation actions are objectives that would be useful to guide and develop project plans, identify dependencies and relationships, pursue funding, and craft new or engage existing partnerships in the SWAP implementation.
Reasonably actionable in the foreseeable future. Issues in the SWAP are not be a long 'laundry list.' Some impacts have happened long ago and the condition of the species or habitat is reflecting that in a permanent way. Conservation actions need to be forward looking -what can we work with now to improve the future for those systems, habitats, and species?
Issues are supported by good information and references. Issues identified for HGCN and SGCN are supported in tables and other data products by citable information (e.g. recovery outlines, Periodic Status Reviews, state or federal listing documents, NatureServe threat analysis, working group documentation, models, synthesis). Actions have a direct connection to the issue and the species/habitats affected by that issue. Where a conservation action is clearly stated or described in another product, that product is referenced, but all of the content is not re-stated in the SWAP. In this way, the SWAP remains a nimble document that can point to evolving and relevant information in other plans that have active working groups or stakeholders or more frequent updates.
Actions are "prescriptive enough." Actions provide a clear framework to address an issue AND leave room for new information, methods, and partnerships in implementation. A good example is a conservation action that recommends species reintroduction. There is a LOT of partnership, policy-work, baseline monitoring, and effectiveness planning that is part of that action, dependent on current and desired habitat conditions, capacity and resources, and -importantly -the state of the practice at the time. Actions in the SWAP must be "prescriptive enough" to say the reintroduction is warranted, but not so prescriptive as to the specific steps to take in implementation. The SWAP is not project-or activitylevel planning.
Existing expertise and 'gnarly problems.' Conservation for many SGCN and HGCN already have communities of practice, expertise to help shape effective strategies, and pathways to good partnerships. And, in some cases, those communities of practice encounter 'gnarly problems' or issues where the action or approach is less clear right now, issues that need innovation or a broader human understanding or community. These days, certain threats and the pace/scale of change (e.g., ecosystem composition shifts, extensive biodiversity loss 51 , invasive species and/or disease spread) are creating problems that our past best practices and existing partnerships may not be sufficient to address. In novel situations, we need novel approaches; a willingness by practitioners, managers, leaders, and stakeholders to try new things and accept the possibility of failure, well-vetted and transparent decisionmaking and action without admiring the problem too long; and a commitment to monitoring and adaptation built into our actions. Structured decision-making, working group or partnership development or enhancement, effectiveness monitoring, and adaptive management are conservation actions, essential before and after work on the ground.

## Conservation Targets

SWAP conservation targets are the habitats and species that benefit from recommended conservation actions in this plan to address issues that affect their persistence, recovery, and sustainability. Affected by loss and lack of connectivity, system to site level degradation, and other specific issues, these are the focal species and habitats that need our attention the most in the foreseeable future.
"If everything is a priority, then nothing is a priority." -said pretty much everyone

## Overview

This document asks the conservation community, planners, agencies, and private land managers to apply their resources and implement recommended conservation actions in this plan. To trust that those efforts would be worth their engagement, it is important that the conservation targets are based on vetted data in institutional databases; published and peer-reviewed literature, assessments, synthesized information, and reports; and accessible materials so that the trail of breadcrumbs to these recommendations is more transparent and defensible. This section defines and summarizes the SWAP conservation targets and provides an overview of how they are used in the Plan. Habitat and species targets are defined by criteria, citable information, and processes (Appendix C). Tables or links to tables resulting from those processes for SWAP species and habitats are found in Appendix D.

## This section summarizes

• the SWAP relationship to WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species (PHS),
• SWAP species -SGCN, Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN), and vulnerable concentrations, and
• Habitats of Greatest Conservation Need (HGCN)
for which threats and actions are identified in Appendix D and distributed throughout relevant geographic sections.

## WDFW Priority Habitats and Species

WDFW maintains the Priority Habitats and Species information (lists, descriptions, management recommendations) that local governments use when designating and protecting Fish and Wildlife Habitat Conservation Areas under the Growth Management Act and Shoreline Management Act, and for which special conservation measures should be taken.
In this revision, WDFW worked across Programs to incorporate more of the PHS information into the SWAP and align products from both sources: 
53
 There is some overlap between species and concentrations which will be highlighted in relevant sections in the rest of this plan. An example is Bocaccio rockfish (a PHS Priority Species and an SGCN) and PHS multi-species rockfish concentrations that are SWAP vulnerable concentrations and contain Bocaccio rockfish. Both the species and the concentration are SWAP conservation targets in marine environments.

## SWAP Species

The 2017 SWAP Guidance and Eight Required Elements are focused on SGCN and the "full array of wildlife … including low and declining populations as the state fish and wildlife agency deems appropriate, that are indicative of the diversity and health of the State's wildlife." Washington state is home to thousands and thousands of species, from the tiniest mites to the mightiest whale. To better manage the task of selecting SWAP species, only native plant, vertebrate, and invertebrate species and subspecies that are considered taxonomically valid are eligible. Additional considerations for eligibility are applied:

## Eligible

• species that do not live in Washington state year-around (migratory) and depend on Washington state for some portion of their lifecycle;
• species that does not currently occur in and may naturally move into Washington state seeking suitable habitats in response to stressors where they currently occur;
• species extirpated in Washington with recovery potential 54 ; or • hunted, fished, or foraged species being stressed by a defined issue (such as disease or invasive species), even if that stressor would not change the harvest opportunity or management. 55

## Not eligible

• non-native species;
• peripheral species for which management actions in Washington would not likely affect the conservation status; or
• species extirpated in Washington without a current recovery community or potential.
With a resulting pool of thousands of species, additional criteria were applied to define three types of SWAP Species 56 :
• Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN),
• Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN), and 54 Woodland Caribou is an example of a Washington extirpated species that is eligible. Although documented extirpated in Washington state, an international and multi-state recovery effort, mostly focused on Canada, is active. WDFW continues to participate in those conversations to track the recovery progress and collaborate to support the effort if or when relevant for Washington. 
55
 WDFW Commission presentation December 14, 2023 
https://wdfw.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2023-12/wildlifecommittee-sgcn-briefing-paper.pdf
 Hunted, Fished, and Foraged Species may be SGCN 56 SWG and C-SWG Eligibility for SWAP Species: SWAP-defined conservation actions for SGCN are eligible. SGIN are eligible for SWG and C-SWG funding if the defined work is to determine whether the species should be an SGCN. Vulnerable aggregations are eligible for SWG if one of the species is an SGCN and the conservation actions are related to the SGCN AND the vulnerable aggregation.

## • vulnerable aggregations.

A note on prioritization. Some states prioritize within their SGCN tables. Washington does not so that the conservation community can adapt over time to new funding opportunities, find patterns in multispecies and habitat conservation to be more efficient and effective, support creative and engaged expertise that evolves over time, and feed state and regional working groups' focus areas as those develop. Conservation of SWAP species and habitats are the priority.

## SGCN

SGCN were identified by two criteria: 
2022
) to enable a state to qualify for the five percent plant conservation supplemental funding outlined in the Recovering America's Wildlife Act (RAWA), when passed and enacted. 
58
 Including plant conservation also communicates a more complete approach to conservation of habitats and systems.
About 52 SGCN (species or subspecies) in 2015 were not selected as SGCN for 2025. It's difficult to be precise with that number because taxonomic questions were answered (lumping, splitting, name changes). Additionally, species were not selected as SGCN because status improved (state or federally delisted), new distribution information helped determine the species was less in need of conservation in Washington, or they fit better in the new SGIN category.
Some SGCN in the 2015 table were labeled as "SC" for "federal species of concern" which is not a federally recognized or defined status, so that designation is not a criterion for SWAP species in 2025.
New SGCN, primarily invertebrates and fishes, were added due to new information from work done under the 2015 SWAP, earlier/broader engagement with peers and expert networks (extensively for invertebrates), improved understanding about impacts of extreme and changing environments on some species and their habitats, and new formal assessments leading to updated defined status.
Some SGCN were 'grouped.' An example: bull trout and dolly varden -both are tagged as SGCN and could 'count' as two, but WDFW manages these two species as an aggregate so they only 'count' as one. Also, many of the salmonid SGCN in the table represent a number of Distinct Population Segments or Ecologically Significant Units.
Subpopulations are treated differently in SWAP 2025. In 2015, some species were identified as SGCN for only a portion of their range or a well-defined subpopulation (e.g. Pacific Cod Salish Sea population, Mountain Quail east of the Cascades). While that is still true and noted in the SGCN status comment as "Regional concern" or "Subpopulation concern" for some of the SGCN in 2025, the plan user will also subpopulation distinctions in the threats and actions of the plan. We acknowledge that we need a better data structure to be able to organize subpopulation data and information for future SWAP iterations and for honing conservation project planning and reporting. This is mentioned in SWAP Planning Conservation Recommendations. Washington's HUC 10 watersheds (Figure 
6
) were selected for SWAP 2025 as a good scale to use with other SWAP data sets and can be scaled up as needed. More information about the process for species range selection is provided in Appendix C2.

## SGCN Distribution

This SWAP presents all SGCN distributions as ranges consistently defined by HUC10 watersheds, a geography of conservation potential for each species. For each species, the SWAP presents the broadest geography (outer boundary) within Washington where a species may/could be found, limited by accessible and suitable habitat in that selected watershed, for any part of the life cycle. Suitable habitat and, therefore, the species may not occur throughout the entire watershed. SGCN watersheds may contain areas where the species does not or cannot live because there might not be suitable habitat, including 'matrix' through which the species travels in seasonal migration or dispersal.
A HUC 10 watershed is selected if there is reliable information: mapped observations, expert understanding of the habitat potentially in the HUC, or documented and planned conservation action (e.g. recovery area for restoration or reintroduction) for the species. The example in Figure7 for sagebrush sparrow shows selected watersheds outlined in blue. This figure will be updated when the final data products and legend symbols are updated. Watersheds are selected for different reasons:
• observation data has been collected for or is available in an institutional system;
• observation data has been documented in literature, personal expert communication, or trusted source that can be referenced;
• watershed is adjacent to a known-occupied watershed and suitable habitat may exist 62 ; or • watershed is documented in a recovery, restoration, or recovery plan for the species and that work is planned or programmed in the foreseeable future in a feasible geography, meaning that future species occurrence is possible (as opposed to in a plan but no potential for implementation).
62 Why select HUCs without known occurrence data? Using only occurrence data may underestimate the range of a species and limit potential conservation. Not all potential places may be accessible or feasible to survey. HUCs should not be selected without some species-potential indicators. In watersheds with some knowledge of habitat potential, highlighting these 'proximal' HUCs could encourage reliable observation collection and engage more audiences in that species' conservation.
HUC 10s were not selected for historical range where there is no foreseeable potential to restore a functional population of the species. A good example of that is woodland caribou (Figure 
8
), which is functionally extirpated in Washington state, but recovery efforts continue in adjacent Canada with potential to re-engage Washington, so HUCs are selected for this species in Washington. This figure will be updated when the final data products and legend symbols are updated. The intention of the range maps is to guide conservation, not capture the full history of the species in Washington state. These maps are based on best available information: mappable observations, records, and other information, most of which are captured in institutional and trusted data systems, sourced from surveys, monitoring, reports, literature, and expert sources. Known habitat use and extent of that habitat could be considered. For example, if a biologist had knowledge of habitat lost to development and subsequent loss of the species in the watershed, they may not have selected the HUC even if observation records were present in that watershed; not all biologists have that information every year for every species. This work is based on the best available information from 1978 to 2025.
All Washington terrestrial and marine watersheds were related to the nine ecoregions so that the information for SGCN could be sorted into ecoregions (process: Appendix C2). There is admittedly fault in this approach for marine, shorebird, and some lowland species because some of the HUC10 watersheds touch marine shorelines and waters but may extend far inland and upland. For example, if a HUC10 that touches a marine area and extends inland a great distance is selected for a shorebird, the data sorting alone may put a shorebird in a mountainous ecoregion, like the North Cascades (Figure 
9
). After sorting the data, subject matter experts reviewed the tables and have sorted out marine species from ecoregions where they do not occur (e.g. North Cascades). WDFW has identified this limitation and will work on solutions to that in the period between revisions (see SWAP Planning Recommendations section).
Much of the habitat association data (SWAP Habitats section and Appendix C3) is also relatable to watersheds at this scale for conservation planning. This gives the plan-user the ability to explore whether multiple SGCN could benefit from overlapping conservation work in a particular watershed, if habitats are similar or overlapping.
An additional resource for SGCN Plant distribution is the NHP Online Field Guide to the Rare Plants of Washington | Department of Natural Resources which also provides range comments, a coarse scale map, and additional identification information. 

## SGCN Habitat Approach

SGCN habitat associations described in this section meet Required Element 2. This, paired with other information in this plan, guides where conservation actions could occur.
Habitat -enough of the right kind in the right places -is the most important piece of species conservation. It is a complex set of biotic and abiotic environmental features and conditions that may change through a species life, may shift on the landscape or over time, and is necessary to support a species' particular breeding, feeding, and sheltering. This applies to plants and animals, invertebrates and vertebrates. Threats to habitat have cascading effects to a species' ability to live its best life and thrive as a population, so a focus on habitat conservation is essential.
Because ecologists and biologists can never know all of the intricate ways that species needs are met at all scales, native healthy and functional habitat conservation is the best way to set a species' population up for success or recovery, preserve relationships among species, and enable species' contributions to the larger environment (example).
Example. Species and habitat conservation work together, and salmon are a good example. Salmon must have healthy functioning riparian systems and streams with particular conditions and structure. For salmon to recover, we need to restore many of these habitats. And, when salmon use those streams, they die in spawning events as part of their natural cycle. Their bodies and nutrients catch in stream pools and side-channels formed by big logs and rocks, often placed there by restoration efforts. Those bodies feed stream invertebrates and streamside plants, that in turn feed and shelter other species. Salmon carcasses provide food for other animals and birds that carry those nutrients far into upland habitats, which can support the restoration and persistence of other species.
Other species recovery actions such as captive-rearing and reintroduction are most successful when the habitat is ready to support the released individuals. And, while single-species site conservation is necessary and often the most feasible place to start, site conservation needs to be paired with system conservation for a view toward sustainability -this is the context that supports the site. For some species, we may not know enough about how it uses or contributes to the site where it occurs, so conserving the broader system improves the opportunity for success (Example Species Spotlight: Limestone Point Mountainsnail and Olympic mudminnow -both of these species have extremely limited known ranges; conserving just the sites where they occur will not likely ensure that they persist as they are a piece of a larger system context). For many species, that single-species and site focus may not be feasible -evidence points to hundreds of invertebrates that need conservation intervention. We know something about their context and their threats, such as bumble bees in shrubsteppe. With the pace and scale of shrubsteppe loss and degradation, we don't have time to try to understand everything a bumble bee does or needs from that habitat before that species might wink out. This is also important in aquatic and riparian systems where all of the species-specific habitats are connected and dependent on the water quality and quantity, instream structure, and the terrestrial contributions to that. Conservation ability -both in human and financial resources -is very limited to create effective and timely conservation if approached species-by-species in all cases. Where we can do more good at the system level, our investments will be more effective and durable.
Conserving ecosystems has direct benefits to species and their habitats and it is a scale we can apply to all species in the SWAP. The ecological systems with which a species is associated contains and supports potential suitable habitat for that species. While species respond to finer scale habitats such as vernal pools or forest stand age or condition and that drives where a species occurs, that fine scale cannot necessarily be mapped or meaningful by itself to the long term sustainability of the species.
Example. If a vernal pool site that a single species of fairy shrimp relies on is conserved, but not the surrounding contributing environment, then the pool environment will change and could be much less suitable or even unsuitable for fairy shrimp. In this case, a complete conservation solution needs to also consider conserving the terrestrial surrounding environment so that structure and contour of the surrounding area allows water to seasonally flow into the pool, upland and riparian vegetation filters pollutants from the inflow, decaying vegetation falls into the pool and provides refuge when the pools dry out seasonally, and birds and other wildlife can introduce the tiny organisms that fairy shrimp eat.
Identifying system relationships for SGCN is important for long-term, durable successful conservation.

## Similar to SWAP 2015

A systems approach was taken in SWAP 2015
foot_17
 Appendix B. In both SWAP 2015 and SWAP 2025, habitat associations for SGCN lean on the assumption that conserving ecosystems has direct benefits to species that occur within them. Using a system approach for habitat association does not always account for specific vegetative conditions. A good example is old-growth forest which is a set of structures, features, and certain conditions within a forest system type. That set of structures, features, and conditions can be critical components of habitat suitability. Systems describe vegetation communities but do not account for ecological condition of those systems, or presence of habitat features (such as cavities in snags) that may be critical to wildlife. To address this, specific habitat features -where known -are included in linked SGCN fact sheets and other resources. This information may evolve the more we learn about a species use of its habitat, habitat shifts over time, and the effects of environmental changes.
In 2015, 85 out of the 268 SGCN 64 were associated with 98 U.S. National Vegetation Classification ecological systems in Washington. The ecological systems map for Washington state is a static product originally developed around 2003, updated nationally at a coarse scale in 2014-2015.

## Systems used in SWAP 2025

A similar approach -using ecosystems for habitat associations -is applied for SWAP 2025, with different data frameworks. Considering that the SWAP work could be improved by a systems data set that is available spatially now and likely to be so for the foreseeable future, scientifically vetted and peerreviewed, improved over time, and used by many other conservation planning efforts and systems at international, national, regional, state, and local scales, WDFW and WA DNR chose to use to two ecosystem data sets. Using habitat associations is admittedly a coarse scale and no remote-sensed or modeled data is going to be as good as on-the-ground information. However, it is a very good place to start creating focal areas for system level and finer scale habitat conservation planning for SGCN sustainability.
SGCN in this plan are associated with ecosystems defined by the U.S. National Vegetation Classification (NVC) 'Groups' and Global Ecosystem Typology (GET) 'Functional Groups'. The NVC is used by many of the SWAPs in the national network, is widely regarded as a standard in other agencies and organizations' vegetation community modeling and conservation planning work, and is a spatial data set that can be used for ecoregional relationships. 65 NVC Groups include vegetation and abiotic factors for both terrestrial and aquatic systems, but that classification does not cover aquatic freshwater and marine types that do not have a vegetation feature. Some SGCN do not have a vegetation relationship in their habitat needs (e.g. many marine species like whales or rockfish) so the GET Functional Groups fill that gap.
NVC and GET are related to each other (Figure 
10
, next page). There are 150 terrestrial and aquatic (including marine) systems that potentially occur in Washington state and were used to identify habitat associations (Appendix C, Table 
C3
). In this table, each NVC Group and GET Functional Group are related back to the Biome and Realm levels to see the relationships.

## NVC Groups Description

Unlike the static ecological systems used in 2015 66 , the NVC Groups are a set of defined vegetation communities that are updated periodically by a national committee of ecologists, data managers, and conservation practitioners; a representative from Washington's Natural Heritage Program sits on this committee. 
67
 In late summer 2025, USNVC updated many of the Groups for Washington state. Most of the changes were one-to-one name changes; one was a many-to-one change; and one type was removed as a type that could occur in Washington (Appendix C3). Although these changes happened after species associations and most data work was complete, WDFW and WA DNR NHP worked together to make many of the changes for the SWAP tables as well as supporting data.
Seventy-nine (79) NVC Groups for Washington were available for SGCN associations. A table of Washington systems updated in September 2025, links to their descriptions, and status are provided in Appendix C3.
The NVC is a hierarchy of nested modeled and mapped (spatial) data that can be related to watersheds and ecoregions and loosely related to the species occurrence data. NVC Groups present the wide range of existing vegetation, from natural types that include old-growth forest stands and seminatural vegetation (including grazed rangelands, old agricultural lands undergoing natural succession, and stands dominated by naturalized exotics) to planted or cultivated vegetation, such as row crops, orchards, and forest plantations. NVC Groups are a coarser scale than species observation data. For vertebrates and invertebrates, subject matter experts used mapped aggregated species observation and NVC systems data, system descriptions, range maps, literature about species habitat use and system relationships, and firsthand knowledge to associate SGCN to NVC Groups. For plant SGCN associations, WA DNR NHP used the NVC hierarchy (e.g., if an SGCN plant occurs in a particular NVC Association, then it is likely to be relatable to the NVC Group in which the Association occurs) 
68
 .
Associating species to a Group does not mean that the species occurs throughout the maximum extent of that NVC type (Example: Marbled Murrelet). A species may be closely associated to a particular system but limited within that system by life history and finer scale characteristics within that system (as in our old growth example above) and its range.
Example. Marbled Murrelet is closely associated with many NVC Group forest systems: North Pacific Maritime Douglas-fir -Western Hemlock Rainforest, North-Central Pacific Maritime Silver Fir -Western Hemlock Rainforest, North-Central Pacific Western Hemlock -Sitka Spruce Rainforest, to name a few. By the mapped extent of those types, one might assume Marbled Murrelets would be able to inhabit much of western Washington. However, Marbled Murrelet potential nesting range is contracted in Washington because they rely on mature and old-growth forest with very specific characteristics for nesting and within a certain distance of marine environments where they forage. Those forest patches are few and far between within those larger forested systems.
NVC Group relationships in this SWAP provide system-to-species connections that can be related to the ecoregion, landscape, and watershed scales before scaling down to project planning at the site scale. NVC Groups and finer scales (Alliances and Associations) provide a consistent hierarchy to describe conservation objectives and desired future outcomes from landscape conservation planning to habitat restoration projects. When using NVC Groups to approach a conservation project, it is useful to also describe the desired future condition of finer scale habitat quality, integrity, or stage in project planning (e.g., early seral forest or old growth/mature forest). The NVC Group data products are to be used in concert with project-level planning, not in lieu of. As the NVC data layers improve, the species -system relationship data could become more useful.

## GET Functional Groups Description

A subset of the GET Functional Group ecosystems
foot_19
 describe non-vegetated aquatic systems such as such as rivers and streams, lakes and reservoirs, artificial wetlands, estuaries and coastal inlets, and marine environments. Some GET Functional Groups were added to the list of NVC Groups to better include fully aquatic habitats that do not have a described vegetated system in NVC, and which are important for SGCN for all or a portion of their lifecycle.
This approach filled some of the system gaps for some SGCN that were not related to systems in 2015.
Overall, for Washington state, GET Functional Groups are not mapped. GET provides some mapping for some systems at a very coarse scale unsuitable for this effort. 
70
 Descriptions of the GET Functional Groups are available online through the IUCN GET Explorer. In Washington, there are 43 GET Functional Groups used in the SWAP. Subject matter experts used GET system descriptions, range maps, literature about species habitat use and system relationships, and firsthand knowledge to associate SGCN to GET Functional Groups.
See also SWAP Planning Conservation Recommendations: relate most GET for Washington state with standardized spatial data relationships for springs, streams, rivers, marine basins, and caves to enable better project planning.

## NVC + GET Benefits

These nationally and globally adopted frameworks allow biologists and ecologists to use the same vocabulary across taxa and regions to describe a relationship between systems and species. The NVC structure is used by several other SWAPs across the U.S. which gives the Washington SWAP relationship to those plans regionally and nationally.
Both NVC Groups and GET Functional Groups as they are related to SGCN in this plan can be useful to understand which SGCN rely on the same systems. Relating species to systems in ecoregions and watersheds can help the conservation community and partners find opportunities and efficiencies in multi-species conservation to benefit more than single species.
Example. Both Sagebrush Sparrow and Sagebrush Lizard are closely associated with Intermountain Basins Big Sagebrush Steppe, an NVC Group. A portion of their ranges defined by HUC10 watersheds overlap. Although they have different lifestyles and finer scale habitats, biologists can look at the range for both species and the extent of the NVC Group as a starting place to explore if there are opportunities in that overlapping geography to do projects that benefit both species and contribute to the integrity of the larger system.
SWAP 2015 and SWAP 2025 use the same vocabulary
foot_21
 to associate SGCN to systems/habitats:
• Closely Associated: The species demonstrates preference for the ecological system, as indicated by greater occurrence, high densities, greater reproductive output, or other indicators of preference, than in other systems. A species that is closely associated to individual ecological systems often rely on one to a few ecological systems for a significant part or all of its life history requirements.
• Generally Associated: The species occurs in, but does not prefer, the ecological system, as indicated by lesser occurrence, lower densities, or other indicators of a general relationship with the ecological system. A species that is generally associated with individual ecological systems can typically rely on numerous ecological systems to meet its life history requirements.
• Unsuitable: A species demonstrates no use or only occasional use of the ecological system.
• Unknown: The species' use of the ecological system is unknown. There were questions or uncertainty about species use of an ecological system.
In this SWAP, biologists faced the same challenge as in the 2015 SWAP: using only species observation data (points and polygons where we know a species has been documented) to relate species to the systems may
• limit potential conservation opportunity -not all areas that could be suitable for a species can be surveyed (i.e., access, capacity, expertise, and other limiting factors shape where surveys can occur and how often);
• miss actual systems and habitats on the ground due to the scale at which NVC is available (species observation data paired with NVC can be used as a coarse filter for landscape-and watershed-scale project planning, supported by remote-sensed vegetation where appropriate and ground-truthed conditions); and,
• not account for the rapid pace of habitat change due to human influences. 

## More detail about the SGCN Habitat

## Vulnerable Concentrations

Vulnerable concentrations are a new group important for conservation in Washington, added in SWAP 2025 and not a Required Element. These are groups of animals that more vulnerable when they get together to breed, rear young, or gather during migration. Impacts to these concentrations may have broader negative impacts to the individual species' populations or the habitats they support or depend on. Often, their shared ecological contribution is greater than the individual parts (e.g. forage fishes); there are gaps in single-species information but well-supported importance of and threats to the collective (e.g. pollinators); and conservation actions for the broader group may be more efficient and impactful than what could be done for the individual species in that assemblage (e.g. migratory birds).
SWAP vulnerable concentrations include PHS groups of the same name and three additional typespollinators, migratory birds including hawk concentrations, and freshwater mussels (Table 
2
 and
Appendix D4
). Several vulnerable aggregations also have a strong connection to one or more Habitats of Greatest Conservation Need and are noted in those descriptions as values for HGCN conservation (SWAP Habitats: HGCN section and Appendix D7). For example, the vulnerable concentrations of forage fishes, marine bivalves, and shorebirds are closely associated with the HGCN Nearshore, and conservation actions for Nearshore habitats can greatly benefit these vulnerable aggregations. Distribution for vulnerable concentrations that are PHS-defined are by county and available online as a Microsoft Excel file and by selected location online in the PHS on the Web tool. Pollinator distribution information varies in detail across the Pacific Northwest (Xerces Society maintains information, WDFW has included ranges for many individual pollinator invertebrates in the SGCN information and conducts surveys for many of the most imperiled species); pollinator SGCN habitat associations taken as a whole could be good surrogate for pollinator distribution and that could essentially highlight all native habitats in the state. Xerces provides a freshwater mussel atlas for western North America -this is at the HUC8 (a scale larger than HUC10) and limited by data shared with Xerces; this is a good overview and could be paired with SGCN freshwater mussel-specific SWAP Range HUC10s that were selected from occurrence data, literature, and other information for finer scales. Migratory bird distribution is vast and variable, depending on the season. For this plan, the best source of distribution information would be SGCN HUC10 from this plan, paired with WDFW occurrence information. BirdCast (screenshot Figure 
11
) can provide additional information about seasonal peaks and live seasonal tracking to inform conservation project timing at the county level.
Conservation actions for vulnerable aggregations and their related HGCN are presented in relevant geographies. 

## SWAP Habitats: HGCN

Many of the habitats in this section are directly related to SGCN conservation -either providing landscape-scale support or specific habitat relationships. This section's information paired with the SGCN habitat associations build more durable conservation actions. This is additive information for Element 2. In SWAP 2015 Chapter 4, Habitats of Greatest Conservation Need (HGCN) included ecological systems considered imperiled as identified by Washington Natural Heritage Program (Ecological Systems of Concern) and ecological systems which were closely associated to six or more SGCN.
In scoping SWAP 2025, many conservation practitioners emphasized habitat conservation as a priority at statewide, ecoregion, and site scales, and we received comments asking to clarify the relationship between HGCN and PHS Priority Habitats. While SWAPs require species-based information, habitat integrity and system conservation are foundations for the success of most direct species conservation actions. Conservation actions for habitat sustainability such as protection, restoration, or enhancement are the best actions we can take to prevent the need to list species, support recovery actions, and improve resiliency for systems.
Species-specific habitat conservation issues and actions are identified in the SGCN Issues and Actions table in Appendix D7. In SWAP 2025, HGCN 75 are stand-alone conservation targets to address habitat and system sustainability and are related to SGCN to make that connection. HGCN Issues and Actions are in this section. Both SGCN and HGCN related to geographic sections.
HGCN can also make conservation and ecosystem services contributions in human spaces -parklands, urban riparian corridors, wildland-urban interfaces, agricultural and ranching lands. Ideally, a higher priority is placed on native wild or conserved habitats and systems as the needs of fish and wildlife are more easily met in places with fewer human impacts. However, habitats in or adjacent to human spaces can be crucial to support species during restoration in native habitats, connect wildlands through urban areas, provide important stepping stones of habitat for migratory species, and engage people in conservation. So, some HGCN may also have representation in human environments.
HGCN have a direct relationship to WDFW's Priority Habitats, the Washington Natural Heritage Plan, resiliency and habitat connectivity resources, site to system conservation, and SGCN habitats. HGCN are identified using science-based resources and supporting documentation. These key conservation targets are redefined for this SWAP edition into three HGCN categories:
• Habitat Support • Strategic Habitats and • Local and Special Habitats. 
75
 During scoping and engagement to revise the plan, these were called "important habitats" (Appendix E: Coordination and Engagement). Washington Department of Fish and Wildlife (WDFW) and Washington Department of Natural Resources (WA DNR) have identified information sources for these HGCN in Appendix D7. These are a good starting place for conservation action planning and implementation, and project teams can apply other sources to meet their specific purposes. Processes -natural or applied by humans -that are essential for habitat systems function or maintenance are related to conservation actions for SGCN and HGCN in relevant geographic sections.
The intention in SWAP 2015 was sound -identifying and doing conservation for systems that support many SGCN
foot_26
 can create beneficial efficiency, bring conservation practitioners from different communities (e.g., species biologists, habitat restoration specialists, landscape ecologists, social scientists) together, and improve resiliency at the system level that also benefits other species that are not SGCN. Because SWAP 2025 includes PHS Priority Habitats as HGCN, some of that system relationship to multiple species is built in, and there is some overlap among PHS and NHP NVC Groups.
Example. Shrubsteppe is a PHS Priority Habitat and related to seven NVC Groups. Of those, five NVC Groups are also identified by NHP for conservation. The number of SGCN associated to those NVC Groups ranges from 1 to 33, with four of the systems associated with 18 or more SGCN.
However, a cut-off driven by the SGCN numbers alone doesn't incorporate philosophical or policy values about what makes a habitat important for biodiversity even though that is one good indicator. For SWAP 2025, we explored that concept (more information in Appendix C4) and did not add Strategic SGCN outside of the vetted PHS and NHP processes. This section describes the types of HGCN in SWAP 2025. Where relevant, the HGCN are cross-walked to the NVC Group(s) and/or GET Functional Group(s) and related to SGCN. Conservation issues and recommended actions are in this section and distributed through relevant geographic sections. More HGCN references and resources are in Appendix D8.

## Habitat Support HGCN

These places provide certain values to many species and habitats at many scales. When these places are conserved, they support longer-term and broader habitat and species sustainability, can be applied at any scale from site to landscape, are supported by data and/or modeled information, and are not single SGCN-specific but are beneficial to several to many SGCN and, more broadly, species and the habitats on which they depend. These places may be single-type ecosystems (e.g. "shrubsteppe") or a matrix of various habitats and systems (e.g., floodplain, riparian freshwater wetlands, and a river system combined), successional stages, and quality to achieve the value(s) of biodiversity areas, corridors, and/or climate refugia.

## Biodiversity Areas

Biodiversity areas are natural areas that support diverse wildlife communities and/or critical and unique elements of Washington State's biodiversity. These areas should be able to sustain minimum viable populations of species, which can persist over the long term without facing immediate extinction due to demographic stochasticity, genetic issues or environmental fluctuations. Biodiversity areas often function as core areas 78 and are important for connectivity. WDFW defines biodiversity areas as: the area has been identified as biologically diverse through a scientifically based assessment conducted over a landscape scale (e.g., statewide, ecoregion, county-or city-wide, watershed) (examples include WDFW Local Habitat Assessments, Pierce County Biodiversity Network, Priority Amphibian and Reptile Conservation Areas 79 ); or the area is within a city or an urban growth area (UGA) and contains habitat that is valuable to fish or wildlife and is mostly comprised of native vegetation. Relative to other vegetated areas in the same city or UGA, the mapped area is vertically diverse (e.g., multiple canopy layers, snags, or downed wood), horizontally diverse (e.g., contains a mosaic of native habitats), or supports a diverse community of species as identified by a qualified professional who has a degree in biology or closely related field and professional experience related to the habitats or species occurring in the biodiversity area. These areas may have more limited wildlife functions than other priority habitat areas due to the general nature and constraints of these sites in that they are often isolated or surrounded by highly urbanized lands. 
80
 Biodiversity areas meet at least one of the following criteria based on the IUCN 2018 guidelines 81 :
• supports a high diversity of species, habitats or ecosystems, especially these of high conservation priority (listed in PHS and/or as Species of Greatest Conservation Need).
• is large enough to sustain genetically diverse, viable population(s) of highly threatened or rare species, ensuring long-term adaptability through sufficient birth and survival rates that counteract mortality and maintain genetic diversity.
• holds geographically restricted (e.g., endemic) populations of species or ecosystems. 
78
 Cores are usually large, internally well-connected blocks of relatively high-quality habitat where habitat function and accessibility to the species that depend on it are not impaired by human activities. "Large" and "high quality" are relative to conservation objectives and may differ by species/habitat relationships and spatial scale of the analysis or planning (statewide, regional, local). "Internally well-connected" means there are relatively few or no human infrastructure barriers or adverse influences on fish and wildlife use of the area. The edge of a core is often a gradient, not a hard line or border, and that edge is determined by influences on habitat quality (e.g. disruptions in canopy closure or loss of understory, light or noise penetration, non-native species invasion) that may affect how the core does or does not support species' needs. • maintains a high degree of naturalness and ecological integrity, meaning its ecological processes and functions are largely intact and relatively undisturbed by human activities.
• serves as a crucial refuge for species or ecosystems, allowing them to persist during periods of environmental stress (e.g., drought, extreme temperatures), or providing a critical refuge from human impacts.
• is irreplaceable for the persistence of biodiversity across the larger landscape (e.g., a core habitat connecting other source populations). The pace and scale of habitat and system science and planning has greatly advanced in Washington in just two years. The Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan advances the concept of biodiversity areas with cores and connectivity values.

## Biodiversity Area Conservation Recommendations

Apply the recommendations of the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan for spatial data improvements that help the conservation community scale and plan better core and corridor protection for biodiversity.
7. 

## Refugia

Refugia are areas where local biophysical conditions reduce the rate or magnitude of climate exposure relative to the surrounding landscape. These locations allow for the persistence of species, populations, or ecological processes that might otherwise decline or disappear under a changing climate (Morelli et al. 2016, Keppel et al. 2015). Refugia may arise from topographic complexity, persistent hydrological inputs, vegetation structure, other site-specific features that moderate local climatic conditions, such as temperature extremes or moisture deficits (Dobrowski 2011; Ashcroft 2010).
Anthropogenic climate change is altering ecosystems across spatial and temporal scales. Overserved impacts include range shifts, phenological mismatches, altered disturbance regimes, and increased physiological stress on species (Parmesan 2006; Pacifici et al. 2015). These stressors can lead to reduced reproductive success, local extirpations, and ecosystem restructuring, particularly for taxa with limited dispersal capacity or narrow in environmental tolerances.
Within this context, climate refugia provide spatially discrete areas where environmental conditions remain within the physiological or ecological limits for resident biota. For plant and animal species, these areas can function as demographic anchors-supporting breeding populations, offering thermal or hydrological refuge during climate extremes, or enabling life cycle completion when broader habitat conditions become unsuitable (Morelli et al. 2020; Krawchuk et al. 2020). In forested ecosystems, for example, dense canopy cover and topographic shading can reduce understory temperatures and moisture loss, facilitating presence of moisture-sensitive species. In aquatic ecosystems, cold-water refuges maintained by groundwater or hyporheic exchange can buffer fish and amphibian populations from thermally stressful conditions during droughts or heatwaves (Issak et al. 2015).
To be effective, refugia must support ecological function and demographic persistence over relevant timeframes. Their benefits are particularly pronounced in landscapes undergoing rapid change, where refugia may serve as important sources of recolonization, genetic diversity, or phenotypic plasticity. However, refugia are not immune to change. Their capacity to buffer climate impacts may diminish under high greenhouse gas emission scenarios or when interacting stressors-such as land use change, invasive species, or altered fire regimes-overwhelm localized buffering mechanisms (Davis et al. 2023; Frey et al. 2016).
Recognizing the importance of these areas, recent conservation planning frameworks have integrated climate refugia into spatial prioritization and adaptive management strategies. These approaches aim to identify and protect areas that offer bioclimatic stability and ecological resilience under climate change.

## Refugia Conservation Recommendations

Consider refugia protection in land use planning decisions, site protection and conservation incentives, water quality and quantity decisions, and landscape core and connectivity planning. Some good resources to inform this work: 

## Corridors and Connectivity

Connectivity refers to the degree to which landscape's structure and composition facilitates animal movement. 
85
 It is defined by the needs of the organisms that live and move within it, making it speciesand context-dependent.
Conceptually a connected landscape is a matrix consisting of larger blocks of quality habitat (cores) linked by habitat of lesser quality but permeable enough to allow movement between cores within species-specific maximum distance. Such a matrix can represent a diffuse connectivity with few barriers to impede or constrain movement, or more concentrated connectivity where habitat loss constrains or narrows movement options, forming corridors between core habitats. Depending on the level of habitat conversion these corridors can be wide or channelized routes allowing wildlife movement through otherwise heavily modified landscapes. 86 Furthermore, corridors can include small patches of quality habitat ("stepping stones"), not large enough to sustain populations but allowing animals to rest, find food and shelter on their journey between cores. The two main components of a conceptual connectivity network are therefore cores and corridors, each with a distinct role in conservation. It is important to note, however, that while these are often modeled as distinct entities, they are not empirically separate categories in the real world. A single location could be validly designated as a core or a corridor depending on the species, scale, and analytical objectives.
Cores are blocks of habitat large enough to support wildlife populations and their essential life functions. 
87
 They are characterized by high habitat quality and a low human footprint, meaning they are internally well-connected and largely uninterrupted by major barriers. Because of their size and lack of fragmentation, these areas provide a high level of intrinsic connectivity. They are also the critical places that a connectivity network seeks to link together. Protecting these intact core areas is the foundational goal of any connectivity conservation effort. 88
Corridors are the pathways or linkages between habitat cores. For most large and highly mobile species, corridors provide habitat sufficient to facilitate movement but are not required to sustain permanent populations. Species with limited mobility may need to reside within corridors and only achieve migration over multiple generations. In core-corridor modeling, corridors differ from cores because their habitat quality is lower or too linear to function as core habitat. 
89
 Corridors facilitate gene flow by allowing individuals to move between populations to find mates. This genetic exchange enhances the overall genetic variability of metapopulations, reducing their vulnerability to demographic stochasticity and preventing inbreeding depression, thereby making them more resilient to environmental fluctuations. 
90
 Corridors also support species seasonal migrations for breeding, foraging, and wintering, e.g., vertical migrations between high and low-elevation habitats.
Finally, corridors can act as a pathway for species to shift their geographic ranges in response to a changing climate. 
91
 Climate corridors are distinct from traditional wildlife corridors because they are explicitly designed to connect habitats along a climate gradient allowing altitudinal and latitudinal range shifts.
86 
Schloss et al. 2021. 87
 Forman. 1995 88 Fahrig. 2003.
89 Bennett et al. 2003, Hilty et al. 2019, Michalak et al. 2025 90 Packer at al. 1991, Cushman 2006 91 Krosby et al. 2010 Key characteristics of wildlife corridors
foot_28
 :
Connects core habitats: must link at least two distinct core habitat areas.
Facilitates safe movement: enables movement of species, especially highly threatened or rare ones (listed in PHS and/or as Species of Greatest Conservation Need), between habitat patches. This includes movements for:
• Daily foraging and resource access.
• Seasonal migration.
• Dispersal.
• Genetic exchange for population health.
Provides suitable internal habitat: offers adequate cover, food, and water within the corridor to support passage or even extended residency for "corridor dwellers".
Contains minimal barriers or impediments to movement such as:
• Human infrastructure (e.g., roads, fences, high-density development).
• Human activity (e.g., noise, light pollution).
• Increased predation risk from "bottleneck" effects.
Optimal physical characteristics:
• Width: generally, wider corridors are more effective, with optimal width determined by target species' home range size and sensitivity to edge effects and adjoining human disturbances (e.g., roadways).
• Length: sufficiently long to connect target habitats while accommodating species' movement and dispersal abilities. Generally, corridors can be narrower when the cores being connected are nearby and must be wider when connecting cores across a longer distance.
• Continuity: can be continuous strips of habitat or "stepping stone" patches, depending on species and landscape.
Landscape context: corridors benefit from minimized human impacts and similar adjacent habitat types.
Enhances population resilience: contributes to the ability of populations to adapt to environmental stressors like climate change by allowing for range shifts.
A specific type of corridor that follows streams and rivers is a riparian corridor. The vegetation and undeveloped floodplains adjacent to streams and rivers make them natural pathways for wildlife movement through otherwise fragmented habitat. 
93
 In urban or agricultural areas, a riparian corridor may be the only intact habitat remaining. 
94
 Riparian corridors also function as climate corridors, allowing species to move up in elevation in response to warming temperatures. 
95
 Protecting and restoring riparian areas supports both aquatic ecosystems and terrestrial wildlife movement. Riparian habitat is identified as a Priority Habitat by WDFW 96 and is protected by local Critical Areas Ordinances under the Growth Management Act.

## Connectivity network across Washington:

The Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan (WAHCAP) maps and defines connectivity in Washington. 
97
 The WAHCAP spatial data on ArcGIS Online provides a set of spatial layers depicting different types of connectivity functions including structural ecosystem connectivity, network importance, landscape permeability, focal species connectivity, climate connectivity, and existing prioritized networks. The maps include ecosystem-specific cores mapped using a tiered approach to represent different thresholds of human impact sensitivity by systematically relaxing habitat condition requirements and human footprint thresholds used to define cores and corridors. The platform also includes a layer illustrating how these individual cores and corridors are integrated into 13 terrestrial Connected Landscapes of Statewide Significance (Figure 
12
). The connectivity across the state varies considerably by region, as described below:
• Southwest Washington and Olympic Peninsula: This area is comprised of one extensive block of highest quality core centered on Olympic Mountains and many smaller cores embedded in a diffusely connected landscape of forestry lands in the southern part of the peninsula and Willapa Hills. Historically rural, this area is experiencing rapid residential and commercial development along I-5 and other major highways, which is resulting in smaller cores and highly constrained corridors in portions of the southern Cascades.
• Northwest Washington: Few, small, and fragmented cores remain in this region due to extensive human impact. The expansion of cities such as Bellingham and Everett, alongside smaller suburban communities like North Bend, contributes to the ongoing loss of remnant forests and landscape connectivity. Furthermore, fragmentation from major highways (I-5 and I-90) significantly restricts wildlife movement.
• further impacts the region's ecological integrity. The highways also provide easier access to recreation areas, particularly within national forests, intensifying the ecological impact on existing core areas.
• Northeast Washington: Characterized by mountains and valleys with sparse urban development (except for Spokane), the region retains relatively large and numerous well-connected, highquality core areas. The Okanogan River Valley, however, is heavily influenced by agriculture, residential development, and US 97, creating a fracture zone within an otherwise diffusely connected landscape. Connectivity between Northeast Washington and the Cascades is ecologically important, particularly for shrubsteppe habitats in the Okanogan River Valley, which face fragmentation from land conversion, development, and wildfire.
• Columbia Plateau and Blue Mountains: The Columbia Plateau is dominated by sagebrush steppe, which has experienced significant declines due to substantial land conversion for agriculture, ranching, residential and commercial developments, and renewable energy projects. The region's remaining shrubsteppe cores on the Columbia Plateau are highly fragmented, with several larger and many small, sparsely distributed cores having relatively weak connections. In contrast, the Blue Mountains predominantly feature dry forest ecosystems with several larger and well-connected cores. Several major highways (e.g., I-90, I-82, US 12, and US 97) significantly fragment cores across both the Columbia Plateau and Blue Mountains, creating substantial barriers to wildlife movement.

## Connectivity Conservation Recommendations

The most important statewide conservation action is implementation of the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan (2025). Additional conservation actions are provided in the Statewide and Transboundary: Habitat Connectivity section.

foot_29

Strategic Habitats are identified that provide significant benefits to many SGCN and/or systems which are at risk:
• 15 Priority Habitats that are not features 99 (Table

## Aspen Stands

Quaking aspen (Populus tremuloides) is the characteristic and dominant tree in this habitat (Crawford and Kagen 2001). It is the sole dominant in many stands although scattered ponderosa pine (Pinus ponderosa) or Douglas-fir (Pseudotsuga menziesii) may be present. Snowberry (Symphoricarpos oreophilus and less frequently S. albus) is the most common dominant shrub (Crawford and Kagen 2001). This habitat generally occurs on well-drained mountain slopes or canyon walls that have some moisture (Crawford and Kagen 2001, WDFW 2015). Rockfalls, talus, or stony north slopes are often typical sites. Aspen stands may occur in steppe on moist microsites, but is not associated with streams, ponds, or wetlands (Crawford and Kagen 2001, WDFW 2015). This habitat is found from 2,000 to 9,500 ft elevation primarily in the montane regions of eastern Washington (Crawford and Kagen 2001). It is found sporadically in valleys west of the Cascades in Washington. Aspen Stands is a designated priority habitat type in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species (PHS) program (WDFW 2008). In PHS, aspen stands are a priority when they are at least an acre in size.
Fire plays an important role in maintenance of this habitat (Crawford and Kagen 2001). Conifers now dominate many aspen stands and extensive stands of young aspen are uncommon partly because of fire suppression. With fire suppression and alteration of fine fuels, fire rejuvenation of aspen habitat has 100 PHS definition of Priority Habitat 
https://wdfw.wa.gov/sites/default/files/publications/00165/wdfw00165.pdf
 been greatly reduced. Heavy livestock browsing, primarily by domestic sheep and cattle, have adversely impacted aspen growth and regeneration (Crawford and Kagen 2001, WDFW 2015).

## Fresh Deepwater

Deepwater habitats are generally pools deeper than 2 meters, permanently flooded lands lying below the deepwater boundary of wetlands where surface water is permanent so that water, rather than air, is the principal medium within which the dominant organisms live. The dominant plants are hydrophytes; however, the substrates are considered nonsoil because the water is too deep to support emergent vegetation. These habitats include all underwater structures and features (e.g., woody debris, rock piles, caverns).
Hydrologically in streams, they are created through massive areas of scour, such as meanders, large wood structures, large boulders, or after any increased roughness areas. Depending on flow, these pools can vary in depth and size. General characteristics include low flow velocities, sometimes near-standing water conditions, and typically sediment particles drop off and aggregate if the scour is not sustained. Deepwater habitats provide rearing habitat, refuge from above water predators with sufficient depth, flow and velocity refuge, temperature refuge, and depending on fish life history strategies can provide areas where to spawn. During low flow or ephemeral conditions, deep pool provides refuge and oasis for fish and wildlife until stream flows return. In some instances can provide sufficient nutrients for fish, amphibian and other water related wildlife to maintain their livelihood until rains return to replenish the stream. They may also be created by structures -dams that impound water behind them. Ecoregion(s): ALL NVC Group(s) and/or GET Functional Group(s):
Artesian Springs and Oases (F2.8) Large Permanent Freshwater Lakes (GET F2.1) Geothermal Pools and Wetlands (F2.9) Small Permanent Freshwater Lakes (F2.2) Freeze-thaw Freshwater Lakes (F2.4) Western Temperate Freshwater Aquatic Vegetation (NVC G544)* Ephemeral Freshwater Lakes (F2.5) Human-created or -modified groups that can function as this habitat: Western Temperate Ruderal Aquatic Vegetation (NVC G974), Large Reservoirs (GET F3.1), Freshwater Aquafarms (F3.4) Additional Resource: Beneficial reuse of dredged material for habitat creation: role_of_the_federal_standard_in_the_beneficial_use_of_dredged_material.pdf

## Freshwater Wetlands

Wetlands are areas where water covers the soil or is present either at or near the surface of the soil all year or for varying periods of time during the year. The type is determined by some period of water saturation (hydrology) that determines how the soil develops and the types of plant and animal communities living in and on the soil. Wetlands may support both aquatic and terrestrial species. Longer-term water presence creates conditions that favor the growth of specially adapted plants (hydrophytes) and promote the development of characteristic wetland (hydric) soils. 
101
 Two general categories of wetlands
foot_31
 are recognized: coastal or tidal wetlands and inland or non-tidal wetlands. For SWAP, Freshwater Wetlands (this category) includes inland or non-tidal wetland such as those along floodplains or rivers associated with riparian habitats, isolated depressions on uplands such as "potholes", ponds, natural lakes, vernal pools, bogs, fens, freshwater marsh, swamps, and wet meadows. Tidal wetlands are part of the Nearshore environment, two other Strategic HGCNs in this section.
Wetlands are vital transitional ecosystems that form the interface between terrestrial and aquatic environments. Found in areas where water collects or flows, either permanently or seasonally, these ecosystems harness solar energy to cycle nutrients and support an exceptional diversity of life, including many endemic plant and animal species. Although commonly associated with standing water, wetlands are not always wet year-round. Their hydrology can vary widely depending on weather, temperature extremes, topography, and groundwater influence. Seasonal and annual variation is a key feature that contributes to their ecological complexity and productivity.
Wetlands perform a wide array of ecosystem functions that provide direct and indirect benefits to both natural systems and human communities. They are critical in purifying water by trapping sediments and filtering pollutants such as nitrogen and phosphorus through plant uptake and microbial processes. This natural filtration supports the health of downstream aquatic ecosystems and improves water quality for human use. Flood mitigation is another key function of wetlands. By temporarily storing excess rainfall and surface water, wetlands reduce the severity and frequency of floods. This capacity to hold water also contributes to groundwater recharge, helping maintain aquifer levels and base flows in rivers during dry periods. In coastal and riparian areas, wetlands play a role in stabilizing shorelines and reducing erosion by anchoring soils with dense vegetation.
Ecologically, wetlands are among the most productive habitats. Despite their importance, wetlands are highly vulnerable to land-use change, development, pollution, and climate impacts. Their loss or degradation can lead to diminished water quality, increased flood risk, and declines in wildlife populations. Recognizing, protecting, and restoring wetlands is essential to maintaining the health of ecosystems and the services they provide to society. Their plant communities form the foundation of complex food webs that support amphibians, reptiles, birds, mammals, and countless invertebrate species. Wetland vegetation provides essential resources such as cover, nesting habitat, and food for a wide range of wildlife. Herbivores rely on the nutrient-rich plant matter, while many species use wetlands for breeding, migration stopovers, or overwintering sites. Wetlands are found across Washington with unique regional hydrology and ecology that protect our water, support biodiversity, and benefit site and landscape resilience for both nature and people. 

## Herbaceous Balds

Herbaceous balds are a designated priority habitat type in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program (WDFW 2008). They typically consist of low-growing vegetation adapted for survival on shallow soils amid seasonally dry conditions and is often on steep slopes (WDFW 2008). Although usually smaller than 5 ha (12 ac), some herbaceous balds can be as large as 100 ha (≅ 250 ac; Chappell 2006, WDFW 2008). Dominant flora includes herbaceous vegetation, dwarf shrubs, mosses, and lichens. Rock outcrops, boulders, and scattered trees are often present in balds, especially Douglas-fir (Pseudotsuga menziesii), Pacific madrone (Arbutus menziesii), and Oregon white oak (Quercus garryana). Mature trees, if present are generally rooted in localized microsites with greater late summer soil moisture (e.g., fissures in bedrock or deeper soil pockets; Chappell 2006). Rock outcrops are often interspersed within bald vegetation at such fine scales that they are typically considered part of the same ecosystem. In contrast, cliffs may co-occur but are classified as a distinct ecosystem. Unlike cliffs or rock outcrops, herbaceous balds have major portions with soil covering rock surfaces (Chappell 2006).
Rangewide, herbaceous balds occur within mid-montane to lowland forest zones from eastern Vancouver Island south to at least the southern end of the Willamette Valley and adjacent slopes of the Coast Ranges and western Cascades (NatureServe 2025). Although uncommon across Washington, balds are widely distributed in western Washington (Rocchio and Crawford 2015). Herbaceous balds are most abundant in the northern Puget Trough (San Juan Islands and vicinity), the driest climatic area in western Washington, and in the Columbia River Gorge, where drying winds may be in important ecological factor (Rocchio and Crawford 2015).
Herbaceous balds are notable for their high plant diversity (Chappell 2006). Some rare or threatened animal species, most notably Taylor's checkerspot (Euphydryas editha taylori), are limited to balds and closely similar habitats (Potter et al. 2016). Maintaining early-seral conditions through periodic fire, mowing, or other disturbance is essential for preventing conifer and shrub encroachment into herbaceous balds. These actions help preserve the open, low-stature vegetation structure that Taylor's checkerspot and other bald associated species require (USFWS 2024).
The exclusion of fire from most of this system over the last 100+ years has resulted in profound changes in bald vegetation (Rocchio and Crawford 2015), including encroachment of Douglas-fir and other woody species, loss of native herbaceous cover, and reduced habitat for open-habitat specialists. In addition to altered disturbance regimes, anthropogenic impacts such as road building, timber harvest, and recreational use further degrade bald ecosystems. Road building, when it occurs within a bald, is clearly the most destructive of these activities, resulting in habitat degradation and the spread of nonnative plants (Chappell 2006). Maintaining ecological integrity in bald systems requires a combination of disturbance-based management such as prescribed fire and protection from further physical alterations.
