## Inland Dunes

Washington's inland dunes are composed of sparsely (<20% cover) to moderately vegetated (>60% cover), active and stabilized dunes and sandsheets, with vegetation cover related to the amount of annual rainfall, rate of evapotranspiration, and wind stress (Hallock et al., 2007; Rocchio and Crawford, 2015). Described in Hallock et al. (2007), four simple dune types have been observed in Washington:
• Longitudinal dunes, long in proportion to width and symmetrical in cross section, form when there is a small to moderate supply of sand, much wind and little vegetation;
• Transverse dunes form when there is a copious sand supply, little to moderate wind and little vegetation;
• Parabolic or U-shaped dunes form when there is a moderate supply of sand, wind and vegetation, with vegetation encroachment being essential to their development; and
• Climbing dunes, which climb the windward side of hills as sand sheets. Dune systems are considered "complex" if they have more than one type of simple dune present in the system.
Also described in Hallock et al. (2007), inland dunes contain three different functional stages: 1) open/migrating, 2) anchored, and 3) stabilized, with these stages differing primarily in the amount of sand movement on the dune surface and movement of the dune itself. The open/migrating stage has large areas of open active surface sand and the dune migrates with the effective wind direction and vegetation cover is minimal. Anchored dunes have active sands present on the dune surface, but movement / migration of the dune is inhibited by vegetation. Stabilized dunes lack active sands, as the dune surface is sealed with vegetation, cryptobiotic crusts, or volcanic ash. The anchored and stabilized stages are considered either "native" or "exotic" based on the dominant (>50% of relative cover) vegetation.
Plants occupying these environments are often adapted to shifting, coarse-textured substrates (usually quartz sand) and form patchy or open grasslands, shrubsteppe, and occasionally woodlands. Hallock et al. (2007) described seven distinct plant community types associated with Washington's dunes (Appendix III), with species composition related to the degree of sand stabilization, vegetation cover and position on a particular dune; however, the authors also noted that several dune systems across inland Washington had unique species compositions that were not captured by those seven community types. Generally, the authors suggested that bunchgrasses and herbs typically dominate initial stages of dune stabilization (Hallock et al. 2007). With increased stabilization shrubs appear, and pine and juniper may occur.
Sand dunes are by nature highly dynamic systems and patterns of plant species composition are closely related to the processes of sand erosion, deposition and dune migration and stabilization (Chadwick and Dalke 1965). These processes may occur rapidly, leaving legacies from previous vegetation types. While repeating patterns of vegetation are observed and allow the identification of community types, they are often present in a spatially complex, fine-scale mosaic, and the boundaries between community types range from distinct to highly blurred (Easterly and Salstrom 1997). Our community types are, however, closely related to the successional processes of dune vegetation and stabilization and often have strong affinities to particular positions within a dune system.
Wind and continual supply of sand are necessary for dune dynamics (Rocchio and Crawford, 2015). As described in Hallock et al., (2007), formation of sand dunes requires a supply of well-sorted fine or medium grained (0.125-0.5 mm) sand and wind (eolian) transport. The wind moves the sand grains by saltation (bouncing) along the surface. Sand accumulates when wind passes from a rough surface to a smooth one (e.g., sand patch), flows over a depression and/or encounters a permeable obstacle such as a shrub. Dunes accumulate (accrete) sand during strong winds and lose sand during gentle winds until they reach a critical size. Once this size is attained, sand is trapped under all wind conditions due to aerodynamic factors that result in sand being dropped at the leeward margin of the dune instead of being carried off the dune (Smith 1982). Long periods of increased precipitation and persistent presence of vegetation may lead to covering of the sand surface with litter and/or microphytic crust, soil formation and partial to complete stabilization of the dunes. In contrast, periods of drought will result in conditions unfavorable to vegetation and can reinitiate the mobility of the sands (Lancaster 1988).
As of 2007, inland dunes in Washington occurred in 13 counties across eastern Washington with the largest extant systems occurring in Benton, Franklin, and Grant Counties (Hallock et al., 2007). Hallock et al. (2007) estimated that dunes historically covered almost 450,000 acres in Washington. Extensive sand dune systems in Washington formed from wind acting on alluvial sediments derived from the Missoula floods (Gaylord and Stetler 1994, Gaylord et al. 2001, Banfield et al. 2002, Dalman et al. 2005), and glacial outburst flood-derived sands (Gaylord et al. 2001). Dune field activity and migration was slowed in the Columbia Basin approximately 3,900 years ago when cooler, moister conditions resulted in expansion of basin vegetation (Gaylord et al. 2001). Further, damming of the Columbia and Snake rivers impacted sand transport and deposition which historically fed dunes along those rivers.
The total extent of Washington inland sand dune systems declined approximately 76% from the early 1970s (Hallock et al., 2007; Rocchio and Crawford, 2015) 

## Juniper Savanna

Juniper savanna is a designated priority habitat type in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program (WDFW 2008). Western juniper (Juniperus occidentalis) is the characteristic and dominant tree in this habitat (Rocchio and Crawford 2015). Ponderosa pine (Pinus ponderosa) and Oregon white oak (Quercus garryana) may also be present. Big sagebrush (Artemisia tridentata) is the most common shrub within juniper ecosystems. Bunchgrasses and sedges common to shrubsteppe such as Idaho fescue (Festuca idahoensis), Sandberg bluegrass (Poa secunda), and bluebunch wheatgrass (Pseudoroegneria spicata) are common within juniper woodlands and savanna (Rocchio and Crawford 2015).
In Washington, this important habitat is mostly restricted to eastern Klickitat, southern Benton and Franklin counties. Sites with either juniper savanna or woodlands are restricted to areas with excessively drained soils, such as sand dunes, rock outcrops or escarpments (Rocchio and Crawford 2015).
With fire suppression, overgrazing, and changing climatic factors, western juniper stands have become denser (i.e., less herbaceous understory) and this system has expanded its range into adjacent shrubsteppe, grasslands, and savannas (Rocchio and Crawford 2015). This elimination of periodic low-intensity fires has degraded existing juniper woodlands while also allowing expansion of this same type into other ecological systems.

## Kelp

Kelp forests are comprised of groups of brown macroalgae which provide structurally complex and highly productive intertidal and subtidal habitats. These ecosystem engineers support critical habitat for a variety of marine life, serving as nurseries, migration corridors, foraging grounds for fish and bird species, and refuge areas (Mumford 2007, Berry et al. 2023). They also provide habitat for vulnerable concentrations such as forage fish breeding habitat and concentration areas, while also delivering ecosystem services such as carbon sequestration, wave attenuation, primary production, and localized ocean acidification buffering (Calloway et al. 2020).
Washington has 22 species of kelp, which include canopy-forming kelp such as bull kelp (Nereocystis luetkeana) and giant kelp (Macrocystis pyrifera), as well as understory kelp such as sugar kelp (Saccharina latissima) (Calloway 2020). Together, canopy-forming and understory kelps provide structure throughout the water column. Kelp forests typically occur on rocky substrates in moderate to high-energy wave environments and extend from the lower intertidal zone to depths of approximately 30 meters, depending on turbidity and light penetration (Mumford 2007, DNR 2023). They are unique in that they can grow on rocky reefs, where few other types of marine vegetation persist, due to their holdfast system.
Kelp forests are broadly distributed along Washington's Pacific Coast, particularly around rocky substrates and along portions of the Strait of Juan de Fuca. In Puget Sound, kelp forests are more fragmented and less dense but occur in high-relief, exposed locations such as the San Juan Islands, and the west side of Whidbey Island (DNR 2023, Calloway et al. 2020).
The status of kelp forests is often driven by local conditions, and there are varying trends across Washington. For example, while floating kelp populations have demonstrated long-term stability along the northern Pacific Coast and Strait of Juan de Fuca, in the central and south Puget Sound kelp is no longer present for approximately 80% of shorelines where it was observed in the past (Berry et al. 2023). Some regions, such as Admiralty Inlet and North Puget Sound, lack sufficient historical data to make assessments of trends (Berry et al. 2023). Trend monitoring is primarily focused on canopy-forming kelp, as it can be assessed in aerial imagery, while understory kelp cannot.
The structure and species composition of kelp forests are maintained by a combination of factors including light availability, nutrient availability (particularly during periods of growth in spring and early summer), wave exposure, and hard substrate (Mumford 2007, Calloway et al. 2020). Kelp forests are experiencing stressors such as invasive seaweeds, warming temperatures, changes in sediment loading, grazing pressure from macrograzers like urchins, and shoreline development and modification (Calloway et al. 2020). While kelp canopy area has remained relatively stable along much of the Pacific Coast, loss and degradation in urban or ecologically stressed regions causes concern regarding long-term resilience in these areas.
Kelp also carries vital cultural significance to Tribal and First Nations communities in the Pacific Northwest, representing a physical link between land and sea as well as interdependence between indigenous people and the sea (Turner 2001, Calloway et al. 2020). Kelp plays a spiritual and symbolic role among many traditional Northwest Coast cultures, as it is often represented in Coast Salish stories and oral histories (Calloway et al. 2020).
Conservation Action.

## Nearshore -Pacific Coast

The Washington Pacific Coast nearshore is the transitional zone between terrestrial, freshwater, and marine ecosystems along the outer coast of Washington. It spans from the mouth of the Columbia River to Cape Flattery at the entrance to the Strait of Juan de Fuca (Swanson 2001, Skegwar and Pearson 2011). The Pacific Coast nearshore includes the shorelines and shallow marine waters of the Pacific Ocean, and estuarine systems such as Willapa Bay, Grays Harbor, and the lower reaches of river deltas. The zone extends from the landward edge of marine influence-typically the top of coastal bluffs, foredunes, or cliffs adjacent to beaches, or the upper limit of tidal influence in estuaries and coastal rivers-seaward to include shallow waters and submerged lands to a depth of 100 meters. This depth allows for the inclusion of subtidal habitats as well as upwelling processes along the coastal shelf (Andrews et al. 2015). The nearshore also includes estuarine bays to their maximum depth. The Pacific Coast nearshore provides ecosystem services including wave attenuation, shoreline stabilization, sediment retention, carbon sequestration, and nutrient cycling. However, these functions are increasingly threatened by shoreline armoring, estuarine filling, invasive species, altered sediment supply, and the impacts of sea level rise (DOE 2015). Shoreline development and coastal infrastructure can disrupt nearshore processes, leading to erosion, habitat loss, and reduced ecological function.
Shore: Also called the marine riparian zone, shore habitat extends inland from the Ordinary High Water Mark (OHWM) to that portion of the terrestrial landscape that is influenced by, or that directly influences, the aquatic ecosystem. The shore takes in feeder bluffs (i.e., eroding bluffs), as they are an important source of sediments that form and sustain beaches. Shores consisting of native vegetation (e.g., trees, shrubs, dune grasses), fine-grained sand, imbedded large woody debris, or actively eroding bluffs are of particular importance. Headlands with concentrated seabird use and marine mammal haulout sites are also significant.
Intertidal: Extends from the OHWM to the extreme lower low water (ELLW). Intertidal areas consisting of rocky substrate, native vegetation (e.g., eelgrass macroalgae, emergent vegetation) or habitatforming species (e.g., goose-necked barnacles, mussel beds, oyster reefs) are of particular importance.
Intertidal areas within a river/stream delta or an area used for spawning by forage fish are also significant.
Subtidal: Extends from ELLW to -100 meters. Subtidal areas within an estuarine embayment or areas consisting of rocky substrate, native vegetation (e.g., eelgrass, macroalgae), or habitat-forming species (e.g., corals, sponges) are of particular importance.
Conservation Actions.

## Nearshore -Puget Sound/Salish Sea

The Puget Sound nearshore is the transitional zone between terrestrial, freshwater, and marine ecosystems (Cereghino et al. 2012). It encompasses areas of the Puget Sound, the Strait of Juan de Fuca, Admiralty Inlet, the San Juan Islands, and Hood Canal. The Puget Sound nearshore extends from the landward edge of marine influence-typically the top of coastal bluffs adjacent to beaches, or the upper limit of tidal influence in estuaries, rivers, and streams-seaward to the maximum depth of Puget Sound (EnviroVision et al. 2010, Schlenger at al. 2011). The Puget Sound nearshore also includes estuaries and bays extending to their full depth.
Habitats in the Puget Sound nearshore are affected by tidal exchange, wave energy, freshwater input, sediment transport, and marine and terrestrial-derived nutrients (Simenstad et al. 2011). This zone includes a variety of marine riparian and shallow-water habitats, including mudflats, salt marshes, eelgrass beds, understory kelp beds, canopy-forming kelp forests, pocket estuaries, and barrier beaches. Geomorphic systems in the Puget Sound nearshore include rocky coasts, beaches, embayments, and large river deltas (Shipman 2008, Shipman 2014); substrate characteristics determine species composition and suitability of habitat for spawning, foraging and rearing for nearshore species.
Structural features such as large woody debris, wrack lines, shellfish beds, and overhanging vegetation provide habitat complexity and refuge. Puget Sound nearshore areas support priority species including juvenile salmon and forage fish during critical life stages, as well as juvenile rockfish, juvenile Pacific Cod, and many other species of groundfish during their early life history stages (Love et al. 2002, Clancy 2009). Pocket estuaries and tidal channels are of particular importance for juvenile salmonids, providing low-salinity refuge and high productivity feeding grounds (Clancy 2009, EnviroVision et al. 2010). The Puget Sound nearshore also provides habitat for vulnerable concentrations, such as forage fish breeding habitat and concentration areas, western Washington breeding concentrations of cormorants, stormpetrels, terns and alcids, and nonbreeding concentrations of shorebirds.
The Puget Sound nearshore provides ecosystem services including wave attenuation and storm protection, carbon sequestration, and water quality regulation. However, shoreline modification degrades nearshore processes and services including sediment supply to beaches, accumulation of woody debris, terrestrial-marine connectivity, and migration corridors for salmon and forage fish (Cereghino et al. 2012, HSIL 2021). Approximately 29% of Puget Sound's 2,500-mile shoreline is armored (HSIL 2021). Coastal development, paired with shoreline modification, inhibits the services offered by the nearshore, particularly with the increasing impacts of sea level rise leading to coastal squeeze of nearshore habitats (Clancy 2009).
Priority habitat zones within the Puget Sound nearshore are:
Shore: Also called the marine riparian zone, shore habitat extends inland from the Ordinary High Water Mark (OHWM) to that portion of the terrestrial landscape that is influenced by, or that directly influences, the aquatic ecosystem. The shore takes in feeder bluffs (i.e., eroding bluffs), as they are an important source of sediments that form and sustain beaches. Shores consisting of native vegetation (e.g., trees, shrubs, dune grasses), fine-grained sand, imbedded large woody debris and wrack, and actively eroding bluffs are of particular importance. Headlands with concentrated seabird use and marine mammal haulout sites are also significant.
Intertidal: Extends from the OHWM to the extreme lower low water (ELLW). Intertidal areas consisting of rocky substrate, native vegetation (e.g., eelgrass, macroalgae, emergent vegetation) or habitatforming species (e.g., native oyster reefs) are of particular importance. Intertidal areas within a river/stream delta, estuarine embayment, or a pocket beach, or an area used for spawning by forage fish are also significant.
Subtidal: Extends from ELLW to the maximum depth of Puget Sound. Subtidal areas consisting of rocky substrate, native vegetation (e.g., eelgrass, macroalgae), or habitat-forming species (e.g., sea pens, native oyster reefs) are of particular importance. Subtidal areas within an estuarine embayment or an area used for spawning by forage fish are also significant.

## Oregon White Oak Woodlands

Oregon white oak (Quercus garryana) woodland is a designated priority habitat type in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program (WDFW 2008). Oregon white oak is the only native oak in Washington and thus represents a distinct and ecologically significant element of the region's biodiversity (Michalak 2011). Oak woodlands in Washington support a rich diversity of flora and fauna and the trees are ecologically important as a source of food and shelter to a wide range of wildlife (Michalak 2011). . The growth and survival of individual Oregon white oaks in a stand is strongly affected by stand density, competitive pressure, tree size, and site conditions (Gould et al. 2011). Oregon white oak woodlands were historically maintained in stands with relatively low tree densities through frequent burning by Native Americans (Thilenius 1968).
The range of Oregon white oak extends from southern Vancouver Island in British Columbia to the coastal mountains south of San Francisco (Peter and Harrington 2002). In Washington, Oregon white oak woodlands are found on both eastern and western regions of the state. In western Washington, they are most common on dry, well-drained sites in South Puget Sound and in the Willamette Valley of southwest Washington (Rocchio and Crawford 2015). Smaller, more scattered stands also occur in the Olympic Peninsula rain shadow. In eastern Washington, drier forms of oak woodland are found in the Columbia River Gorge, extending north through Yakima and Kittitas counties. There, oak occurs primarily on foothills and low-elevation slopes, often near the forest-steppe ecotone (Rocchio and Crawford 2015, WDFW 2015).
Most Oregon white oak woodland stands in eastern Washington are dominated by a mix of Oregon white oak and ponderosa pine (Pinus ponderosa) or Douglas-fir (Pseudotsuga menziesii), where scattered pine or fir comprise the upper canopy (Rocchio and Crawford 2015, WDFW 2015). Shrubsteppe vegetation may be prominent in some stands and create a distinct tree/shrub/sparse grassland habitat (Rocchio and Crawford 2015, WDFW 2015). The understory of oak woodlands in eastern Washington may include dense stands of shrubs or, more often, be dominated by bunchgrasses, sedges, or forbs. In the Columbia River Gorge, Oregon white oak can be found in dense patches often associated with grassland or shrubland balds within a closed Douglas-fir overstory forest landscape. Oregon white oak woodlands on the eastside of Washington occur at elevations ranging from 1,500 to 6,300 feet (Rocchio and Crawford 2015).
Oregon white oak woodlands in western Washington are most prevalent on gravelly outwash plains and on dry sites that historically experienced frequent pre-settlement fires (WDFW 2015). They occur as either large or small patches. The presence of Oregon white oak either as single species patches or where mixed with conifers characterizes these woodlands. Native shrubs such as snowberry (Symphoricarpos albus), Oregon grape (Mahonia aquifolium), Serviceberry (Amelanchier alnifolia) and the nonnative Scot's broom (Cytisus scoparius) are common in the understory (Rocchio and Crawford 2015). Many prairie grasses and forb species will also occur in the understory of oak savannas and open woodlands in western Washington (Vesely and Rosenberg 2010).
Oregon white oak woodlands are among North America's most imperiled vegetation types (Vesely and Rosenburg 2010, Rocchio and Crawford 2015). Estimates suggest that the extent of oak woodlands account for less than 10 percent of their historic range before non-indigenous settlement (Vesely and Rosenburg 2010). Almost all contemporary woodlands dominated by Oregon white oak occur in an early successional state due to human disturbances and because of the loss of cultural burning by native Americans (Vesely and Rosenberg 2010). In the absence of fire, Oregon white oak is usually excluded, or their growth becomes stunted with the presence of faster growing conifers (Vesely and Rosenberg 2010). Since the suppression of burning, more than half of the pre-settlement oak savannas and open woodlands are now dense forests of Douglas-fir, grand fir (Abies grandis), and bigleaf maple (Acer macrophyllum; Vesely and Tucker 2004). In some parts of the East Cascades, fire suppression combined with grazing has influenced fine fuel production and has led to encroachment by conifers and establishment of dense patches of small, shrubby oaks (ODFW 2016). Expansion of residential and commercial development has also replaced oak woodlands and has led to a significant decline in the quantity and quality of Oregon white oak habitat (Michalak 2011). All these impacts have since left only fragmented remnants and have dramatically reduced the extent of this once-expansive ecosystem (Nolan and Azerrad 2024). 

## Conservation Actions

## Issue

## Riparian and Floodplain

Riparian areas are transitional between terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems and are distinguished by gradients in biophysical conditions, ecological processes, and biota. They are areas through which surface and subsurface hydrology connect waterbodies with their adjacent uplands. They include those portions of terrestrial ecosystems that significantly influence exchanges of energy and matter with aquatic ecosystems (i.e., a zone of influence).
Riparian ecosystems are sometimes referred to by different names: riparian areas, riparian zones, or riparian habitats. To compound confusion, the "riparian zone" is sometimes identified as a distinctive area within riparian ecosystems (Sedell et al. 1989; Naiman et al. 1992; Steiner et al. 1994). Some definitions include the adjacent waters whereas others do not. Unless stated otherwise, we consider riparian area, riparian habitat, riparian corridor, and riparian ecosystem to be synonyms. Furthermore, our definition of riparian ecosystem does not include adjacent waters (i.e., rivers or streams) and recognizes the riparian zone as a distinctive area within riparian ecosystems. The riparian zone contains wet or moist soils and plants adapted to growing conditions associated with periodically saturated soils.
Riparian ecosystems are priority habitats in part because wildlife occurs more often and in greater variety in riparian areas than in any other habitat type. "Natural riparian corridors are the most diverse, dynamic, and complex biophysical habitats on the terrestrial portion of the earth" (Naiman et al. 1993).
Although riparian ecosystems constitute a small portion of the surface landscape, approximately 85% of Washington's wildlife species are known to use riparian areas associated with rivers and streams (Knutson and Naef 1997). Of these, 170 species including 134 mollusks, 11 amphibians, 3 reptiles, 10 birds, and 9 mammals may be riparian obligates, i.e., require riparian habitat to survive (T. Quinn, unpublished). In addition, habitat for many upland and aquatic species is directly enhanced by the presence of adjacent riparian areas. A principal reason for high fish and wildlife diversity is that riparian ecosystems are exceptionally productive. Riparian ecosystems are characterized by the availability of water, milder microclimate, and relatively fertile soils. These factors enhance the productivity of plant communities and support a complex food web that includes a rich variety and abundance of fish, invertebrates, amphibians, plants, bacteria, fungi, reptiles, birds, and mammals (Cummins 1974; Johnson and Carothers 1982).
Briefly, riparian areas help provide a variety of ecosystem goods and services: provisioning services such as food and water; regulating services such as decreasing flood flows; supporting services such as nutrient cycling, sediment and pollutant filtering, and carbon sequestration; and cultural services such as recreational, spiritual, and other nonmaterial benefits (MEA 2005). Riparian areas are vital "inbetween" zone where land and water meet. This area is incredibly important because it directly influences the water and the life within it by affecting things like shade, water temperature, the entry of wood and nutrients, and the removal of pollution from the land before it reaches the water. 

## Seagrass

Seagrass meadows in Washington are composed primarily of eelgrass and surfgrass. These flowering marine plants anchor in intertidal and shallow subtidal environments, forming complex benthic habitats that stabilize sediment and improve water quality. They are highly productive nearshore habitats and support a wide array of fish, waterfowl, and invertebrate species. Seagrass meadows also support vulnerable concentrations such as forage fish breeding habitat and concentration areas; breeding concentrations of cormorants, storm-petrels, terns, and alcids; and nonbreeding concentrations of loons, grebes, cormorants, fulmar, shearwaters, storm-petrels, and alcids.
Seagrass meadows also hold significant cultural value for Tribal Nations in Washington. These habitats support first foods such as herring, shellfish, and salmon, serving as part of the ecological basis for traditional fishing practices and seasonal gathering activities (DNR 2023). Many Tribal communities recognize eelgrass as vital to cultural continuity, treaty-reserved rights, and long-term environmental stewardship, leading restoration efforts and replanting projects (Northwest Treaty Tribes 2024).
Surfgrass (Phyllospadix spp.) is typically found in wave-exposed, rocky intertidal and shallow subtidal areas, sometimes co-occurring with kelp forests (Christiaen et al. 2022). Surfgrass thrives in regions such as the Strait of Juan de Fuca, the San Juan Islands, and isolated coastal headlands (Christiaen et al. 2022). While not as extensive in distribution as eelgrass, surfgrasses are ecologically important for stabilizing sediments in high-energy environments and providing habitat for invertebrates and juvenile fish (Thom et al. 2011).
Eelgrass is the most widespread seagrass in Washington. Native eelgrass (Zostera marina) establishes in shallow, protected waters with soft sediment and can be found in areas of the Puget Sound along with Pacific Coast estuaries, including Willapa Bay and Grays Harbor. In the Puget Sound, approximately half of eelgrass grows on tidal flats, while the other half grows within small beds along the shore (Christiaen et al. 2022). Eelgrass performs a variety of ecosystem services including water quality enhancement, ocean acidification mitigation, and algal bloom control (Dowty et al. 2010, Christiaen et al. 2022). Eelgrass meadows also serve as efficient carbon sinks due to their high primary productivity and low decomposition rates (Christiaen et al. 2022). In addition to the native Z. marina, there is a non-native species (Nanozostera japonica) of eelgrass in Washington. Non-native eelgrass generally occupies the high intertidal zone, while native eelgrass is typically in the high subtidal and low intertidal. Although they generally occupy different tidal elevations, the two species overlap in occurrence in some areas (Christiaen et al. 2020). N. japonica is commonly seen in the Central Puget Sound and Hood Canal (Christiaen et al. 2022). There is uncertainty regarding the interaction between native and non-native eelgrass, as some studies find establishment of non-native eelgrass can inhibit the establishment of Z. marina in restoration sites, while others find that the presence of N. japonica allows Z. marina to move further up the intertidal (DOE 2025). N. japonica is listed as a noxious weed by Washington due to its potential to negatively impact shellfish aquaculture.
While limitations in historic data records makes quantifying eelgrass losses challenging, monitoring efforts in the Puget Sound indicate that eelgrass area has declined over recent years, potentially due in part to marine heat waves in 2015-16 and 2019 (Christiaen et al. 2022). Interannual variability in eelgrass also creates monitoring challenges, and while there is evidence of stability over larger time frames across the Puget Sound, there is significant variability at smaller spatial scales. Sites with declines in eelgrass in the San Juan Islands and the Strait of Juan de Fuca outnumbered sites with increases in recent (2016-2020) monitoring efforts, with eelgrass located in embayments showing higher vulnerability (Christiaen et al. 2022). The Skagit delta has also shown a significant decline in eelgrass populations with sites showing a decrease of over 200 hectares between 2004 and 2020.
Localized declines are due to several stressors including marine heat waves, nutrient runoff, habitat conversion through dredging and filling, shading from overwater structures, and shoreline armoring (Dowty et al. 2010, Lambert et al. 2021, Christiaen et al. 2023). Given the need to support belowground tissues, seagrasses require more light when compared to other marine vegetation (Christiaen et al. 2022). As a result, stressors such as shading and algal blooms have a greater impact on them. Sea level rise also poses the threat of coastal squeeze (i.e., the inability of eelgrass habitat to migrate landward due to barriers like modified shorelines or coastal development) (Smith and 

## Shrubsteppe

Shrubsteppe is an arid to semi-arid ecosystem that typically comprises a combination of perennial grasses, forbs, or shrubs; one or all of these structural types may be at a given site depending on recent disturbance, soil characteristics and climate. Although an occasional tree may be present, typically the lack of moisture and/or frequency of fire prevents trees from becoming dominant. Key grasses include, but are not limited to, Idaho fescue, bluebunch wheatgrass, Sandberg bluegrass, Thurber's needlegrass, and needle-and-thread. Big sagebrush is the most abundant shrub, but other species such as antelope bitterbrush, three-tip sagebrush, stiff sagebrush, dwarf sagebrush, and rubber rabbitbrush can be dominant or co-dominant. Undisturbed habitat typically has shrub canopy cover between 5% and 30% cover (Johnson and O'Neil, 2001). Shrubsteppe often supports a diverse layer of forbs (i.e., broadleaf herbaceous flora) such as balsamroot, lupine, buckwheat, yarrow, and phlox. Shrubsteppe ecosystems also tend to have biological soil crusts (cyanobacteria, mosses, and/or lichens) depending on management and disturbance history. At disturbed sites, invasive non-native species such as Dalmatian toadflax, knapweed, cheatgrass may be abundant, while other non-natives like intermediate wheatgrass, and crested wheatgrass may be prevalent after previous seedings aimed at improving grass density. Soil crusts benefits habitat by locking in soil moisture, reducing erosion, and by increasing the soil's nutrients and productivity (Belnap et al., 2001; Kaltenecker, 1997; St. Clair and Johansen). They also help prevent the establishment and spread of invasive plants such as cheatgrass (Belnap et al., 2001). Shrubsteppe disturbed especially by fire or grazing/browsing may lack perennial grass, forbs, or shrubs during periods of post-disturbance recovery.
The primary mechanisms maintaining the characteristics and composition of shrubsteppe are climate, soils, and historically, fire, as a natural disturbance. Precipitation in Washington's semi-arid shrubsteppe zone occurs mainly in late autumn and winter, with annual precipitation ranging from 6 inches in the lowest parts of the Columbia Plateau to 22 inches in higher elevations near the transition with forested zones; limited water in shrubsteppe constrains the growth of trees. Winters are relatively cold, and by late spring, rainfall diminishes rapidly, and summers are dry and hot, which makes the water-holding capacity of shrubsteppe soils a significant influence on native plant assemblages. The variation in this soil characteristic across the landscape contributes to the diversity of plant species and assemblages in shrubsteppe. Although lithosol soils hold moisture, it can be spotty making a refuge for forbs (and a diverse array of wildflowers) and stiff sage that use taproots to exploit the moisture-holding fissures. Sandier soils in areas with lower precipitation support dry-land grasses, such as Needle-and-thread and Indian ricegrass, while deeper soils with moderate precipitation support bluebunch wheatgrass and sagebrush. Finally, fire is a natural and important part of shrubsteppe ecosystems, historically playing a large role in creating a mosaic of stands of different sizes in various seral conditions (Remington et al., 2021, West 1999; Knick et al. 2005). Fire severity and frequency prior to European settlement varied among different plant associations and site characteristics and likely returned to sites at intervals of 10 to more than 200 years. This temporal and spatial variation helped maintain a patchy distribution of shrubs, both within locally and across landscapes.
Shrubsteppe extends throughout many western states and into south-central British Columbia. In Washington, it occurs throughout the Columbia Plateau and into the surrounding higher elevations regions (Johnson and O'Neil, 2001). Once covering more than 10 million acres in Eastern Washington, a significant portion of the shrubsteppe landscape has been lost or degraded. Private land ownership by colonists displaced indigenous communities and significantly altered the landscape through introduction livestock, development of an engineered irrigation network and then hydro-electric power production, and conversion to agricultural uses (Sleeter et al., 2012). Quantifying estimates of shrubsteppe loss is difficult as assessments of historical presence are likely inaccurate and estimation methodologies vary (M. Schroeder, personal communication, December 17, 2023). However, even during the mid-1990s, WDFW estimated that nearly 60% of the original shrubsteppe habitat in Washington had been converted to other landcover (Dobler et al., 1996). More recently, Miller et al. (2011), estimated that 76.3% of historical sagebrush distribution in Washington had been lost.
What little remains of our state's native shrubsteppe habitat is under threat due to increased human pressure, invasion of non-native annual grasses, a rapidly changing climate, and the unprecedented extent, frequency, and severity of wildland fire; these threats are interrelated and difficult to address independently. Most of the shrubsteppe habitat that occurred historically in deeper soils was lost to agricultural conversion in the 1900s, and Washington continues to see habitats converted to agricultural uses, including wheat, fruit, and wineries. Now most extant shrubsteppe encompasses areas of less productive shallow and rocky soils which influence habitat quality. Grazing practices also altered shrubsteppe throughout the American west, including Washington, and poorly managed grazing is considered a modern threat to shrubsteppe habitat (WSRRI 2024). Further, land use and development to accommodate growing human populations in eastern Washington, gravel mining, and renewable energy development (e.g. large industrial solar facilities) are resulting in significant losses and fragmentation of remaining habitat. This fragmentation and increased human pressure and presence has contributed to the increased presence and spread of invasive plants.
Non-native annual grasses such as bulbous bluegrass, medusahead, and cheatgrass are heavily detrimental to shrubsteppe habitat. Cheatgrass has invaded an estimated 31.5 million acres throughout the Intermountain West. Cheatgrass germinates early giving it a competitive advantage in access to soil moisture against bunchgrasses and other native species. It also dries out early in the season, providing fine fuels to carry fires across large areas and lengthening the fire season. Cheatgrass, where present, can dominate bare soil postfire, and can provide enough fuel for another fire within two years (Pilliod et al. 2017). The transition to non-native annuals has contributed to movement further away from pre-1800 fire return intervals, even in the last 25 years.
As the climate changes, eastern Washington is facing even hotter and drier summers than were historically typical, and the state, like others, is experiencing extreme weather events, prolonged droughts and heatwaves. These put more demand on moisture-limited systems, influencing over time, shifts in vegetation composition and abundance, while also increasing the likelihood that fire, which was a natural and primary driver in creating and maintaining shrubsteppe habitat variation, will have catastrophic impacts to habitat and wildlife. Current fire return intervals are roughly 10 years or less, especially in cheatgrass dominated areas and some shrubsteppe plants simply cannot persist at that frequency. For example, some slow-growing shrubs (e.g., Wyoming big sagebrush) do not easily reestablish post-fire (3, 78) and cannot develop critical structural characteristics important to dependent wildlife (e.g., shrub-nesting birds and big game, 62, 67). The average size of fire has increased; based on fire data from the Washington Department of Natural Resources, Downes (2021) reported that the number of fires that burned more than 5,000 acres, increased in all size categories from 2000-2010 to 2011-2020. Fires greater than 5,000 acres increased more than 200% in the 2011-2020 decade, and fires greater than 50,000 increased more than 650% (Downes 2021). And, fires are recurring in the same areas, spanning shrubsteppe types (Zerbe et. al. 2022). Although frequent and high intensity fire is detrimental, past and ongoing fire suppression to address human health and safety has also contributed to altering natural fire cycles by producing atypically dense shrub stands that can increase fire severity (e.g. extent, temperature, impact) when they burn. Such fires damage soils and seed stocks reducing or eliminating the potential for native species recovery without human-assisted restoration. Under Washington's Growth Management Act, all cities and counties are rquired to adopt critical areas regulations to help preserve the natural environment and maintain fish and wildlife habitat (among a number of objectives). Crticial areas include fish and wildlife habitat conservation areas. In support of local jurisdictions in Growth Management Act implementation, technical assistance, including updated data and planning tools and recommendations based on best available science, should be provided in updating and implementing plans and regulations for fish and wildlife habitat conservation area designation and protection. Local jurisidction capacity, including both staff and funding, should be inreased to better designated and protect fish and wildlife habitat conservation areas in their land use plans and regulations.

## Streams/Instream including Headwaters

More information will be added to this section for instream habitats, beyond headwaters Headwater streams, typically defined as first-order channels with no upstream tributaries, are critical to the functioning and integrity of freshwater ecosystems. These small streams may exhibit perennial, intermittent, or ephemeral flow regimes, depending on local hydrologic and climatic conditions, and are often sourced from conveyances, natural drainages, or springs. Despite their size, headwater streams play a disproportionately large role in supporting downstream water quality, regulating flow regimes, and sustaining ecological productivity.
These systems contribute essential nutrients to downstream waters and support diverse communities of fish, wildlife, and native vegetation. Their physical, chemical, and biological heterogeneity creates a range of microhabitats that promote species richness and ecological resilience. For aquatic organisms, particularly fish, amphibians, and reptiles, headwater streams offer clean, cold water often characterized by distinct geochemical signatures. These mineral compositions influence species-specific imprinting, guiding natal homing behavior crucial for reproduction. Alterations in water chemistry can disrupt larval development and orientation, with cascading effects on population viability. Moreover, impaired water quality can reduce macroinvertebrate abundance, limiting a foundational food source for many aquatic species.
Terrestrial wildlife also depends on headwater systems. These streams provide reliable freshwater, foraging opportunities, and seasonal refuge. Their riparian zones and adjacent floodplains offer important resources for migratory species, including habitat for pollination, grazing, browsing, and overwintering. In many cases, these areas serve as breeding and rearing sites due to the abundance and diversity of native vegetation and prey species.
Hydrologically, headwater streams account for the majority of stream miles in many watersheds and play an essential role in attenuating surface runoff, thereby reducing downstream flood risks. They initiate hyporheic exchange and drive nutrient cycling processes, particularly within intact riparian wetlands and floodplain complexes. These areas also contribute to climate regulation through carbon sequestration in densely vegetated zones. As foundational components of watershed function, headwater streams support interconnected food webs and sustain ecological diversity.
Instream habitats within these systems provide numerous biotic functions, including life cycle support for macroinvertebrates, fish production and rearing, and amphibian development. They form the basis of complex food webs that integrate aquatic and terrestrial species, linking energy and nutrient flow across ecosystem boundaries.
However, headwater streams are increasingly vulnerable to anthropogenic pressures. Land-use changes, including forest practices, agriculture at higher elevations, water withdrawals, and infrastructure development, disrupt stream connectivity and hydrologic function. Unregulated grazing, vegetation removal, and recreational impacts can degrade riparian conditions and reduce ecosystem resilience. Climate change further exacerbates these challenges by altering hydrologic patterns and increasing stress on sensitive species.
Protecting headwater streams is essential to maintaining ecological integrity, biodiversity, and water resources across broader landscapes. Conservation efforts should prioritize maintaining natural flow regimes, riparian buffers, and connectivity to safeguard these indispensable ecosystems.

## Westside Prairie

Westside prairie is an herbaceous, non-forested plant community that is a designated priority habitat type in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program (WDFW 2008). It is fire-adapted and historically maintained by indigenous peoples who intentionally burned prairie to prevent invading trees and shrubs, increase the growth of food plants such as camas, and improve habitat for game species (WDFW 2025). In Washington there are two types: 1) dry prairie, and 2) wet prairie. The more common dry prairie occurs on deep, gravelly, well-drained glacial soils in bottomlands, along valley margins, and in lower foothills. In parts of Puget Trough, prairie can sometimes be recognized by mounded topography.
Although most dry prairie in Washington occur in the Puget Lowlands, smaller patches of prairie are also found in rocky soils of the San Juan Islands and in the rain shadow of the Olympic Peninsula (Steelquist 2024).
Wet prairie in Puget Sound is usually found on glacial outwash soils in swales or low-gradient riparian areas, while in southwest Washington they lie mostly on clay-rich soils that are saturated to the surface during the early part of the growing season (WDFW 2008, 2015, 2025). Hydrology plays a central role in wet prairie function, influencing plant composition, phenology, and restoration outcomes (Krueger et al. 2014).
Westside prairie ecosystems extend from Oregon's Willamette Valley through Washington's Puget Sound region north into the Georgia Basin region of southern British Columbia (WDFW 2025). Prairie in Washington is mostly found in South Puget Sound but occur as far north as the San Juan Islands and as far south as Clark County (WDFW 2015).
Native bunchgrass, sedge, and forb vegetation typify westside prairie (WDFW 2008). Dry, upland prairie vegetation consists of low growing native grasses and forbs. The most common native grasses in dry prairies are Roemer's fescue (Festuca roemeri) and California oatgrass (Danthonia californica; Rocchio and Crawford 2015). Prairie is also populated by a rich mix of native wildflowers such as common camas (Camassia quamash), Golden Paintbrush (Castilleja levisecta), and lupine (Lupinus spp.). Mosses, lichens, and bare ground may also be found in the spaces between the grasses and cover of wildflowers (WDFW 2008). Wet prairie is dominated by a highly diverse community of grasses and sedges and to a lesser degree by forbs or shrubs (WDFW 2015).
Native westside prairies are among the most endangered ecosystems in the United States (Noss et al. 1995, Noland and Carver 2011). Present-day dry prairie in the Pacific Northwest covers less than 10 percent of its historic range, while wet prairie has been nearly extirpated from both the Willamette Valley and South Puget Sound (Chappell et al. 2001, Krueger et al. 2014, Rocchio and Crawford 2015).
The majority of extant westside prairie in Washington is heavily degraded (Rocchio and Crawford 2015). Key threats include habitat conversion for development and agriculture, fire suppression, and the spread of invasive species (Crawford and Hall 1998, WDFW 2015). Conifers and especially Douglas-fir (Pseudotsuga menziesii) have displaced prairie in the absence of low-intensity fire (WDFW 2015, Dunwiddie and Bakker 2011). Non-native annual and perennial grasses and Scotch broom (Cytisus scoparius) have also displaced native vegetation, forming dense monocultures, altering soil chemistry, and reducing light, moisture, and nutrient availability for native grasses and forbs (Chappell and Crawford 1998, Rocchio and Crawford 2015).
In wet prairie, hydrologic alteration, such as ditching, drainage, or adjacent upland development, lowers the water table and disrupts the seasonal saturation essential to native sedge and forb dominated communities (Krueger et al. 2014). These impacts are exacerbated by climate change that alter hydrological function, contributing to earlier seasonal drying and increased drought frequency.
Together, these threats lead to altered species composition and structure, loss of native biodiversity, and reduced ecological resilience.
Although westside prairie looks much different than it did historically due to the spread of invasive plants, it still has significant wildlife habitat value (WDFW 2025 Historically, frequent fires prevented the establishment of shrubs and trees (Rocchio and Crawford 2015; pp 201). The lack of fires as a result of fire suppression and the cessation of cultural burning on westside prairies over the last 100 years has allowed Douglas fir trees and shrubs to encroach, except on the driest sites. This encroachment can cause prairies and grasslands to become forests and shrublands. Reintroducing fire through controlled burning can help to set back succession and limit tree and shrub encroachment. 8. Nonnative species are prominent in westside prairie and generally increase after ground-disturbing activities (Rocchio and Crawford 2015; pp 201). The reintroduction of fire (as part of a comprehensive restoration plan) can be used to support the reduction or elimination of nonnative and invasive plant species from westside prairie (Nolan and Carver 2011; pp 55)
. 7.1 Fire & Fire Suppression 1.2 Ecosystem & Natural Process (Re)Creation
Historically, frequent fires prevented the establishment of shrubs and trees (Rocchio and Crawford 2015; pp 201). The lack of fires as a result of fire suppression and the cessation of cultural burning on westside prairies over the last 100 years has allowed Douglas fir trees to encroach, except on the driest sites. This encroachment can cause prairies and grasslands to become forests. Fire suppression has also led to increases in shrub cover and the transformation of some prairies to shrublands. Reintroducing fire through controlled burning can help to set back succession and limit tree and shrub encroachment (Rocchio and Crawford 2015; pp 201) 

## Direct Economic Incentives

At least a third of historic prairie assessed have been lost to development (Crawford and Hall 1998; pp 13) and development has since then continued to lead of losses and fragmentation of westside prairie habitat given most prairie habitat is on private lands (Altman, Evans-Peters, Kim, Maness, Stephens, and Taylor. 2017; pp 10). A strategy to address future losses is for local governments to offer economic incentives (e.g., tax breaks for habitat protection) to incentivize the protection of westside prairie habitat. 1.2 Commercial & Industrial Areas

At least a third of historic prairie assessed have been lost to development (Crawford and Hall 1998; pp 13) and development has since then continued to lead of losses and fragmentation of westside prairie habitat given most prairie habitat is on private lands (Altman, Evans-Peters, Kim, Maness, Stephens, and Taylor. 2017; pp 10). A strategy to address future losses will be to acquire and protect areas of prairie habitat. 1.2 Commercial & Industrial Areas

At least a third of historic prairie assessed have been lost to development (Crawford and Hall 1998; pp 13) and development has since then continued to lead of losses and Comment fragmentation of westside prairie habitat given most prairie habitat is on private lands (Altman, Evans-Peters, Kim, Maness, Stephens, and Taylor. 2017; pp 10). A strategy to address future losses will be to purchase development rights on important prairie habitat to remove the threat of the habitat being lost to development.

## Tourism & Recreation Areas

1.1 Site/Area Stewardship Some recreational activities (off-trail use, dogs, ORVs, horseback riding) may lead to soil compaction, vegetation loss, invasive spread, and wildlife disturbance (ex: TCB larval trampling, MPG tunnel collapse, OVS flushing, etc). Signage, seasonal closures, designated trails, and leash enforcement reduce these stresses. 12: Small Ecologically Limited Population Size

## Species Stewardship

Applies to TCB, OVS, MPG, and SHLA, which have isolated populations and vulnerable to site specific or stochastic events. Needed for targeted species protection (avoid bird nesting season for mowing, species surveys, TCB augmentation, translocation, etc). Limited human-created habitat surrogates for native habitats were considered for inclusion in this section. Many have a strong connection to particular ecoregion SGCN (e.g., shrubsteppe Conservation Reserve Program lands in the Columbia Basin that are important to pygmy rabbits and greater sagegrouse) and are included in the ecoregion, rather than in this section. Other types, such as wellestablished or 'legacy' constructed wetlands or artificial reefs are also important, but we lacked the level of information that could be useful for this plan to identify conservation actions tied to these features. We will continue to explore this topic for another revision.

 Colonial breeding sites and migratory bird stopovers are in part PHS.  HGCN Features Distribution: All of these features are widely distributed. Ecoregion relationships for these features are built into, but not limited to the SGCN ecoregion relationships. For example, snags are a Local and Special HGCN and important for some songbirds, like SGCN Slender-billed Whitebreasted Nuthatch; this nuthatch is associated with Puget Lowland ecoregion. So, snags are important for that species in that ecoregion. Snags also occur in all ecoregions and are important for many species, beyond SGCN such as all cavity-nesting ducks, woodpeckers, tree swallows, and chickadees. HGCN Features are best identified during project-planning by experts familiar with those species and structures.

## HGCN Features Habitat Associations.

All of these features may be part of many NVC Groups. In many ways, these features are important because of the functions they provide in many types of habitats, rather than the vegetation community or broader habitats they represent.
The following sections describe the SWAP Local and Special HGCN that are not DNR NVC Associations. Conservation actions for these features are included generally here, and more specifically associated with SGCN need and actions in Appendix D7. ## Bat Foraging Areas, Roosts, Maternity Colonies, and Hibernacula

Bat hibernacula, roosting and maternity colonies are considered vulnerable concentrations. Bats group together in a variety of habitats for thermoregulation, safety, sociality, and to reduce energy expenditures and are often most vulnerable while in these colonies (Kunz 1982, Fenton 1997). Colony sizes can vary depending on the species, with some forming colonies in the hundreds to thousands while others in dozens, regardless of size forming colonies is an important part of bat life history and protecting that habitat is critical to species survivorship and population resiliency (Kunz 1982).
Many bat species in Washington hibernate during the winter to survive when there are limited resources (Weller et al. 2018). Bats are vulnerable during this time, when disturbed, bats will burn critical fat reserves that are needed to survive through the winter, which can lead to poor health and even mortality. Bats will use a variety of habitats for hibernation selecting based on microclimate needs, distance to water and foraging habitat as well as protection from wind, weather and predators (Weller et al. 2018). Examples of important habitat for hibernation in Washington include cliffs, talus, caves, snags, abandoned mines, human-made structures, and root wads in old growth/mature forests (underlined habitats are also stand-alone Feature HGCN and benefit other species).
During the summer females group together to form maternity colonies where they give birth and rear their young called pups (Kunz 1982). Most species in Washington give birth to a single pup each year, due to their low fecundity, any loss of young each year can be detrimental to the recovery and resiliency of a population. It is energetically demanding for the females during pregnancy and pup rearing as is it for pups to grow quickly before winter (Kunz 1982). Therefore, bats will select roost conditions that decrease that energetic cost. This includes roost that are warm, protected and within proximity to water and foraging habitat. Bats use a variety of habitats for their maternity colonies in Washington, such as cliffs, talus, caves, snags, bridges, bat boxes, buildings, suitable trees in old growth/mature forests, aspen stands, oak woodlands, and other forest types.
Bats will aggregate outside of hibernation and maternity season, in transitional, swarming, and night roosts. Though reproduction and hibernation are not occurring at these roosts, they are vital to sustain healthy bat populations. Bats need safe and protected places to roost at night while they rest between bouts of foraging or to consume insects (Barclay 1982). Without this habitat their foraging success can decrease leading to poorer health and even mortality. Before and after hibernation bats need to eat a lot to replenish their fat reserves to prepare for hibernation (fall) or pregnancy (spring) or migrate to and from Washington. They will seek productive foraging areas and will need transitional day roosts that are safe, protected and offer a microclimate to reduce energetic costs between foraging and roosting (Anthony et al. 1981, Barclay 1982). Without that habitat, it compromises their ability for successful reproduction or survivorship. Swarming roosts are where bats mate and socialize, having habitat with appropriate structure for this is vital for reproduction success (Neubaum and Siemers 2021). All of Washington's bat species use transitional, swarming, and night roosts in a variety of habitats in Washington, such as, cliffs, caves, snags, bridges, bat boxes, buildings, and suitable trees in old growth/mature forests, aspen stands, oak woodlands, and other forest types.
Bats play a critical role in Washington's ecosystems, they are the primary nocturnal predator of insects, many of which are pests in agriculture and forestry. They save billions of dollars in the US in pesticide costs and crop damage (Boyles et al. 2011). Given this it's critical to protect bats and their habitat. 

## Issues

## Caves

Caves are a priority habitat in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program (WDFW 2008). They are defined in PHS as "naturally occurring cavity, recess, void, or system of interconnected passages (including associated dendritic tubes, cracks, and fissures) which occurs under the earth in soils, rock, ice, or other geological formations, and is large enough to contain a human. Mine shafts (a human-made excavation in the earth usually used to extract minerals) may mimic caves and abandoned mine shafts with actual or suspected occurrences of priority species should be treated in a manner similar to caves."
Caves generally provide a stable environment (Benedict 1979, Holsinger 1988) with an overall climate less variable than at the surface (Poulson and White 1969). Within caves are areas and passages that exhibit different conditions with regard to light and climate. These areas are termed the entrance, twilight, and dark zones. Caves generally contain little or no vegetation inside (Culver 1982). Cave vegetation is limited to the entrance and, to a lesser extent, the twilight zones (Senger and Crawford 1984). Cave system dark zones contain no primary producing plants (Vandel 1965, Culver 1982), although chemosynthetic autotrophs may be present (Barr 1967).
Bats are strongly associated with caves, and cave-dwelling bats are indeed critical components of cave ecosystems (Baker et al. 2015). Bats are also extremely important contributors to terrestrial ecosystems. All bats utilize terrestrial environments for foraging and hunting, though only a portion of bat species occupies caves (Baker et al. 2015). Other species of concern that are also commonly associated with caves for all or a portion of their life include American pika (Ochotona princeps), amphibians (salamanders, frogs), bushy-tailed woodrats (Neotoma cinerea), and numerous invertebrates (troglobites and troglophiles; Peck 1998, Howarth 2009).
Threats to cave systems and the animals which inhabit them are most often a result of human activities (Baker et al. 2015). This includes disturbance to caves from human intrusions into these fragile ecosystems, such as recreation, vandalism, and illegal garbage disposal. Modification of entrances (such as digging out the opening to make it broader, removing vegetation around the mouth of the cave, diverting water flow) and the ground directly above caves (such as paving or creating impervious cover, logging or clearing, fire, quarrying) can impact the function of habitat provided by cave ecosystems (Baker et al. 2015) not only physically but chemically. These create disturbances from natural and artificial light intrusion, pollutant inputs, change in water quantity and quality of flow or infiltration, and may increase human traffic. NVC Group(s) and/or GET Functional Group(s) Aerobic Caves (S1.1)
Human-created or -modified groups that can function as this habitat: Anthropogenic Subterranean Voids (S2.1)

## Site Infrastructure

Threats to caves and the animals which inhabit them are invariably a result of human activities (Baker, Taylor, Thomas, Lavoie, Olson, Barton, et al. 2015; pp 3). These intrusions occur through recreation, vandalism, and illegal garbage disposal. Modification of entrances and natural and artificial light intrusion also impact cave habitat function. ## Cliffs

Cliffs are barren or sparsely vegetated ecosystems found on steep rock faces. Dominant parent materials include basalt in eastern Washington (particularly in the Columbia Basin) and various igneous, sedimentary, and metamorphic bedrock types of rock in the montane regions of western Washington (Rocchio and Crawford 2015). Cliffs often occur near other sparsely vegetated rock features including unstable scree and talus slopes. Species richness of cliff plant communities appeared to be controlled by aspect, microsite size, and cliff surface roughness (Graham and Knight 2004). Diversity increases when cliff microhabitats are compressed into a small area. Unfractured cliffs with no rooting space for vascular plants is habitat for lichens which is often next to a ledge where accumulated organic matter, minerals and water support grasses, sedges or small trees (Larson et al. 2000). Cliffs, in general, support high endemism of plants and can be refugia for old trees (Larson et al. 2000). This ecosystem usually occurs in inaccessible locations and thus is protected from most disturbances resulting from human activities (Rocchio and Crawford 2015). However, there is evidence of links between rock climbing and disturbance to cliff-dwelling wildlife (Knight and Cole 1995). Cliffs are a designated priority habitat type in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program (WDFW 2008). In PHS, priority cliffs are defined as being "Greater than 25 feet high and occurring below 5000 feet above sea level." NVC Group(s) and/or GET Functional Group 

## Outreach & Communications

There is evidence of links between rock climbing and disturbance to cliff-dwelling wildlife (Knight and Cole 1995; pp 56-57). Strategy to address this problem should include outreach via tools such as signage and outreach to local organizations to get the word out about cliff habitat where sensitive species are known to occur.

## Colonial Breeding Sites

Colonial breeding sites for waterbirds in Washington are found across a diverse range of habitats on isolated coastal and inland landscapes. These include offshore rocks, sea stacks, offshore rocky and vegetated islands, sandy spits, natural and artificially created islands in large bodies of water such as rivers, lakes, and coastal bays, steep coastal cliffs, and even human-made structures like rooftops, docks, or pilings. Structural features vary depending on species: cliff-nesting seabirds require vertical relief and ledges, while ground-nesting species prefer open or gently sloped areas with sparse vegetation or suitable burrowing substrates (WDFW 2015). Tree-nesting colonial species prefer particular species of trees and percentage of overall vegetative cover (WDFW 2015). Vegetation composition reflects habitat type and typically includes native coastal grasses, forbs, and low shrubs. Freshwater sites typically include emergent vegetation such as cattails, bulrushes, sedges, and willow shrubs, while saltwater marshes commonly support salt-tolerant plants (WDFW 2015). Some colonial breeding bird species nest on exposed gravel or shell substrates with little to no vegetation. Invasive species such as European and American beachgrass (Ammophila sp) and Phragmites australis have increasingly altered habitat structure in some areas, impacting habitat suitability (WDFW 2015). Critical to these sites are their isolation, correct nesting substrates, and the right balance of vegetation cover or openness tailored to each species' nesting requirements.
The primary ecological processes that maintain these habitats include minimal disturbance from predators and humans, intact natural hydrologic and geomorphic dynamics, and access to adjacent marine and freshwater prey resources. Natural hydrologic regimes-seasonal flooding, tidal inundation, and water level fluctuations-help maintain habitat structure by limiting excessive plant encroachment, cycling nutrients, and supporting dynamic vegetation mosaics (Sheldon et al. 2005, WDFW 2015). Physical forces such as wind, wave action, erosion, natural and artificial substrate deposition, and sediment transport continually shape nesting substrates and preserve the openness or structural complexity required by different species (Sheldon et al. 2005, WDFW 2015). Vegetation dynamics are influenced by salt spray, seasonal disturbances, herbivory, and nesting activities, including soil turnover by burrow-nesting birds. Predation pressure is minimized through site isolation-often on islands or other inaccessible locations-and seasonal vegetation growth that provides cover during nesting. The productivity of surrounding marine and freshwater ecosystems is critical for reproductive success, as it sustains forage fish and invertebrate prey populations. Where these natural processes have been altered or degraded, human interventions-such as invasive species removal, nest substrate restoration, and seasonal access restrictions-are often necessary to maintain or restore the ecological functions essential for successful breeding.
Colonial breeding sites are broadly distributed across Washington's marine and freshwater environments. Saltwater colonies occur along the outer Pacific Coast, including the Columbia River estuary, Puget Sound, the Strait of Juan de Fuca, and numerous coastal bays and islands (Duff et al. 2014). Notable marine colony sites include Protection Island, Smith and Minor Islands, Destruction Island, Tatoosh Island, and various rocky islets and sandy spits within the San Juan Islands and Willapa Bay (Hansen et al 2019). Freshwater colonies are found in trees adjacent to water bodies of all sizes, on islands in large lakes and reservoirs, river systems including the Columbia, Spokane, and Skagit Rivers, and occasionally inland marsh complexes (WDFW 2015). Other important freshwater and estuarine sites include the barrier islands of the Columbia River estuary, Nisqually Delta, and Skagit Bay islands (Roby et al. 2021). Many of these sites are within state or federal protected areas, although the degree of active management and monitoring varies.
Colonial breeding sites in Washington hold exceptional conservation value due to their ecological specificity and the disproportionate number of priority species that depend on them. This HGCN type is broadly associated with wetlands, marine waters, shorelines, floodplains, sand bars, spits, and riparian systems. These habitats support some of the largest and most ecologically significant aggregations of diverse waterbird species, many of which rely on secure, undisturbed locations for successful reproduction. Vulnerable aggregations that depend on these habitats include breeding concentrations of cormorants, storm-petrels, terns, and alcids in western Washington, and grebes, cormorants, terns, as well as semicolonial stilts, phalaropes, and avocets in eastern Washington (WDFW 2008). By concentrating nesting efforts in colonies, these sites support critical social behaviors that enhance reproductive success and species survival. They also serve as key indicators of the health and integrity of Washington's aquatic ecosystems, contributing to biodiversity, nutrient cycling between land and sea, and the resilience of coastal food webs. However, the overall extent and functional quality of these sites have declined significantly from historic conditions. Several species have experienced colony abandonment or contraction due to cumulative stressors. While targeted conservation actions have improved conditions at some priority sites, most colonies remain vulnerable to ongoing and emerging threats. Given their limited distribution and high sensitivity, sustained protection, proactive management, and habitat restoration are essential to maintaining Washington's colonial breeding sites and achieving long-term conservation goals. SGCN -vulnerable concentrations: Breeding colonies -herons; Eastern Washington breeding -Grebes and Cormorants; Eastern Washington breeding -Terns; Western Washington breeding -Cormorants, Storm-petrels, Terns, and Alcids; Western Washington -breeding waterfowl; Eastern Washington breeding phalaropes, stilts, and avocets (this last group is usually a loose congregation more than a "colony" but the concentration falls easily within the strategic habitat purpose) Additional Resource: Beneficial reuse of dredged material for habitat creation role_of_the_federal_standard_in_the_beneficial_use_of_dredged_material.pdf

## Migratory Bird Stopovers

This feature habitat is directly connected to and described in the Statewide/Transboundary Conservation Need: Migratory Bird Conservation. This important feature is associated wtih many, many types; the location, accessibility, patch size (depending on the species needing to stopover), forage availability, and limited disturbance during use are key factors in determining whether a feature is useable during migration. Urban forest patches, wetlands of all types, riparian corridors, old growth and mature forest, shorelines, and marine waters are all key migratory bird stopover features. This feature is highlighted here for connection as an HGCN, but the Statewide section is the deeper dive. 

## Old Growth and Mature Forest

Old-growth and mature forests are designated priority habitat types in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program (WDFW 2008). They play a crucial role in biodiversity, supporting various species of plants and wildlife, including rare and imperiled species like the northern spotted owl and marbled murrelet, which relies on these closed canopy forest habitats for survival. Old-growth and mature forest conditions vary in detail among essentially all forest types in terms of their exact physical and biological attributes, which is why type-specific definitions are necessary to accurately characterize old-growth and mature forest for each type of forest in Washington (Franklin et al. 2005). Old-growth and mature forest conditions also differ profoundly between moist westside forests, which are characterized by highly infrequent, stand replacement events, and dry eastside forests, which historically were characterized by frequent low-severity fire events (Franklin et al. 2005).
Old-growth forests are dynamic systems distinguished by old trees and related structural attributes (Barndt et al. 2023). Old-growth encompasses the later stages of stand development that typically differ from earlier stages in a variety of characteristics, which may include tree size
foot_32
 , accumulations of large dead woody material, number of canopy layers, species composition, and ecosystem function (USDA Forest Service 1989). In addition to their ecological attributes, old-growth forests are distinguished by their ecosystem services and social, cultural, and economic values (Barndt et al. 2023).
Mature forests are delineated ecologically as the stage of forest development immediately before old growth (Barndt et al. 2023). Mature forests exhibit structural characteristics that are lacking in earlier stages of forest development and may contain some but not all the structural attributes in old-growth forests. The mature stage of stand development generally begins when a forest stand moves beyond self-thinning, starts to diversify in height and structure, and/or the understory begins to reinitiate. Structural characteristics that mark the transition from an immature to mature forest are unique to each forest type; they may include but are not limited to abundance of large trees, large tree stem diameter, stem diameter diversity, horizontal canopy openings or patchiness, aboveground biomass accumulation, stand height, presence of standing and/or downed boles, vertical canopy layers, or a combination of these attributes (Barndt et al. 2023).
Since the time of non-indigenous settlement, approximately 72% of the original old-growth forest has been lost in the Pacific Northwest, largely through logging and other developments (Stritthold et al. 2006). The greatest present-day threats to the existence and ecological function of old-growth and mature forest come from fire and fire suppression as well as from climate change and disease. There is an increasing body of research documenting the effects of prolonged fire exclusion in forest types that are characteristically maintained by frequent disturbance regimes (Hanberry et al. 2020, Reilly et al. 2021, Woodbridge et al. 2022, Brodie et al. 2023). Fire suppression and exclusion alters disturbance processes that eventually result in adverse outcomes, such as uncharacteristically severe fires and reduced resistance to insect pests, drought, and other environmental stressors. Climate change is a complex stressor, that can also interact with and exacerbate other stressors or threats (e.g., fires, insects; USDA Forest Service 2023).

## Pollinator Habitats

Pollinators are essential to our environment. The ecological service they provide is necessary for the reproduction of over 80% of the world's flowering plants (Ollerton et al. 2011, Winfree et al. 2011, Arnett 2014). While many animals act as pollinators, insects such as butterflies, moths, bees, flies, wasps, beetles and more do the bulk of the work (WDFW 2025). While habitat needs can vary for different species, all pollinators need flowers, shelter, nesting areas, and protection from pesticides (WDFW 2025).
Areas supporting a diverse community of flowering plants provide an essential resource to foraging pollinators. Habitat containing assemblages of flowering plants that are continuously blooming from early spring through fall is ideal to support pollinator species diversity (Sardiñas et al. 2018). These types of plant assemblages are necessary to provide foraging resources throughout a pollinator's adult life cycle (Kremen et al. 2002). Extended periods during the growing season when little or nothing is in bloom limit the potential of habitat to support pollinators (Sardiñas et al. 2018). In addition, plants with varying flower sizes, shapes and colors, as well as varying plant heights and growth habits, support a greater number and diversity of pollinators (USDA 2025). This is important because pollinator species have morphological features specific to foraging on certain flowering species (USFS 2025). Some more specialized pollinator species have co-evolved with specific plants and thus require certain plants for foraging and reproduction. For example, monarch butterfly (Danaus plexippus) has coevolved with milkweed (Asclepias spp.) and rely on this group of plants to complete their life cycle.
Insect pollinators also require suitable shelter for nesting and overwintering. Most bees and wasps create small nests beneath the soil or within dead plant stems or cavities in wood (Jordan et al. 2020).
Other beneficial insects such as butterflies, wasps, moths, and ground beetles seek shelter in places that offer protection from predators and the elements, such as in leaf litter and brush piles. These features provide the most effective pollinator habitat when they are within the species' flight range of nearby foraging resources (Rands and Whitney 2011, Xerces 2018).
In many places pollinators are at risk from habitat loss. (Xerces Society 2025). Land-use activities are a key factor causing pollinator declines. These declines are indirectly mediated by reductions in floral resource abundance and diversity, which affects pollinator health and foraging (Birkenbach et al. 2024). Pesticide use and introduced diseases also pose a threat to pollinators. Pesticides contaminate pollinator habitat at levels that can harm native bees and butterflies (Gilburn et al. 2015, David et al. 2016). Commercial bees can also transmit pathogens to wild bees when sharing the same flowers (Nanetti et al. 2021, Colla et al. 2006).

## Direct Economic Incentives

Provide economic incentives to managers of working lands for maintaining important pollinator habitat features and for enhancing lands to provide better habitat for pollinators.

## Livestock Farming & Ranching 1.1 Site/Area Stewardship

Simplifying habitat and removing floral plant diversity has been shown to influence the diversity of pollinators including wild bees on a landscape scale (Cavigliasso, Phifer, Knowltonb, Licatad, Flaspohler, Webster, and Chacoff 2022). Stewardship of lands for wild pollinators should focus on retaining diverse native flowering plants and/or enhancing simplified landscapes with diverse mixes of native flowering plants. The Xerces Society Pollinator Habitat Assessment Guidel for Natural Areas and Ranglands (Xerces Society 2014) should also be consulted for guidance as that provides good guidance for management of these lands for native pollinators.
