## Snags and Logs

Dead wood in the form of snags and downed logs contributes to biological richness as substrate, cavity and foraging sites, and shelter or cover (Rose et al. 2001, Bunnell et al. 2002). More than 100 species of birds, mammals, reptiles, and amphibians are known to requires snags for nesting, roosting, shelter, denning, and feeding (WDFW 2011). Downed logs serve many of the same functions for wildlife (Bottroff 2009). These features are also significant habitat inputs for streams and rivers when they create structure and cover for fish and instream invertebrates; as they decay, they provide food for instream invertebrates and add nutrients to aquatic systems. Tree species, the stage of decay, the distribution of other snags in the area (clumped or scattered), and the successional stage of the plant community where the snag occurs are important factors in providing habitat function to wildlife (Idaho Department of Lands 2009). The height and diameter of a snag or log are arguably the most important factors to their function as wildlife habitat. In general, the bigger the snags and logs the more function they will provide (WDFW 2025).
Snags and logs are a designated priority habitat type in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species (PHS) program (WDFW 2008). In PHS, priority snags have a minimum diameter at breast height of 20 inches in western Washington and 12 inches in eastern Washington and are at least 6.5 feet in height. Priority logs are at least 12 inches in diameter at the largest end, and no less than 20 feet long. Priority snag and log habitat in PHS includes individual snags and/or logs, or groups of snags and/or logs of exceptional value to wildlife due to their scarcity or location in a particular landscape. Areas with abundant, welldistributed snags and logs are also considered priority snag and log habitat in PHS (WDFW 2008).
Factors that may contribute to the decline of important snags and logs include intensive forest management, fire suppression, and other factors that influence forest sustainability and age-class diversity. Intensive forest management has been shown to simplify stand and landscape structure (Rose et al. 2001). Only two rotations of managed Douglas-fir have been estimated to reduce the abundance of dead wood by 90%, compared to levels in natural old-growth systems. Fire suppression has also been demonstrated to increase the density of smaller trees, which in turn decreases abundance of larger diameter trees. This results in lower recruitment of large snags and downed wood. Complex linkages between temperature, carbon dioxide, plant growth, decomposition, and nutrient cycling influenced by climate change could also affect large wood recruitment by altering the size and relative distributions of carbon and nutrient pools in forest soils and vegetation (Rose et al. 2001).

## Logging & Wood Harvesting

1.1 Site/Area Stewardship Intensive forest management has been shown to simplify stand and landscape structure, which in turn decreases abundance of larger diameter trees (Rose CL, Marcot BG, Mellen TK et al. 2001; pp 594). This results in lower recruitment of large snags and downed wood (Rose CL, Marcot BG, Mellen TK et al. 2001; pp 594). Forest health treatments, changes in forest harvest rotations, and leaving snags and logs as well as larger trees for recruitment as snags and logs should be implemented on managed forestlands.

## Talus

Talus in Washington is represented by homogenous areas of rock rubble ranging in average size 0.15 -2.0 m (0.5 -6.5 ft), composed of basalt, andesite, and/or sedimentary rock, including riprap slides and mine tailings. These areas may also be associated with cliffs.
Talus slopes are a landform comprised of talus (or "scree") which accumulates over extended periods below rockwalls which were formed by glaciation or erosion (e.g., from the Missoula Floods). A talus slope's size and shape reflect the shape, size, and composition of the source material and stage of development. Commonly, the size of debris increases further downslope due to fall-sorting, which occurs when smaller debris is trapped between larger particles and coarser debris -with its greater momentum --travels further downslope. Further sorting can occur over time due to surface flow of the talus deposits (Curry 2025). Talus slopes can significantly affect groundwater flow and storage. In one small (0.5 ac) alpine meadow in the Canadian Rockies, a talus slope was the primary groundwater reservoir, suppling 75% of streamflow during storms and the winter base-flow period (McClymont et al., 2010).
Plant colonization or recolonization of talus slopes is highly dependent on temperature and moisture regimes. Despite differences in plant succession based on aspect (Daubenmire and Slipp 1943), succession typically begins with moss (especially in wetter, north faces) and lichens, pioneering herbaceous species common to the ecoregion (e.g., kinnikinnic, phlox, lupine), shrubs (e.g., thimbleberry, Oregon grape, Nootka rose), and trees (e.g., vine maple, red alder, Douglas fir, and western redcedar). Vegetation stabilizes talus slopes and provides habitat for various wildlife species.
The primary deposition mechanism of talus is rockfall, caused by freeze-thaw cycles, erosion (water and wind), biogeochemical weathering, and thermal expansion-contraction. Some talus slopes receive significant debris from avalanches and debris flows which can be triggered by earthquakes typically larger than magnitude 4.0 (Curry 2025). Secondary rockfalls can be triggered by human or animal activity. Rates of rockwall retreat, while highly variable, are highest immediately post-glaciation and diminish with time (Ballantyne, 2002). Talus deposits are often long-lasting landscape features with older features having negligible rates of current accumulation. Talus slopes are commonly preserved as inactive formations under established vegetation. Because of the dynamic nature of many talus slopes, vegetative communities are subject to disturbances (e.g., debris flows) which can locally reset plant colonization.
Talus slopes are a common landform found in every county in Washington. Wherever cliffs or rockwalls exist, talus slopes are likely to exist as well. High-elevation talus slopes in the Cascades and Olympics are associated with glaciers and permafrost; such talus slopes are more susceptible to increased slope instability from climate change (Curry 2025). Given talus slopes' inherent instability and hazards, direct anthropogenic changes to talus slopes are not widespread. It is likely that the current extent and quality of talus slopes is similar to historic conditions. At a site-scale, where talus slopes have experienced degradation, it is typically due to mining or human-induced landslides associated with road construction, utility construction, or logging. In some locations talus slopes have been degraded by recreational impacts (e.g., trampled plants, increased erosion). Unseasonably warm and extreme warm temperatures will likely affect talus slopes by increasing rates of rockfall due to changes in freeze-thaw cycles, more intense rainfall events, and increased thermal expansion, and may affect the relationship talus has to groundwater resources. Changing fire regimes may also lead to increased landslide frequency in steeper terrain. 

## The topics in this section

• require statewide or transboundary policy, legislative action, budget commitment, legal framework, or formal guidelines to effectively address a high priority issue; and/or
• High priority conservation actions must be coordinated and sequenced across multiple regions to be effective (i.e., without that broader collaboration, significant gaps in or poor sequencing of the work reduces the effectiveness overall).
There is often overlap in and relationship among the issues in this section. Progress made in one arena often has positive benefits to another. For instance, conservation progress in land use planning and riparian protection and management could benefit connectivity, water quality and quantity for fish and wildlife, salmon recovery, forest health, and First Foods sustainability.
Most of these transboundary conservation needs are essential to support ecosystem function and species conservation, and the success of many of the recommendations in this section address habitat protection, restoration, and management. The effectiveness of the recommendations in this section are the direct or indirect result of human influence -where and how we choose to live, work, and play; the choices and tradeoffs we make, or can make, at various scales in natural resource economies (forestry, fishing, agriculture); how and when we use and manage natural resources; and how we respond to large environmental change like amplified risks of wildfire, drought, flooding, diseases, and invasive species.
This section provides descriptions, issues, and conservation recommendations for statewide and transboundary topics that fit the two criteria and description above. Similar, scaled-down actions may also be in relevant ecoregion sections. For instance, the statewide habitat connectivity issue and actions relate to the regional recommendations in the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan; in the Columbia Plateau ecoregion, the focus will be on the individual corridors (e.g. Lower Crab Creek), SGCN and HGCN benefitting from these connectivity actions.

## Habitat Connectivity

Habitat connectivity is a major conservation issue at all scales and this is perhaps one of the most important conservation issues we could address. Habitat fragmentation has long been identified as a significant threat to biodiversity, species persistence, and habitat resilience.
All statewide to species-level conservation issues need connectivity considerations, and three themes emerge: terrestrial, aquatic and riparian, and migratory bird stopovers and overwintering habitats.
There is overlap in this plan among these, and across other transboundary conservation needs and recommendations (e.g. riparian and floodplain, salmon recovery, land use planning). This section calls attention to connectivity as a focal area, regardless of habitat quality. We are quickly losing the opportunity to keep and make connections among biodiversity areas and cores, subpopulations throughout a population, and the transitions across elevations (from sea to mountains).
Statewide, habitat connectivity is a theme at many scales. Local jurisdictions are including it in planning guidance, Comprehensive Plans, and open space grants (e.g. Pierce County Conservation Futures 108 ). Washington Department of Transportation is addressing it with wildlife crossings and connectivity planning for human and wildlife safety (they are a co-author and major contributor to the WAHCAP) and in culvert replacement to improve fish passage, an essential connectivity issue for salmon recovery. Dam removal projects across the state are improving aquatic connectivity, not just for fish passage, but for aquatic system function, riparian restoration, and marine health (e.g., Elwha River and system restoration 109 and Glines Dam removal 110 ).

## Terrestrial Connectivity

In 2025, the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan was developed as a statewide terrestrial habitat connectivity prioritization framework (Figure 
12
, Habitat Support HGCN section 

## Aquatic and Riparian Connectivity

Connectivity of aquatic ecosystems is fundamental for all aquatic life. Freshwater mussels, one of the most imperiled species groups; aquatic invertebrates; salmonids and other rare freshwater fishes; lamprey; and migrating and resident cetaceans all must be able to move through their aquatic environments to find food, mate, and disperse through their whole life cycle. Other statewide sections in this plan go into more detail with specific conservation recommendations for riparian and floodplain connectivity, fish passage and salmon recovery, and water quality and quantity for fish and wildlife. We mention it here because aquatic connectivity quality -both physical and chemical -is foundational to so many other conservation efforts, and we encourage plan users to consider how their actions can benefit aquatic connectivity in all of these other areas of need.

## Protect and restore habitat connectivity for migratory birds

Washington is part of the Pacific Flyway and important for the western edge of the Mississippi Americas Flyway. Migratory birds of Washington connect the state to locations throughout the United States and the Western Hemisphere. 113 Conservation of Washington's migratory birds across their full annual cycle requires non-breeding and migratory stopover habitat conservation in addition to their breeding ground habitat. 
114
 HGCN such as Nearshore -Pacific Coast and Nearshore -Puget Sound (e.g., estuaries, bays, marine waters), Fresh Deepwater (e.g. Lakes), Instream (e.g. rivers), Freshwater Wetlands, Seagrass, kelp/kelp forest, old growth / mature forest, Oregon white oak woodlands, and other stopovers such as agricultural lands and alpine meadows provide essential resources including food, shelter, and resting areas needed to complete long-distance migrations. 
115
 Many species require specific habitat types during migration that offer high foraging efficiency and low disturbance, making stopover sites vital to survival and reproductive success. These habitats enable migrating birds to build energy reserves for physiologically intensive migrations to areas such as coastal Alaska. 
116
 On the Pacific and Puget Sound coasts, estuaries such as Willapa Bay, Grays Harbor, Padilla Bay, and the Skagit and Stillaguamish deltas provide expansive intertidal flats, eelgrass beds, and salt marshes that support large concentrations of shorebirds, waterfowl, and marine birds during migration and overwintering (Drut and Buchanan 2000).
Inland, wetland and floodplain systems in regions such as the Columbia Basin and Yakima Valley include seasonal marshes and inundated agricultural land that serve as key migratory stopovers for waterfowl (Drut and Buchanan 2000, Thomas et al. 2004, WDFW 2020). River corridors such as the Columbia, Skagit, and Snake Rivers also provide migration pathways and habitat through a combination of open water, sandbars, and riparian zones. High-elevation wetlands, subalpine lakes, and meadows in the Cascade and Olympic Mountains are also important stopovers for songbirds, shorebirds, and waterfowl migrating through mountainous flyways. The importance of water and water-supported habitats and riparian are common, crucial themes.
Thirteen neotropical migratory species that breed in Washington have been identified as focal migratory species across multiple groups and initiatives such as the Road to Recovery Tipping Points, USFWS

Apply the WAHCAP recommendations for implementation, evolving and improving the data, steppeddown / finer-scaled recommendations, and engagement/use.

## Riparian, Floodplain, and Wetland Ecosystems

Floodplains are lowlands adjacent to river systems that are subject to natural flooding. Soils, natural disturbance (flooding, rainfall runoff), hydrology (surface and groundwater), and human uses (development, agriculture) of floodplains influence the function, extent, and structure 120 (e.g. size and age classes of trees, presence or absence of a shrub or grasses layer) of riparian ecosystems. Riparian refers to the land located immediately adjacent to a body of water, such as a stream, river, lake, wetland, bay, or ocean: a crucial transitional zone where terrestrial (land) and aquatic (water) ecosystems meet and interact. This zone directly influences the water and the life within it, affecting available cover, shade, water temperature, instream nutrients and structure (such as from logs, leaf litter, sediment, rockfall), and the removal of pollution from the land before it reaches the water. The amount and type of vegetation in that zone affects how well the riparian ecosystem serves those functions. A more thorough description of scientific understanding regarding Washington's riparian ecosystems can be found in Volume floodplain soil health and bank stability, instream and adjacent-to-stream SGCN habitat, aquatic and terrestrial habitat connectivity 122 , and stream and river resilience with uncertain and new patterns of drought and flood. 123 Riparian conservation is a high priority in Washington 124 and the habitat type is prioritized in western states' SWAPs. Conservation needs include protecting, restoring, and managing the vegetation communities and the ways in which ground and surface water are available to support the system. 
125
 Riparian policy and science that influence voluntary measures and regulation are often debated across the Pacific Northwest. 
126
 A 2023 study explored data and information comparing the current status of riparian buffers in Washinton state on public and private lands (to the extent possible with accessible information) and acknowledged complex social and cultural structures that affect riparian best practices, conservation policies, and regulation. 
127
 This work, the Washington Riparian Task Force (2025), new Forest Practices rule to reduce warming in non-fish bearing perennial streams in Western Washington (2025) 128129 , Northwest Treaty Tribes' expressed values and concerns about the effectiveness of current conservation efforts 130 , and regionally-specific economic sectors' concerns (e.g., commercial forest managers 131 , agricultural producers 132 ) mean that any ecologically effective riparian conservation enhancements will be under great scrutiny and need to be science-supported, strategic, and contextsensitive.
We have identified many important riparian conservation outcomes in Washington state: maintain diverse and system-appropriate native vegetation (grasses, trees, and shrubs), deliver nutrients (e.g., leaf litter) to build bank soils and feed instream productivity, buffer streams from runoff pollutants, 
122
 Although WAHCAP is terrestrially focused, the report acknowledges the critical roles of riparian corridors at all spatial scales as crucial conservation features, particularly in fragmented or human-dominated landscapes, natural pathways for wildlife movement. 123 WDFW. 2025. 
https://wdfw.medium.com/a-new-way-of-looking-at-washingtons-waterways-ad05d872c4ce
 manage established invasives such as reed canary grass and Himalayan blackberry, protect soil moisture, foster cold water refugia and maintain normal stream temperatures, moderate floods and maintain stream health during drought, and provide naturally occurring mature and late-successional forest benefits such as large woody debris inputs to streams. Riparian management zones of sufficient width, native vegetation, and mixed structure are very beneficial. Riparian management zones that protect the entire area where riparian ecosystem functions occur support aquatic species, enhance habitat connectivity, and allow SGCN to forage and move to meet their needs. Narrow riparian zones along streams offer some benefits to stream habitats, although are more vulnerable to edge effects: open to predators, soil moisture loss, invasive species, and daily and seasonal temperature swings due to a higher ratio of edge-to-interior vegetation. For example, microclimate edge effects and limited substrate availability can hinder epiphyte growth and survival
foot_34
 which are important for Marbled Murrelets, meaning wider riparian buffers (especially those considered mature and old growth or buffering such stands) provide greater benefits to wildlife like nesting marbled murrelets.
Wetland loss and conservation. Freshwater wetlands are an HGCN and are directly related to many SGCN across the state. Wetlands protect and improve water quality, store floodwaters and slow or reduce flooding, provide aquifer recharge, are exceptional carbon sinks 
134
 , and serve extremely important habitat functions for fish and wildlife. In 2024, US Fish and Wildlife Service released the decadal national "Wetlands Status and Trends" 135 report stating that wetland loss has increased in the contiguous 48 states (excluding Hawaii and Alaska) by 50% since 2009 primarily to drainage and fill impacts, and most of the loss was in vegetated freshwater and saltwater wetlands. Remaining wetlands are being transformed from vegetated wetlands, like salt marsh and swamp, to non-vegetated wetlands, like ponds, mudflats, and sand bars. Since the late 1700s to 1990, Washington lost about 31 percent of its wetland areas leaving approximately 938,000 acres of recognized wetlands in Washington. Since 2009, this loss has accelerated despite state regulation that is more protective than federal regulation. 
136
 Wetlands are one of the most productive and biodiverse habitats, with 40 percent of all plant and animal species living or breeding in wetlands. From the USFWS report: "Approximately half of all Endangered Species Act species in the United States are wetland dependent, and one key conservation action to prevent the need to list more species is to conserve wetlands. Wetlands provide stopover and wintering habitats for more than 4 billion birds from Canada as well as breeding habitats for nearly five billion migratory birds en route to the tropics. Wetlands also provide shelter and vital nursery habitat for many species of fish and are an important source of cultural resources for communities and many Native American Tribes." And, wetland carbon sequestration is very important. 
137
 Wetland science and regulation are often at the forefront land use debates as many wetlands may only be "wet" for part of the year. Even ephemeral wetlands (i.e., those that periodically dry out) have value to fish and wildlife that rely on those resources when they are seasonally or annually wet, and many species may use the damp or deeper wet areas in the soils and vegetation as cover and shelter during dry periods. Long-term adaptive conservation, management, and monitoring in wetland-related projects (losses, restoration, or creation) often do not include annual or regular wetlands change and loss data updates to spatial products in a consistent format that could better inform future project environmental review, restoration, response to changing weather patterns and refugia conservation, or ecological effectiveness of mitigation policies.

## Riparian, Floodplain, and Wetland Conservation Recommendations

Riparian conservation is in the spotlight. Interest, science, capacity, funding, and urgency are driving development of protection and restoration policies, programs, and techniques in Washington, including the Riparian Task Force and recommendations regarding an Ecological Improvement Framework.
• Fully implement legal requirements. Fully implemented, monitored, and enforced Washington Growth Management Act, Washington Shoreline Management Act, and Forest Practices Rules are essential to protect existing and functioning floodplain and riparian habitats. State agencies can support implementation with state-of-the practice information and technical guidance for local governments, land use planners, and land managers, and adapt that information and guidance based on management and monitoring outcomes (i.e., adaptive management). Provide state and local governments with adequate, dedicated funding for well-staffed compliance monitoring and enforcement of protections of existing riparian habitat.
• Build and maintain support and funding for strategic and effective riparian conservation outreach and voluntary conservation. The most effective voluntary riparian conservation solutions will be watershed-based and strategically implemented with strategies that resonate with local communities and regional audiences. To build and maintain support, it will be important to track implementation and progress toward watershed-framed objectives with a defined and transparent pathway for adaptive management actions. Riparian-focused incentive funding programs need consistent and stable (e.g. • Implement recommendations from the Riparian Task Force Report. These are "intended to work together … and cannot be effectively implemented as individual strategies." 147 While primarily focused on salmon and steelhead, many SGCN and HGCN dependent on riparian habitats and the supporting functions they provide to instream habitats would benefit from implementation of the recommendations in this report.
• Protect, connect, and restore floodplains. Floodplain function is essential to riparian habitat conservation. Strategic restoration, incentives (e.g., protection, voluntary acquisition), groundand surface-water quantity protection, wetland protection, and regulation to deliver floodplain protection and connectivity provide essential foundations for long-term durable riparian conservation as well as protections for human health and safety in water-adjacent communities.
Contribute fish and wildlife technical information, including focal areas from the Riparian Task Force and the Riparian Data Engine, for the Floodplains by Design partnership to find common ground in restoration and preservation priorities that benefit human communities, HGCN, SGCN and the habitats on which they depend.
• Use the best available information to prioritize riparian protection, connectivity, and restoration. Support data stewardship and delivery of the WDFW Riparian Data Engine 148 (maps, spatial data, searchable information, and how to guide) and the Statewide Fish Passage Barrier Assessment (prioritize barrier removal, improve aquatic connectivity) to optimize investments that improve riparian habitat condition and function and aid in salmon recovery. Meet the nextgeneration needs for the Riparian Data Engine: secure stable funding to maintain the tool with updated data; identify a reliable, compatible data source for channel migration zones; develop a method/tool to collect user feedback to improve the tool; explore ways to provide regionally • Wetland conservation. Produce a contemporary/updated Washington statewide freshwater and estuarine wetland mapping inventory 149 inclusive of spatially explicit information on wetland function and carbon sequestration, carbon-rich as-yet-unmapped forested wetlands 150 , and natural ephemeral/seasonal wetlands in eastern Washington, compatible with data structures that would feed a future update to the National Wetlands Inventory Geospatial Dataset. Update and align mapped WDFW Priority Habitats and Species for HGCN (freshwater wetlands, nearshore) and Washington Department of Ecology wetland spatial products. Apply the updated information in stages as available to existing wetland protections and regulations in Washington state and use this information in wetland-impacting projects to inform avoidance, minimization, and mitigation that achieves ecological improvement. Promote wetland conservation on private lands through technical assistance and incentives (e.g., Wetlands Reserve Easements) provided by WDFW biologists, land trusts, and conservation districts, with an emphasis on those places that improve sustainability for SGCN and vulnerable concentrations. Produce wetland outreach products that showcase why seasonally or annually dry wetlands still have a conservation value.

## Land Use Planning with Conservation Values

"Tremendous population growth and development, increasing land-use conflicts, and increasing lists of species and ecosystems that are of concern suggest that we are losing

## ground." -Washington Biodiversity Council 2007

How and where humans develop, convert, and use the natural landscape determines which species will and will not do well in Washington's future. The retention and restoration of habitat in the developing landscape provides ecological services important to humans and communities. 
151
 Through the Growth Management Act (GMA), cities and counties in Washington designate and protect five kinds of natural resources critical areas with certain principles: use the best available science, promote "no net loss" of ecosystem function and value, give special consideration to anadromous fishes (primarily salmon and steelhead), and support viable, protected populations through habitat and wildlife population connectivity. ) to review federal agency activities and the issuance of federal permits and licenses that may affect any land use, water use, or natural resources in the coastal zone. 
154
 Statelevel councils also operate at project to regional scales to set and deploy environmental standards for certain kinds of project development. For example, the Energy Facility Site Evaluation Council (EFSEC) provides a process to approve hundreds to thousands of acres of industrial solar and wind power installations, battery energy storage, biofuels, green hydrogen, nuclear power, thermal power, and transmission in Washington state. Whether land use policies and priorities consider the long-term alignment of human and wildlife benefits depends largely on the values and engagement of people and communities statewide.
Spatial data for city boundaries and the unincorporated Urban Growth Areas (UGAs) for 27 Washington counties that participate in GMA is available in the Washington Spatial Data portal. UGAs are updated in conjunction with the annual statewide parcel layer update (last update as of this plan writing was completed April 2025). These are areas where more urban and suburban development are anticipated, and where impacts that are directly related to that type of growth are expected to occur (see next section Enhance Voluntary Conservation for more context on rural and agricultural land effects).
Washington state's population is growing rapidly, overtopping 8 million people in 2024, and is expected to grow about 1% per year based on the annual forecast, updated each November. In Washington, the largest driver (82%) of population growth is net migration -people moving in minus people moving out. 155 , 156 Over 76% of state population growth in 2024 occurred in the five largest metropolitan counties, with Whitman, King, and Spokane growing the fastest. The top 10 cities for numeric change, in descending order, are Seattle, Tacoma, Bellevue, Vancouver, Redmond, Shoreline, Spokane, Spokane Valley, Auburn and Marysville. In a summary of the last five years, the top five counties with the greatest percent population increase (Figure 
13
) are usually considered largely rural, with low Population growth is not inherently bad for conservation, although natural resources sustainability requires managing growth in ways that consider human and conservation values. The ways and extents to which humans use resources, create or expand a development footprint, and generate pollution and waste can affect habitat suitability and sustainability for plants, fish, and wildlife. More people usually means more land and water use for residential development and commercial services, roadways, power and power lines, drinking water, and waste and pollutant management. New development that converts, isolates, and degrades natural habitats and working lands that are compatible with plant, fish and wildlife conservation takes a cumulative toll and introduces other issues: isolated fish and wildlife subpopulations that can collapse, more edges and avenues for invasive species, natural disruption (e.g. lighting at night, water availability decrease), competition and predation (e.g. feral pets), and increased traffic (animal death due to collisions, from amphibians to elk). Development project proponents have varying levels of engagement with local jurisdictions, state councils, and resources agencies for guidance scoping, planning, and designing development.
Existing development, even if compliant with permitting and practices of a time, can have unanticipated or cumulative negative effects as time passes and we learn more about our environment. For instance, armoring shoreline habitat on Puget Sound diminishes habitat access and suitability for many marine species, from harbor seals to forage fishes; in 2018, armoring was present along approximately 27 percent of Puget Sound's shoreline. 
159
 As sea level rises and the risks to shoreline properties increase, it is likely that there will be increased pressure to build new shoreline defenses or expand or raise existing armoring. 
160
 Even with integrated "no net loss" standards in environmental, development, and land use policy, Washington has struggled to not only keep pace with, but get ahead of, natural resources' degradation and species declines as we learn more about how our built environment and land use decisions affect natural resources over time. We need to apply the knowledge we have now to the conservation outcomes we want in the future by using land use practices that consider landscape-scale system connectivity, fish and wildlife needs from potentially affected lands and waters, and ecosystem processes and functions.
Understanding trends and human dimensions in regional population growth, transportation and other infrastructure, natural resources-dependent markets (e.g. livestock, agriculture, timber) to which Washington contributes, and future technologies (e.g. offshore wind, tidal energy, irrigation) will be key to next generation land use planning and regulation. Understanding these trends allows for strategic avoidance and minimization of, and compensation for environmental impacts from these development activities, at both small and large scales. There is an opportunity to increase the pace and scale of biodiversity conservation and provide benefits to people and animals by pairing the protection provided by avoidance, minimization, and compensation with additional restoration actions to recover already degraded habitats.

## Land Use Conservation Recommendations

"Strengthening the implementation of existing land use regulations is essential to maintain baseline environmental conditions. Once effective protections are in place and development impacts are fully offset, development could contribute to measurable ecological improvements."
foot_41
 PHS provides science-based recommendations stating, in general, that wildlife is best served by:
• Keeping large, connected patches of undeveloped native vegetation intact.
• Encouraging and maintaining low zoning densities within and immediately surrounding highvalue habitat areas and encouraging maintenance of native vegetation.
• Managing road systems to minimize the number of new roads and new barriers to important animal movement corridors.
• Planning open space to incorporate high-value habitat and corridors for animal movement.
• Zoning for higher densities within urban and developed landscapes to avoid sprawl.

## Designate, enhance, protect, and connect critical areas, habitat cores, and connectivity corridors.

Make it easier for local and regional land use planning jurisdictions (e.g. cities, counties, regional planning collaboratives) to incorporate into their comprehensive plans guidance to protective critical areas, habitat cores, and connectivity corridors. Such technical guidance resources include Priority Habitats and Species management guidelines; Riparian Data Engine 162 ; Shoreline Planners Toolbox and resilience guidance 163 ; WAHCAP 164 ; Land Use Planning for Salmon, Steelhead, and Trout 165 ; and HGCN conservation recommendations identified by the SWAP. Find opportunities to enhance conservation pace and scale by ensuring the avoidance, minimization, and compensation actions for critical areas, habitat cores, and connectivity corridors cumulatively reflect regional restoration and protection priorities.

166
 . For renewable energy development, particularly industrial solar and wind power and transmission, consistently inform site selection, scoping, design, avoidance, minimization and compensation for impacts to SGCN and HGCN during construction, operation, and maintenance. Encourage local jurisdictions and EFSEC to
• adopt and promote the Department of Ecology Programmatic Environmental Impact Statements (PEIS) tools for onshore wind, solar, and green hydrogen production 167 ;
• adopt and incorporate WDFW terrestrial Wind and Solar Guidelines (2025) 
168

• work with natural resources agencies' technical experts to interpret conservation-relevant data and information during site selection and project planning and ensure any compensatory mitigation contributes to watershed and regional conservation benefits.
strategy to achieving conservation outcomes. Two concepts work hand-in-hand to enhance effective voluntary conservation:
• raising awareness of conservation issues in targeted ways that inspire people to take action at home, in their own communities, and on their own lands; and
• purposeful and strategic facilitation and support of voluntary conservation action that directly benefits habitats and species by addressing the causes of their vulnerability or decline Beneficial conservation on private lands occurs through local relationship-building, outreach, and conservation incentive delivery that finds shared benefits with people's values and communities. Urban residents to rural ranchers and farmers to private forest managers to land trusts contribute to biodiversity conservation. The scales and conservation effects of these contributions are different. For instance, urban and suburban residents who support pollinator gardens, natural greenspace and parks, and their own Habitats at Home can model co-existing with wildlife, promote habitat connectivity and native habitats in their communities, and provide a bridge to wildland conservation topics with audiences that might not yet connect with natural resources agencies.
Compared to urban and suburban audiences, there are private residential areas in the interface between urban/suburban and rural, sometimes referred to as the exurban. These lands provide an opportunity to reach landowners with more acres than urban or suburban audiences (generally between 2 and 20 acres). Homeowners' Associations and communities encompassing these areas are avenues for coordinated outreach that could improve awareness and voluntary adoption of local and regional conservation recommendations, such as using less broad-application pesticide, appropriate septic system maintenance (related to ground and surface water quality), or converting lawn to native landscaping for pollinators and other wildlife to minimize the conversion effect at the edges of working lands and natural habitats.
Working lands such as privately owned farms, ranches, and forests that produce food, timber, and fiber, support rural economies, and provide wildlife habitat, clean air and water, and recreational opportunities can be compatible with, even critical for, local and landscape level biodiversity conservation. In 2020, approximately 32% of Washington's acres were private working lands. In 2024, Washington Department of Agriculture reported that agricultural lands (non-timber) totaled over 10 million acres. Washington's small forest landowners (SFLO) account for 15% of our state's forest acres.
Protecting these working lands and other larger blocks of private land from conversion to urban and residential development, restoring habitat and landscape linkages, and using sustainable grazing, agriculture, and forestry practices to support soil, water, air, and overall environment benefits are important conservation considerations.
There are several appraisals of natural resource conditions and conversion of non-federal land that report similar declining trends of working lands. . US Department of Agriculture Natural Resources Inventory reported in 2017 in Washington state that cropland, pastureland, rangeland, and Conservation Reserve Program land totaled approximately 14,354,900 acres -a little over one third of Washington's total acres. Based on Washington Department of Agriculture data, that acreage dropped about 4 million acres in seven years. In measuring loss of these rural lands over time, sources vary in whether they consider complete conversion only or also consider the impact of exurban land use. The American Farmland Trust Farms Under Threat resource finds that farmland loss (e.g., paved, fragmented, or converted to use that jeopardizes agriculture) and exurban large-lot subdivision for farmettes, ranchettes, and residences (i.e., not working lands) are major threats to farming and rangeland. In 2021, University of Washington reported some significant findings about Washington's Small Forest Landowners (SFLO) loss, conversion to residential uses, and potential conversion: in 2007, 71% of SFLO forest acres were in the forestry or natural land use classes, by 2019, SFLO forest acres in forestry or natural land uses declined by 121,500 acres (or 5.7%) while Residential increased by 48,600 acres (or 9%). Somewhere between 25,000 and 50,000 small forest landowners are likely anticipating selling all or some of their forest land by 2030.
This conversion of working lands to residential and commercial development is permanent loss in wildlife habitat potential. Those conversions also bring other issues such as lighting at night that disrupts nocturnal animal activities like bird migration, smaller parcel fragmentation that leads to greater variety in site management and requires more effort in conservation outreach, more fencing, different types and rates of application of pesticides and herbicides, feral pets that introduce different types of diseases and predation pressures, increases in managed corridors for infrastructure, and more traffic on existing roads that increase the potential for wildlife collisions. This type of working lands conversion also impacts the success and persistence of existing and remaining working lands. While working lands can bring their own challenges to conservation, they can also provide important resources for species habitat including connectivity, cover, and forage, and can be managed to buffer and reduce impacts that come from complete conversion of wild lands and waters. Washington rangelands are a significant source of realized and potential ecosystem services through strategies that enhance water quality and quantity, reduce noxious weeds and wildfire risk, and conserve and enhance native habitats (e.g. riparian, grasslands, meadows, and shrublands).
The potential for private lands to benefit conservation is significant and relies on willing landowners and strategically delivered incentives and technical guidance. Engaged private citizens and landowners who voluntarily support and apply good stewardship are essential to the future of conservation in Washington at many scales. Private landowners and volunteers contribute to conservation awareness and community support, invasive plant removal, habitat restoration and protection, and many other collaborative efforts. Importantly, helping working lands stay working is the first line of defense against permanent conversion. Conservation benefits are more likely to be durable when incentives are reliable enough to support a change in practices and demonstrate measurable conservation progress and effectiveness. Conservation incentive providers (e.g., private lands and habitat biologists, land trusts, conservation districts) that connect with working and private landowners directly are key to successfully finding opportunities for meaningful, strategic engagement.

## Voluntary Conservation Recommendations

Use urban and suburban education and outreach programs to promote outdoor appreciation, environmental stewardship, and behavior change related to HGCN and vulnerable aggregationspecific topics.
Programs like WDFW's Habitat at Home and lesson planning for K-12; Watchable Wildlife funding grants; "Leave No Trace"; ethical wildlife interactions (e.g., Orca boating distance rules, wildlife viewing); Working Lands for Wildlife; Conservation Districts' Conservation Resource Library and outreach programs under "Conserve WA"; WA DNR Youth Education and Outreach Program; and resources/connections provided through the Puget Sound Partnership "Social Approaches" are directly related to these objectives. Focus learning and awareness on statewide and multi-regional topics that can be related to lands and waters which people are most likely to experience or could recognize in their day-to-day living to spark curiosity and appreciation of contemporary conservation issues:
• HGCN (corridors, wetlands, riparian, migratory bird stopovers, nearshore, old growth/mature forest, shrubsteppe, instream habitats) and
• vulnerable aggregations (e.g. pollinators, breeding colonies of birds, forage fishes and food webs, marine mammal haul outs, roosting bats, shorebird concentrations).
Develop targeted conservation messages and materials for private landowner audiences in the following focal areas: Salish Sea and Pacific coastal; westside and eastside forests; and eastern Washington shrubsteppe to encourage site stewardship that
• reduces impervious cover and conversion to lawns;
• improves stewardship and diversity of natural habitats, including wildfire resilience while keeping native habitat values ("Defensible space" and FireWise Communities are frequently implemented concepts in exurban outreach. Adding wildlife conservation values to this curricula can encourage native landscaping.); and
• protects and restores riparian and wetlands, acknowledges the conservation values of HGCN, and reduces (or encourages label-prescribed awareness and use of) pesticide and herbicide application to reduce impacts on pollinators, bats, small mammals, and raptor SGCN.
Influence direct and measurable conservation progress in strategic focal areas for private working lands conservation, incentives, and technical guidance. Agricultural conservation organizations find that there is common ground in biodiversity conservation and working lands sustainability. Provide producers with options and incentives that enable them to deploy wildlife-friendly practices (e.g., rotational grazing, wildlife friendly fencing, riparian restoration and protection, sustainable forest management), avoid conversion to urban and suburban land uses, and restore habitats on low productivity ag lands to buffer natural habitats and restore connectivity.
Use the WAHCAP and the 2022 American Farmland Trust "connectivity and conservation" report, Washington Shrubsteppe Restoration and Resiliency Initiative 30-year strategy, Joint Venture wetland conservation objectives for working lands, Riparian Data Engine and riparian conservation objectives, and the distribution/concentration of HGCN and SGCN to identify strategic state-level objectives for Working Lands for Wildlife, technical guidance, and other incentives.
Use incentives to engage landowners in efforts to improve fish and wildlife habitat connectivity, conservation of migratory bird stopover and breeding areas, riparian corridor extent and function, and conservation of SWAP species and habitats. Include the producer communities in this effort to understand needs and overcome barriers to participation. 

## Salmon and Orca Recovery

Salmon and Orca recovery often take center stage and much of the conservation spotlight in Washington state. Pacific salmon and steelhead are central to the culture, economy, and ecology of Washington. The SWAP identifies orcas and nearly all salmonids in Washington as SGCN and many of the species that depend on them or share their habitats. Salmon habitats -both freshwater and marineare crucial targets for research, protection, and restoration; and the terrestrial habitats in contributing watersheds are key to habitat sustainability. Explore the watersheds related to listed salmon species (chinook, chum, coho, sockeye, steelhead, and bull trout) to get an idea about the extent of salmon recovery need in Washington state. 
170
 Largescale salmon conservation actions benefit many species and habitats. Washington state shares these concerns with many Tribal governments, Canada, Idaho and Oregon, and other western Pacific coast states.
Salmon occur statewide, require well-connected marine and freshwater habitats, are indicators of watershed health and contributors to terrestrial and aquatic systems, and, with their complex lifecycle and strong ties to humans' identity, they are a fascinating keystone species. Scientists estimate 138 species of wildlife, from whales to insects, depend on salmon for their food. Recovering salmon is important beyond ensuring salmon persist in Washington: healthy populations of salmon support a robust economy, feed iconic orcas, contribute nutrients to our forests and waterways, enrich a cultural heritage and way of life, and fulfill obligations to Tribes. 
171
 And yet, with all of that recognition and attention, fourteen species of salmon and steelhead in Washington State are still at risk of extinction.
We must do more of the right things in the right places to ensure they don't become extinct, which would cause devastating ecological and cultural impacts.
Urban and industrial land conversion, forest and agricultural practices, water diversion, municipal water demands, overfishing and hydropower development have all contributed to the decline of the number and health of salmon stocks in Puget Sound watersheds and the Columbia River system. 
172
 Humans have damaged their habitat, made migration and access to their home waters harder, polluted their waters, increased predation (such as introduced warmwater fishes), and forced them to compete with hatchery fish for limited resources. 
173
 They face marine environment challenges like ocean warming and acidification, increased predation from marine mammals, and overfishing.
For anadromous salmonid SGCN, hatchery production and hatchery-origin fish can have various impacts on natural populations. Management of these risks is on-going and must continue to meet federal Endangered Species Act-related recovery goals. For many SGCN fish species, mortality due to fisheryrelated impacts (unintentional or incidental catch, illegal harvest) is a threat that continues to need direct management and public education. While WDFW has made significant strides to minimize genetic impacts to freshwater salmonid species from interbreeding with hatchery bred and released non-native salmonids, there is still work to be done. Invasive non-native freshwater fishes can pose competition and predation threats to various SGCN species, especially those with limited native ranges (e.g., Pygmy Whitefish). Lack of data on abundance, distribution, breeding habitats and/or viability status is considered a threat for many SGCN salmonid species and will require significant investment to rectify. 
174 , 175
 In 2021, the State updated its statewide salmon strategy and reconvened the Natural Resources Subcabinet to coordinate state agency implementation of the strategy. The Governor's Salmon Recovery Office (GSRO) (RCW 77.85.030) developed a biennial work plan that aligns state agency priorities with the statewide strategy, regional salmon recovery plans, and Tribal priorities. This work is ongoing and informs state budget and policy priorities for salmon recovery. In 2018-19, the Governor's Southern Resident Killer Whale (Orca) Task Force aligned recovery goals and strategies with the Puget Sound Partnership orca vital sign target and the NMFS 2008 orca Recovery Plan in the Southern Resident Orca Task Force Final Report and Recommendations. Many of these recommendations document the strong link between salmon and orca recovery. Washington, salmon recovery is guided by regionally specific, scientifically rigorous recovery strategies in eight regions. (While these regions do not align exactly the ecoregions of this Plan, regional salmon recovery strategies are referenced in the relevant ecoregion sections). There are common salmon recovery themes across these regions: predation and food web, polluted waters, fish passage barriers, changes in hydrology and fire, hydropower, hatcheries, harvest regulation, and habitat loss and degradation. For Orca recovery, several key themes overlap with salmon recover: prey (salmon) population improvement and conservation, contaminants, climate change, funding and accountability, and future growth.

## Salmon and Orca Recovery Conservation Recommendations

Support and implement the Six Sovereigns Agreement 176 : Yakama, Umatilla, Warm Springs, and Nez Perce Tribes, and Washington, Oregon, and Idaho state governments to implement a comprehensive approach to Columbia Basin salmon restoration and related energy, water, and development-related issues. Look for opportunities to engage with the Upper Columbia United Tribes (UCUT) to provide full tribal representation in the discussions and implementation approaches.
Follow riparian protection, management, and investment recommendations from the Riparian Task Force. Are there particular actions from or directly a companion to the RTF that would benefit from a call out in the SWAP for salmon recovery?
Complete and implement the Fish Passage Prioritization Strategy. Led by WDFW, project partners include state agencies, Fish Barrier Rremoval Board organizations, salmon recovery regions, and fisheries enhancement groups. WDFW is also closely coordinating with tribal governments to develop the strategy. A progress report was released in September 2025. Use, curate, and maintain the Washington State Fish Passage barrier mapping tool as a companion to the prioritization strategy implementation. Riparian conservation has a strong connection to fish passage improvement (e.g. riparian protection and restoration as part of road crossing and culvert barrier improvements can mitigate influence of toxic stormwater).
Improve ecological and genetic outcomes of salmon hatchery programs in Washington State --many of which WDFW is working on or already implementing, in collaboration with tribal and federal partners. 
177
 Actions include:
• Broodstock Management -Manage a hatchery's influence on wild populations via designing a hatchery program as either a segregated or integrated program. Hatchery programs with a conservation goal typically employ integrated broodstock management.
• Right-Size Hatchery Programs to avoid exceeding carrying capacity, align production with ecological limits, and scale down where natural populations are at risk. 

## Indigenous First Foods Sustainability

This section was developed by a small working group out of the Northwest Indian Fish Commission Wildlife Committee and the WDFW SWAP Coordinator. We acknowledge that it is not comprehensive or representative of all Tribes' First Foods. We hope that introducing the topic in this section gives us a platform for ongoing and future learning, communication, and collaboration to amplify this conservation value through implementation and future SWAP revisions.
Indigenous conservation values are deeply rooted in cultural, spiritual, and social traditions that recognize a reciprocal relationship with natural resources, including plants, animals and the ecosystems that sustain them. Indigenous Led Conservation (ILC) recognizes that indigenous peoples have stewarded the sustainable harvest of locally available plants and animals for subsistence, medicine, ceremony, and other cultural connections since time immemorial 179 and continue to do so.
With tribes, the state is committed to co-management of treaty protected resources. In addition to comanagement responsibilities, WDFW and tribes often collaborate and cooperate closely in SGCN and HGCN conservation activities such as restoration to benefit salmonids (in-river, wetlands, riparian; engineered log jams; brood rearing programs), dam removal and mitigation/culvert replacement for fish passage, forestry management, invasive species surveillance and response, research, and recovery actions (e.g., mountain goat, fisher, Columbian sharp-tailed grouse). Tribes contribute significant technical expertise, information, experience, and resources toward success of conservation efforts.
The Washington SWAP grapples with biodiversity loss and promotes actions that support recovery and enhance natural resource sustainability. The biodiversity conservation solution must include indigenous voices, information, and expertise as part of the strategy to connect people working to counter biodiversity loss. With this revision, the SWAP includes First Foods that meet the intention of the SWAP to start to weave indigenous knowledge and engagement into the SWAP's conservation actions and improve relationships with Tribes for future updates and revisions. WDFW and Washington state recognize the vital contributions Tribes make in support of sustainability and stewardship of these natural resources for use and enjoyment of future generations. With more time, capacity, and resources, the SWAP connection can be strengthened between revisions and during implementation.
First Foods sustainability fits into a much broader context of species that are culturally important to tribes. Indigenous Peoples of this region are connected to the lands and waters in and around Washington; they seek to maintain a varied, wild-sourced diet that changes with the seasons, and access to other important natural resources for bodily, cultural, and spiritual health. 
180
 Those resources that are hunted, fished, foraged and collected for sustenance and subsistence are often referred to as First Foods.
A few examples include plants and their roots (sword fern shoots, alder catkins, nettle), berries (huckleberry, salmonberry, cranberry), mushrooms, eggs (birds, fish), mammals (black-tailed deer, elk, mountain goat, black bear), birds (waterfowl), fish and shellfish (salmon, rockfish, smelt, native littleneck clam). Several SGCN can be identified as First Foods. In this plan, we have not identified all SGCN and SGIN for "First Foods" species because many species and their relationships to tribes are protected and sacred information. That said, where it was offered and appropriate, we have tried to weave that information into SGCN descriptions, habitat values, and HGCN. Species such as cedar (bark), orca, loon, salmon, eagles (feathers), and beavers are culturally significant in other ways, and may be central to some Tribes' identities. HGCN stewardship and restoration are important to conserve First Foods such as HGCN westside prairie conservation which can benefit camas and montane systems protection which can benefit huckleberry
foot_46
 (Appendix C4: Adding Strategic HGCN).
With this section, the SWAP does not seek to identify all tribally important species, but to share that tribes' First Foods and other cultural linkages are an important conservation value and consideration in many conservation actions.
With more time and capacity from tribes, WDFW, and tribal consortia (e.g. Affiliated Tribes of Northwest Indians, Northwest Indian Fisheries Commission, Upper Columbia United Tribes, Columbia River Inter-Tribal Fish Commission) and their relevant committees, a more thorough consideration of species and habitats that are important to tribes could be considered, better addressed, and incorporated throughout the plan. With this section, we bring First Foods sustainability into the SWAP and it is our shared intention to explore it further in future revision(s). The Additional Information section could be helpful in that future work.
First Foods are affected by the same sustainability challenges as most fish, wildlife, and plants in Washington state:
• land conversion and development, and the loss of working lands • lack of terrestrial and aquatic habitat connectivity • degraded habitat where these foods are found • invasive plants and animals (green crab, quagga and zebra mussels, knotweed, knapweeds, Scotch broom);
• certain types or timing of recreational activities • wildlife diseases (treponeme-associated hoof disease in elk, hair-loss syndrome in blacktailed deer, chronic wasting disease in cervid populations)
• growth of poorly regulated commercial markets (huckleberries, mushrooms, floral greenery collection such as ferns and salal)
• effects of shifting weather patterns and intensity • ocean acidification and sea level rise, and • wildlife conflict 182 .
These threats have already reduced some First Food resources to the extent that they are not currently meeting the subsistence needs of Tribes (e.g. salmon and steelhead are widely acknowledged to be in this situation). Pressure from these threats is likely to increase over the foreseeable future, further reducing the availability of these natural resources to Tribes.

## Indigenous First Foods Conservation Recommendations

Identify, initiate, and/or participate in landscape-scale conservation efforts where First Foods need more consideration as conservation targets. • Consider native habitats that support First Foods as a criterion when acquiring conservation lands (e.g. Readiness and Environmental Protection Integration lands, local land trusts, Trust Land Transfers, hydroelectric mitigation lands).
Establish a First Foods Conservation Working Group to make conservation recommendations that enhance the viability, sustainability, and availability of First Foods to Tribes.
• Gather baseline data to identify the current status of First Foods availability compared to subsistence and cultural needs.
• Identify subpopulations that are particularly important for subsistence and cultural needs.
• Identify areas where diminishing access threatens availability of First Foods to Tribes.
• Support and encourage sustainable harvest and collection practices.
• Identify conservation opportunities that support sustainable stewardship and use of First Foods in locations where Tribes can access these resources.
• Support stronger enforcement of existing laws related to recreation and the harvesting of natural resources, with an emphasis on commercial sale of First Foods, illegal trail creation, driving off-road in closed areas, and other damaging recreational practices.
Additional Information (references cited in this section are in References) 

## Water Quality and Quantity for Fish and Wildlife

Water is one of our most important natural resources and, as most western states can attest 183 , is the most contentious in conservation. Fish, wildlife, plants, habitats, and people 184 depend on water availability in the right places in the right seasons in the right amounts. While many audiences consider Washington to be "water rich," our ecology, species and habitats, and human communities are dependent on this finite resource.
Water supply is limited by nature and its chemistry and quality are naturally influenced by the substrates it flows over and through. Both water quantity and quality that are essential for the survival, distribution, and diversity of Washington's SGCN and HGCN are also influenced by human activities: where, when, and if we catch rainfall and snowmelt, how we manage flows and withdrawals, whether we value and manage water we can't see (e.g., groundwater, ephemeral wetlands when they are dry), how we manage pollutants and wastes (e.g. treated effluent, stormwater, industrial brine), and how we plan for and respond to emerging issues like chemical wastes discarded or contained years ago, regional surges in server farm (data center) development and expansion 185 , or extreme weather patterns that influence drought, flooding, and changed timing of precipitation. All of these issues are tied to many of the other statewide, transboundary, and ecoregional issues in this plan.
Water quality and quantity conservation are essential to SGCN and HGCN conservation and directly linked to human needs and uses. In Washington state, the Department of Ecology (Ecology) is the primary regulator of water quality, cooperatively implementing and enforcing the Clean Water Act with the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency and implementing the State Water Pollution Control Act. The Washington Department of Health has a role protecting water quality through the regulation of public water systems. Ecology also has authority over surface and ground water allocation and management, 
183
 While much of the contemporary water-management related writing about the western U.S. is focused on the Colorado River, California, or the southwestern states (e.g., authors Mark Reisner, Jack August, Robert Glennon), the vast Columbia River basin has seen its fair share of surface and groundwater controversy since time immemorial related to dam construction and operation, Treaty rights, irrigation, and salmon recovery -all water rights and management issues. 
184
 We often think about drinking water, water for irrigating crops, and water we enjoy in recreation or for scenic vistas. Water is also essential in other food production, growing fiber for clothes, processing raw materials for industrial manufacturing which we rarely see. 
https://www.usgs.gov/water-science-school/science/whathydrology
185
 Stanford University. 2025. 
https://andthewest.stanford.edu/2025/thirsty-for-power-and-water-ai-crunchingdata-centers-sprout-across-the-west/
 providing policies and procedures related to water rights, streamflow restoration, protection of instream flows, irrigation efficiency, and dam safety and construction. 
[1]
 Water use of any sort is subject to the prior appropriation doctrine, also referred to as "first in time, first in right." The rules and planning around surface and groundwater use in Washington are complex.
Washington's RCW Chapter 90.03 is known as Washington's "water code" and forms the foundation of surface water management. Chapters 90.44, closely aligned with the water code but was enacted subsequently and focuses on groundwater. Multiple chapters articulate watershed planning priorities (e.g., 90.82 and 90.94 RCW). Water Resource Inventory Areas or WRIAs are closely, but not exactly, aligned with HUC8 watershed scale and are the units of local government for water resources planning to protect existing water rights, instream flows for fish, address water quality issues, and economic considerations for people and their communities. Fifteen plans developed under RCW 90.94 are done so in collaboration with Ecology which adopts plans that meet legal sufficiency, and other agencies provide information resources to support plan development and implementation. These have implementation plans and most have been updated recently. 
[2]
 Groundwater withdrawals managed by Ecology are either permitted or exempt from permitting requirements if the water use meets a set of requirements generally applicable to smaller, domestic uses.
Water in streams and rivers for fish and wildlife, from headwaters to marine environments. Streams, rivers, riparian habitats, springs are all HGCN dependent on the quality and quantity of water in our streams and rivers. Salmon, other aquatic fish and wildlife, and riparian and instream habitats depend on reliable water flows, especially during critical periods in their lifecycles. Unless adequate minimum flows are established for fish and wildlife and enforced by Washington state agencies, water withdrawals may result in dewatering important mainstem habitats as well as pools and quiet backwater areas that provide essential habitat for the growth and development of juvenile fish, amphibians and aquatic invertebrates. Inadequate flows and water depth in these backwater areas deprive developing fish eggs of oxygen, make it easier for fish predators to find their prey, and generally interfere with the journey of migrating fish. Interrupting or delaying migration can cause adult fish to resort to spawning in unsuitable habitat. 
186
 Water in streams, like wetlands, also contributes to groundwater recharge and reliability, and the persistence and function of spring systems. Low water levels lead to warmer stream temperatures (habitats unsuitable for many cool-and cold-water dependent native fishes, freshwater mussels, and other invertebrates important to stream systems), increased algal growth that can affect the amount of dissolved oxygen available for aquatic life, and more frequent toxin-producing algae blooms in streams and estuaries. Salmon, other aquatic fish and wildlife, and riparian and instream habitats depend on reliable water flows, especially during critical periods in their lifecycles. Water uses and withdrawals may result in dewatering important mainstem habitats as well as pools and quiet backwater areas that provide essential habitat for the growth and development of juvenile fish, amphibians and aquatic invertebrates. Inadequate surface water flows and water depth can deprive developing fish eggs of oxygen, make it easier for fish predators to find their prey, and generally interfere with the journey of migrating fish. Interrupting or delaying migration can cause adult fish to resort to spawning in unsuitable habitat. 
187
 Water in streams, like wetlands, also contributes to groundwater recharge and reliability, and the persistence and function of spring systems. Low water levels lead to warmer stream temperatures (habitats unsuitable for many cool-and cold-water dependent native fishes, freshwater mussels, and other invertebrates important to stream systems), increased algal growth that can affect the amount of dissolved oxygen available for aquatic life, and more frequent toxin-producing algae blooms in streams and estuaries.
Instream and downstream conservation outcomes are affected by decisions such as the amount and duration of withdrawals, amount and timing of releases from dams or irrigation management, instream and riparian vegetation management that changes flows or floodplain character and function a. Additional, emerging water management issues include changing weather patterns that decrease snowpack, contribute to faster snow melt and flooding, and increase frequency or duration of hot and dry summer patterns or drought.
Several priority water quality problems affect SGCN and HGCN related to Washington's fresh, estuarine and marine waters:
• fecal coliform bacteria contamination • contaminated sediments, which are a particular problem in Puget Sound • elevated water temperature, which can quickly alter or degrade an aquatic ecosystem;
• increased sediment in streams, which can blanket important food sources and fish spawning areas
• excess nutrients and pesticides washed into lakes and streams from lawns, golf courses and agricultural fields, which can directly poison aquatic organisms or contaminate waterways
• pollutants in nonpoint stormwater runoff • contamination potential from Hanford Nuclear Reservation which would affect the Columbia River system and its tributaries
• significant shifts in ocean chemistry (acidification 188 ) and temperature (e.g., "the Blob" 189 )
187 SWAP 2015 
188
 Ocean acidification is caused by CO2 absorption in seawater and wastewater discharges high in organic pollutants. More than 30% of Puget Sound's marine species are vulnerable to ocean acidification which affects their ability to form their shells, skeletons, and other calcified body parts (SWAP 2015). 
189
 A suffocating, hot patch of marine waters called "the Blob" caused massive seabird, Pacific cod, and other animal die-offs in 2013, and caused toxic algal blooms and may have contributed to a 75 percent decline in whale Washington Department of Ecology recognizes the need in their 2025-2030 Strategic Plan (Goals 1A, 1D, 2B, 2C) to restore stream flows to save fish from extinction and to consider the pressures of warming temperatures and changing weather, especially drought, that increase the need to plan well for the future water needs in Washington. They are committed to meeting the challenges and working creatively and collaboratively to address current and future water needs of people, farms, and fish (which can benefit other aquatic organisms and habitats).
Barriers to aquatic connectivity. Washington Department of Ecology reports there are 1,242 regulated existing dams in Washington state as of January 2025. 190 U.S. Army Corps of Engineers provides an online map viewer called the National Inventory of Dams of Public Service (link) which is a subset of 835 Washington dams. Dams are intended to obstruct the natural flow of streams and rivers and impound waters for human purposes (drinking water, recreation, flood control). For many aquatic species, dams and diversions have a negative and sometimes significant aquatic habitat effect on connectivity, water quality, and water quantity (especially downstream of dam operations). Through a natural ecology lens, dams have detrimental effects on streams, rivers, riparian, estuaries and marine habitats when they alter natural seasonal flow fluctuations and stream temperatures; interrupt the transport of nutrients and sediments normally deposited along floodplains, deltas, and estuaries; fragment resident aquatic wildlife populations and create barriers for some terrestrial species; and limit access between the ocean and spawning areas. Even on newer dams, spinning turbines that generate electricity often disorient, injure, or kill juvenile fish on their downstream migration to the sea. Ongoing efforts that can mitigate for the adverse impacts of past water diversions and dams include adding or improving fish ladders on hydroelectric dams, screening fish out of irrigation culverts, and requiring adequate year-round instream flows for fish and wildlife. These efforts have become more common and better-funded since the listing of numerous Northwest salmonids under the federal Endangered Species Act. o consider changes to water law to make the legal concept of beneficial use more favorable to leaving water instream See also Recommendations in Riparian Conservation, Salmon and Orca Recovery and Voluntary Conservation sections as these also provide potential benefits to instream water quality and quantity.

## Water for Fish and Wildlife Conservation Recommendations

## Invasive Species Prevention, Surveillance, Detection, Response and Management

The Washington Invasive Species Council (WISC) has assessed more than 700 invasive species in and around Washington to understand which pose the greatest threat to the state's environment, economy, and human health. Fifty (50) priority invasive species were identified in 2019 through a repeatable process and most are impacting Washington ecosystems, natural resources, and economies now. Some of these species are prohibited and regulated aquatic species have a high risk of becoming invasive and established. Prevention of arrival/release is preferred; in lieu of that, millions of dollars are invested in surveillance, detection, and response help prevent establishment, contain expansion, or manage harmful ecological effects. Many of the SGCN and HGCN in the SWAP identify specific invasive species threats as important to resolve as part of restoration, recovery, or reintroduction plans. This section takes a broader look at those very specific issues and proposes recommendations.
In 2020, WISC developed and approved an updated five-year statewide strategy to prevent invading plants and animals from taking hold in the state's forests, waters and farms. The state's economy, recreation, values, culture and native species are threatened by invasives if they establish in Washington. Scientists estimate that $1.2 billion annually is at risk if there is no prevention or management of invasive species. 
191
 Hydroelectric power, irrigation, recreational and commercial crabbing and fishing, trees and forest health, state lands revenue, aquatic habitats, shellfish, and cultural values tied to natural resources, to name a few things, are adversely impacted by invasive species. From the public to state and federal agencies, ecologists to law enforcement, local to regional planners, Tribes to international governments, the solutions to the invasive species problem takes all of us.
"We know how to stop invasive species. However, to do so will take all of us working together. Everyone has a role to play, from hikers to anglers, farmers and ranchers, agency officials and decision makers." -Washington Invasive Species Council
The strategy calls for a broad range of actions focusing on preventing new species from establishing here, educating the public and rapidly deploying when species are found to prevent their spread. The 5year strategy (2025-2030) is available at Washington Invasive Species Council -Reports and provides priorities, direction, and formalizes the council's commitment to invasive species control and prevention. • Advancing professional certifications

## Invasive Species Conservation Recommendations

• mandatory watercraft inspections;
• AIS prevention permits for watercraft and seaplanes;
• vessel inspections, ballast water and tank sediment management and proper discharge, biofouling and hull-fouling management; and
• enforcement of relevant federal and state regulation.

https://invasivespecies.wa.gov/projects/
