## Forest Protection, Restoration, and Health

The 
199
 The Forest Practices HCP is a federal incidental take permit under the Endangered Species Act (ESA)] (date) which covers certain forestry-related activities that are subject to the Washington Forest Practices Act, chapter 76.09 RCW for several salmonid and other aquatic and riparian dependent species, but not upland species such as the marbled murrelet or the northern spotted owl woodlands, large snags, balds, mineral springs, and large, structurally unique trees), old growth forests in five planning units, and unstable slopes.
With the 2008 Farm Bill, Congress tasked the states and territories to assess the condition of trees and forests within their boundaries, regardless of ownership, and develop strategies to:
• conserve working forest landscapes,
• protect forests from harm, and • enhance public benefits from trees and forests.
The resulting State Forest Action Plans were completed in 2010, updated in 2015, comprehensively revised in 2020 by all 59 states and territories, and many are undergoing revision in 2025. These offer practical and comprehensive roadmaps for investing federal, state, local, and private resources where they can be most effective in achieving national conservation goals. 
200
 The Washington State Forest Action Plan provides a comprehensive review of forests across all lands -public, private, rural and urban -and offers solutions to conserve, protect and enhance trees and forests for people and wildlife. Nearly annual reports (available at the link above) are produced to inform revision approximately every five years. There is a strong relationship between the State Forest Action Plan's priority ecosystem conservation and restoration actions for wildlife and salmon recovery, the SWAP, and other conservation plans across the state for fish and wildlife resources and their habitats.
State rules and planning, along with WDFW PHS Management Guidelines, local jurisdictions' planning and implementation of the Growth Management Act, and other species-benefitting conservation agreements such as the Fisher Candidate Conservation Agreement with Assurances
foot_49
 , are efforts that can contribute to preventing the need to federally list additional species under the Endangered Species Act, protect the economic viability of the timber industry in Washington, and potentially contribute to HGCN and SGCN conservation across the state.
Washington's vast and productive forests are fundamental to our natural resource economy, recreation and outdoor industry, rural livelihoods, clean water, and many SGCN. Even with the importance of forests to so many of Washington's values, forest acres declined by 394,000 acres (or 2%) by 2019. 
202
 As discussed in the sections above (Voluntary Conservation, Salmon and Orca Recovery), conversion of forests to unforested landscapes and developed uses (commercial and residential) are significant concerns for the persistence and integrity of forested lands in Washington state. Four major threats to forest system sustainability and fish and wildlife habitat are
• conversion to residential uses (e.g. homes, second homes, farmettes, ranchettes) particularly in suburban and exurban areas, which signal growth direction of urban areas, and commercial development;
• internal and core connectivity disruption of larger forest patches due to conversion or harvest patterns across ownerships;
• size, frequency, and intensity of wildfires placing rural homeowners, communities at the forested wildland-urban interface, and forest-dependent threatened and endangered species at tremendous risk, paired with the values and perceptions of people that develop and take actions to remediate those risks; and
• loss of large tract representation, distribution, and ecological function of old growth and mature forests and riparian forests.
foot_51
Apply ecologically appropriate fire resiliency and forest health management, including Firewise Community outreach and actions for fire resilience in the Wildland -Urban Interface (WUI), that consider full life cycle needs of SGCN and HGCN functions.

Many of these topics are addressed in SGCN and HGCN conservation actions. These statewide and transboundary topics already have active conservation partnerships taking action, and we intend to work collaboratively with tribes and partners over the next several years to capture the need and top three to five recommended actions for the next SWAP revision: 

## Ecoregions Maps and Tables

Ecoregions are places with similar physical and environmental characteristics, where ecosystems are mostly alike and are mapped at a very large scale. Ecologists who map these places consider geology, landforms, soils, vegetation, climate, land use, fish and wildlife, and hydrology. 
204
 Ecoregion characteristics are influenced by human uses, and our emotional and cultural connections to these places where we live, work and play. These mapped boundaries are helpful to organize how the conservation community can work at big, similar landscape and ecosystem scales to improve resilience and conserve habitats and species at many scales. For instance, ecologists working across the Willamette Valley Ecoregion in Oregon and Washington can share similar resources, techniques, and expertise to conserve, restore, and connect larger areas of native prairie vegetation communities; special features like dry prairie, mounds, and wet prairie; and SGCN populations and subpopulations such as pocket gophers, butterflies, amphibians, native grasses, rare plants, and related priority communities such as oak woodlands.
This ecological organization provides boundaries and labels to help with partnership-building, community engagement, and communicating progress even though those boundaries are not hard lines on the ground. Ecological systems blend together at these boundaries, and conservation projects rely on real-world survey and monitoring data and well-informed modeled knowledge for project-level planning, implementation, monitoring, research and adaptation. Over the last 30 years, there have been several versions of Washington ecoregions, depending on the environmental factors that planners

)

 , each with a graphic of the ecoregion showing public lands and links to tabulated biodiversity conservation targets. For additional details, please refer to other tables in Appendix D:

• BLMT SGCN which lists the ecoregion's SGCN, and • BLMT NVC + GET + HGCN which lists the NVC Groups and GET Functional Groups that are likely to occur in the ecoregion, which ones are HGCN and why, whether each type has SGCN Closely Associated.
(file name: 20251015_BlueMountainsEXAMPLE_SGCNHGCN_DRAFT_SWAP2025.xlsx) This is the example table mentioned in the introduction to the Ecoregions section. While not a direct impact in the Blue Mountains ecoregion, wind energy development in adjacent areas can create impacts to migratory birds, nesting and foraging hawks, and bats that pass through or use the Blues and deep river canyons at certain flight periods in their lifecycle. Several ecological vulnerabilities in this ecoregion will also affect people, primarily related to observed and expected earlier/faster snowmelt, decreased snowpack, and higher air temperatures/warmer weather (Halofsky and Peterson 2017):

## Additional Ecoregion Conservation Information

• Earlier spring and higher volumes in headwaters and perennial streams -increased peak flows can damage roads near streams affecting access for recreation and fire-fighting, lower water quality for fish and aquatic invertebrates, and blowout or scour instream habitats. Floodplains and riparian areas are also vulnerable to vegetation loss from more intense flow/flooding.
• Lower summer flows in perennial streams -negative effects to downstream municipal and public water use during peak demand periods for irrigation and power supply; lower instream flows, less stream connectivity, and warmer in-stream temperatures which adversely affect native coldwater fish and the invertebrates that rely on those stream conditions (may favor nonnative warm-water adapted fish); low flows also affect aquatic recreation opportunities and firefighting water resources.
• Seasonal water availability, seasonal water features (e.g. ephemeral wetlands), and soil moisture across the region will decrease and, in some areas, stay dry beyond the summer season, which can adversely affect wetland dependent rare plants, migrating birds, amphibians, and aquatic invertebrates directly, and can require other wildlife to travel farther for water resources which increases body stress. Effects may be cumulative over time and persist into the future. This will shift vegetation communities toward shrub and grasslands, reducing the forestgrassland mosaic. Drought-tolerant and non-native plant species will be more successful.
Intensive dry summers and longer-term drought also affects agriculture costs, available range forage for livestock, and the need for supplemental feed. This can also produce conflicts if wildlife seek supplemental food and water where people are providing that for their crops, orchards, livestock, and outdoor pets. Ephemeral wetlands important for certain insects and amphibians and earlier, longer dry conditions will strand eggs and larvae. Low soil moisture increases forest and grassland vulnerability to intense wildfire and harmful insect damage.
• Shifts in some areas to entirely snow-free mid-elevations will contribute to less spring and summer water availability overall. Vegetation, fish and wildlife may not be able to fully recover between seasons which can compound vulnerability to and effects of fire, insects, disease, and lower reproduction.
• Species that depend on in-stream cool water refugia, groundwater-dependent vegetation and systems, riparian and floodplains vegetation (cottonwood, willow, aspen) and substrates, and wetlands will be the most vulnerable. Species that favor upland, dry-adapted vegetation and post-fire communities may not be as affected by these changes.

## Reporting Progress and Update Opportunities

WDFW will, at a minimum, follow current USFWS Guidance (2017) for update processes and procedures (Element 6), unless and until that guidance changes for SWAP pieces that are directly connected to live data sources. Revisions require staff capacity, financial resources, and time from collaborators and can be constrained by these. Review and update will occur at least every ten years as is required, and the next revision will be due October 1, 2035.
SWAP 2025 is using a framework and supporting data structures that will enable more frequent data updates for existing SGCN, SGIN, and HGCN. This will eventually help WDFW web-enable SWAP content, connect web delivery to data and other resources that are regularly updated, and keep our SWAP products more relevant for users. Any changes that might add or subtract SGCN, threats and actions for those species, or significantly change the conservation approach for the SWAP need to be coordinated through the USFWS minor or major revision process laid out in the Guidance prior to making the change.
WDFW will be tracking requested changes for potential revisions between the ten-year intervals. Opportunities to provide citation-supported suggestions and recommendations will be posted periodically on the WDFW SWAP webpage.
WDFW will be working on implementation tracking as a result of this SWAP and will report on its relationship to the WDFW 25-Year Strategic Plan.
WDFW serves the whole public and engagement is intended to foster good governance through transparency, communication, and public service; foster SWAP implementation effectiveness; and actively create and maintain innovative and enduring conservation.
foot_53
 WDFW will be hosting much more engagement opportunities between revisions to promote SWAP content and use, receive feedback, and improve our next SWAP.
It is not enough to simply implement projects and spend the money; monitoring and documentation must show that conservation progress is being made or a course-correction is needed. Monitoring and documenting the response of targets to applied conservation actions reports conservation progress, shapes adaptive management, and demonstrates effectiveness -not just spending the money, but moving the needle toward sustainability, and justifying a change in approach, if needed.
As SWAP 2025 is implemented and more partners and communities learn about new available information and data, WDFW will document opportunities to ingest new relevant information and determine priority, need, and capacity to address.

## SWAP Planning Recommendations

In the course of this revision, WDFW identified recommendations for Plan improvement that could be pursued between revisions to improve each next plan with better organization, data collection and processing, or synthesis. • WDFW PHS Priority Habitats and Species online tools, and • species-habitat ecoregional (finer scale) modeling of the most important biodiversity areas (e.g. map of biodiversity importance based on richness of imperiled species, range-size rarity, protection rarity, and unprotected areas with imperiled species richness).
Improve NVC + GET system spatial data for Washington state, and define appropriate uses. Both NVC Groups and GET Functional Groups have limitations in fine-scale conservation project planning (NVC is available spatially but is a coarse filter; GET is presented at inconsistent spatial scales by type). To be able to use the consistent vocabulary benefits AND better relate big-picture, system-level thinking to fine-scale actions needed on the ground:
• use NVC to prioritize at coarse scale, document how NVC is used in project planning, and add finer scale system mapping to projects where feasible to share that information with WA DNR to improve NVC utility for all Washington conservation users;
• select, document, and assign state-available current data sets to GET Functional Groups to create a spatial data set useful for springs, caves, freshwater, estuarine, and marine conservation in the SWAP data set.
Species distribution models. Develop and document non-salmonid species distribution model methods that can include/be related to WDFW and WA DNR institutional data sets, HUC10 watersheds, NVC Groups, and spatial data representation of GET Functional Groups for Washington state (including appropriate marine systems). For the next revision, where applicable, update species range and distribution.
Subpopulation ranges. Using the HUC 10 structure, identify terrestrial and freshwater SGCN subpopulations and update the SWAP database so that data displays can be searched on the SGCN subpopulations of concern.
Marine conservation organization for range and habitats. Identify the most relevant basin data set for Washington state conservation, relate the marine SGCN to those marine basins, and provide a crosswalk to the HUC10s selected in the SWAP (this may be a separate set of data and relatable to the GET Functional Group related data recommendation).
Review the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan (WAHCAP) recommendations for data and product improvement and seek ways to integrate the spatial WAHCAP data with relevant SWAP data.
Review montane and ocean systems identified in Strategic HGCN (Appendix C4) using PHS and NHP processes in the next five years, document the findings, and include those findings in the next SWAP update to shape the future HGCN list. Enter Narrative or Bulleted List Here models, and other information. We used TaxoID to organize species-specific SWAP required information.
To collect vertebrate and invertebrate species-related SWAP information for this revision, WDFW Wildlife Science created an ArcGIS-based data entry tool with four units, relatable to the TaxoID for each species. The ArcGIS tool provides the ability for subject matter experts (SMEs) to
• choose a species to review, see existing status and some institutionally available aggregated data and information, and select a SWAP label (SGCN, SGIN, Other) (Unit: Species Status);
• pick HUC10 watersheds associated with precalculated data and other information to document the species range of conservation opportunity (Unit: Range);
• pick NVC Group and GET Functional Group habitat associations for each species (Unit: Habitat Associations); and
• identify important contemporary threats and actions using the Conservation Standards vocabulary (Unit: Threats and Actions).
Most units of the tool provided fields for SMEs to provide citable reasons for their selections, capturing the reason(s) behind species labels and conservation recommendations.
The benefit of the tool is that we now have a SWAP database that
• is tied to the definitions and frameworks of the SWAP • relates to Taxo for current nomenclature and status as those changes are made (e.g. updated scientific names, new species, federal or state listing status, NHP NatureServe ranks)
• holds the history of reasons between revisions • uses consistent language across years (whether updating every ten years as required or more frequently)
• can enable online display (at some point)
• can be related to other national SWAP queries and database displays, and • will allow for an easier defensible SWAP update (would not have to wait ten years).
Most of the plant information was also put into the same format to create tables for this plan; however, WA DNR NHP is the steward of plant data, so the SWAP database that resides in WDFW will be a static snapshot in time between revisions for SGCN plants.
We learned a lot deploying the new tool and will need to make some changes in the coming years to the tool structure and the instructions for using it. It allows WDFW to more iteratively, transparently, and consistently capture and present SWAP species-specific information.
This appendix provides details about how selection processes for SGCN and related information, SGIN, vulnerable concentrations, and HGCN were applied by SMEs based on data, expertise, conservation professional network connections, literature, models, and other information.

## SGCN

This is the required category for SWAP. SGCN criteria were revised from 2015 to be more transparent and repeatable. Two primary criteria were defined for SGCN:
• Defined Status. Species that fit this set of criteria have a vetted process that supports a defined status.
o Several considerations and sensitivities arose as we applied the criteria, and these were addressed:
• 'Enough' information. To designate an SGCN, we must also provide the required elementsinformation on distribution and abundance (some understanding of status), habitats the species relies on, and threats and actions. Many proposed SGCN that could not meet this consideration, and for which environmental or contextual threats could be defined, were put in the SGIN category.
• Climate influence on natural movement. Some species were considered even if not native to Washington now, due to the influence of weather patterns, phenology shifts, and vegetation changes that influence habitat suitability. No species were identified as SGCN that meet this consideration, but we will retain that as an option for future updates.
• Extirpated with Recovery Potential. A few species are officially considered extirpated in Washington, but still are conservation targets for USFWS, adjacent states, and/or other governments with active recovery efforts. Woodland caribou is a good example of this consideration.
• Uncommon/Peripheral. In the SWAP, this consideration means that the species population is not likely to be influenced by management in our state; the species is not detected here regularly. These are usually birds that may be on the state species lists, have records in eBird, or are detected here periodically as an accidental or vagrant. In this sense, "uncommon" does not mean rare. It just means we don't detect them in Washington very often if at all, and we would not base management decisions on a species that isn't dependent on Washington's habitats. Although these species are not considered for the SGCN table, data on them is always welcome.
It is important to document uncommon and peripheral occurrences to better understand longterm shifts and any future influences on patterns of species collapse, range contraction or shifts, historical importance in recovery planning, and genetic, evolutionary, and biodiversity considerations. 215
Eleven (11) species met the PHS species criteria and were identified as uncommonly or infrequently occurring in Washington state with populations that are not expected to be affected by conservation management activities in Washington. None of these are proposed as SGCN but could benefit from conservation actions taken for vulnerable aggregations and HGCN.
• American golden-plover The interactions among these four species and salmon recovery are very complex site-and system-specific conservation and management balance considerations. These species are not considered SGCN but will be considered in vulnerable aggregations and HGCN conservation benefits.
• Hunted, Fished and Foraged SGCN. See WDFW Commission Wildlife Committee briefing paper, December 14, 2023. Commission documents on the WDFW website are available for more than ten years.
• Subpopulations. Divisions smaller than populations, including subpopulation, evolutionarily significant unit (ESU), or distinct population segment (DPS) were identified by some SMEs as the focus unit for conservation. The SGCN table identifies the subpopulation if that unit was identified, although the range and habitat associations may reflect the entire population in Washington state. Subpopulations were highlighted in threats and actions where relevant. WDFW has identified a SWAP Planning Recommendation to improve our subpopulation data in the SWAP database (i.e., identify HUC10 watershed range for the subpopulations as a subset of the parent population record).
The results of this process are in two tables: 

## A Note on NatureServe Ranks in Washington

Many states have a "natural heritage program" and are part of the North American NatureServe network that stewards biodiversity data. Many states use this network as a common language for collecting, managing, and sharing species, site, and ecological system information with standard and rigorous methods across the network to define status of species and systems. This shared language has been very helpful to inform conservation at the site to landscape scale. consistent framework, not that different from SWAP 2015 (Appendix B) which used HUC 10 and HUC 12 scales, well-supported by information, and meets the intention of Element 1.
HUCs selected for a species indicate that the species may occur within that HUC, not that the species occurs throughout the entire HUC.
The outer boundaries of HUCs selected for each SGCN (example Figure 
24
) frame the broadest geography within Washington where a species is known to occur or could occur based on defensible information (e.g. recovery plans, reintroduction plans, habitat associations, occurrence or survey data in an institutional database or trusted source, such as research grade iNaturalist or validated eBird data). A selected HUC does not indicate that suitable habitat is widely or evenly distributed throughout the HUC; the area could contain lands and waters where species do not or cannot live, including 'matrix' through which the species travels in seasonal migration or dispersal. • Species found in HUC, observation data available but not yet in an institutional database (species data may have been available from recent or current years, but not yet processed into the agency database)
• Species found in HUC, observation data is in an institutional source and/or a trusted source (e.g. verified research grade iNaturalist)
• Species found in HUC, information from a trusted source (not necessarily observation data) is available in literature or by WDFW personal communication with an expert in the field (data has not been published, but the source is reputable and acknowledged as an expert in that species)
• Species may be found in HUC, proximal to known occurrences (many of these are similar or the same as those HUCs turned on as 'proximal' in SWAP 2015)
• Species may be found in HUC if documented recovery, reintroduction, or restoration actions may occur in the future 217
• Species may be found in HUC (circumstances noted in comments; upon review and validation of the supporting information, such as suitable habitat known to occur, many of these may be more appropriately attributed to other reasons).
SMEs were supported in their selections with institutionally provided observation data aggregated by HUC. In some instances, that precalculated data might have shown a HUC to have more than one percent of the observation data, but the SME, regional biologists, and/or other experts conferred and determined that suitable habitat or species were no longer present for reasons that either permanently or long-term impacted the species potential to occur there (e.g., development, invasive species, wildfire). Figure 
25
 shows an example screenshot from the tool where a SME reviewed a HUC with observations, did not select the HUC, and provided a citation supporting that decision. • focus for identifying threats and actions;
• documented observations and information that the SME 'trusts' (1978 to present) and which can be citable in the plan;
• any part of the species life cycle important to species conservation;
217 These documented actions may be in a recovery plan, post-fire restoration plan, grant scope, reintroduction plan, or other citable source. This is not a commitment to do this work as those projects are funding, capacity, and expertise dependent, but is meant to show conservation opportunity potential for the species.
• 'conservation potential' -species may occur through conservation action in the foreseeable future (e.g., a recovery plan includes action in this area, restoration is possible for the species and its habitats, landscape connectivity could enable species persistence or restoration) and feasible geography. HUC 10s were not selected for historical range where there is no foreseeable potential to restore a functional population of the species.
Why select HUCs without current occurrence data in an institutional system? All selected HUCs are supported by information that can be referenced. Using only occurrence data may limit conservation potential (i.e., species may not have been surveyed in all potential places due to access, capacity, or funding limitations). For species that are already imperiled, it is important to communicate opportunities to recover the species in places where they don't currently exist and stabilize/improve populations where they currently occur. Proximal HUCs tell us that we need more information in the institutional systems, to inform opportunities, document change, and develop further actions. These are areas where a conservation project may be to survey for species in areas where the species has previously not been detected, to add that information to institutional databases. Other types of project planning must consider institutional data and information to have well-curated reference information to start with -essentially, a good project baseline from which to measure effectiveness.
Marine, Island, and Shoreline Species. Terrestrially defined HUCs are imperfect options for marine-and island-related species: several islands may be grouped into one HUC; an island may show as part of a mainland HUC; and, marine areas such as estuaries, bays, or shorelines may be delineated somewhat coarsely including some of the terrestrial watersheds. All Washington terrestrial and marine watersheds were related to the nine ecoregions so that the information for SGCN could be sorted into ecoregions.
There is admittedly fault in this approach for marine, shorebird, and some lowland species because some of the HUC10 watersheds touch marine shorelines and waters and may extend far inland and upland, placing a marine-dependent species in a mountainous ecoregion. For example, a shark is attributed to a marine-touching or marine-focused HUC10; the data sorting may put that shark also in a mountainous ecoregion, like the North Cascades (figure below, same as Figure 
9
 in plan body), if the HUC spans both the lowland and highland ecoregions. After sorting the data, subject matter experts reviewed the tables and have sorted marine species out of ecoregions where they clearly do not occur (we don't have sharks in the mountains!). WDFW has identified this limitation and will work on solutions to that in the period between revisions (see SWAP Planning Recommendations section).
Sorting species and habitats into ecoregions by HUC. Once SGCN were assigned to HUCs, then HUCs were sorted into ecoregions. Each HUC was attributed to one or more ecoregions. Nine HUCs were largely in an ecoregion, with just a tiny sliver in another ecoregion. These nine HUCs were removed from the ecoregion where the sliver occurs because of their very low percentage area within the ecoregion (Table 
7
). One additional HUC sliver was considered and could still be removed if species range review determines that is appropriate: HUC 1711001204 Lower Sammamish River with 74 acres in the West Cascades ecoregion. There are 150 terrestrial and aquatic (including marine) systems that potentially occur in Washington state (Table 
C3
), and each SGCN was given an association using the same definitions used in SWAP 2015 (Appendix B, page B-4):
• Closely Associated: The species demonstrates preference for the ecological system, as indicated by greater occurrence, high densities, greater reproductive output, or other indicators of preference, than in other systems. A species that is closely associated to individual ecological systems often rely on one to a few ecological systems for a significant part or all of its life history requirements.
• Generally Associated: The species occurs in, but does not prefer, the ecological system, as indicated by lesser occurrence, lower densities, or other indicators of a general relationship with the ecological system. A species that is generally associated with individual ecological systems can typically rely on numerous ecological systems to meet its life history requirements.
• Unsuitable: A species demonstrates no use or only occasional use of the ecological system.
• Unknown: The species' use of the ecological system is unknown. There were questions or uncertainty about species use of an ecological system.
Vertebrate and invertebrate associations were made by WDFW SMEs viewing aggregated observation data for species, where available; literature, expertise, and professional networks; and NVC Group and GET Functional Group descriptions. Selections were made in an ArcGIS tool.
Plant SGCN were associated to NVC Groups by WA DNR NHP through their NatureServe occurrence processes, which use the entire NVC Classification. 
219
 . Because this hierarchy is embedded in WA DNR NHP data management structures, all plant SGCN are considered closely associated to the NVC Group identified in the SWAP work. No GET Functional Groups were identified for plant SGCN during the 2024 SME work; however, the change noted in Table 
18
 for Temperate Pacific Seagrass Bed will potentially change that in the WA DNR NHP database (that was not changed for the SGCN plants habitat associations Table 
D6b
 but we will try to change in the final 2025 SWAP with DNR's coordination).

## Challenges and Issues

NVC Groups. This coarse scale is useful for seeing patterns in SGCN associations, landscape conservation opportunities that can benefit many SGCN, and ecoregional relationships. It is not a complete wall-towall land use or type classification data set; there are gaps in spatial data. And, it is not appropriate for project-level planning at the site scale because the spatial NVC Group data is not that finely accurate. NVC Groups spatial data was aggregated into a 1-mile hex grid pattern to generalize the scale and discourage inappropriate fine-scale use. This conversion from ecological systems static map to the NVC Group spatial data will have some "growing pains" as we learn more about the best use and limitations of this information. For now, it's a good set for our SWAP purposes. And, as we use it, we will make other spatial data relationships to these Groups to refine the site-scale planning abilities and retain the ability to "roll up" to these coarser scales for reporting implementation or sorting actions, species, and habitats that are of interest. We will need to define appropriate uses for this data set related to SWAP conservation action planning.
Other Spatial Data Issues. Not all NVC Groups are available for Washington as a spatial data set (only 52 out of 79 mapped) and none of the GET Functional Groups, NVC ruderal types, or NVC anthropogenic types are mapped for Washington state at a scale that is useful. We realized into the work that we would have benefitted from institutional state data set relationships to many of the unmapped types to make the SME work easier (e.g. GET M1.2 Kelp Forests related to DNR's kelp spatial data). Even though we supported SMEs with aggregated species observation data, they could not easily see the habitat association relationships on a map when we did the ArcGIS tool work. SMEs had to review the descriptions and species aggregated observation data and make an informed decision.
Marine and Freshwater Species Habitat Associations. As with SWAP 2015 decisions, this was a difficult exercise. In 2015, many freshwater and marine species were associated with mapped terrestrial or contributing systems (e.g., bull trout was associated with North Pacific Maritime Douglas-fir -Western Hemlock Rainforest). That decision makes sense when we think about all of the influences on the aquatic environments -terrestrial threats and conservation actions impact the aquatic environments, so the questions are always, "Do you include the terrestrial habitats in aquatic species' associations? If yes, how far into the uplands do you go with your thinking?" Complicated further by unmapped GET types (all non-vegetated aquatic types are GET and unmapped), how would we make this exercise valuable to conservation action planning or implementation? SMEs made good choices, sticking very close to the habitats that the species occur in (or could occur in, with restoration or recovery work). In some instances, riparian vegetation types may be selected for instream SGCN because of a close association or input from streamside vegetation. It's important to keep in mind that upland restoration and protection actions are very beneficial for instream habitats and aquatic species, and in most cases the conservation actions reflect this need.
Ruderal vegetation. "Ruderal" is a term used in NatureServe vegetation classification
foot_62
 to capture human-influenced or human-shifted systems. Different from completely converted and managed land use types that are still vegetated, like orchards or agricultural fields actively in use, ruderal types give an "in between" classification option: not quite native, not quite totally converted or gone. While these types often have a dominant invasive species presence, they also have key identifying features of the natural type. In many landscapes, ruderal ecosystems occupy large areas--sometimes more than any other category of communities--and can provide important biodiversity functions. Sometimes, these ruderal types are useful for wildlife especially if adjacent to native habitats and if they can be restored to native. Annual grasslands in a sagebrush type are a good example (Davies et al. 2021) and we see this in Washington state. Another good example is North Pacific Maritime Coastal Ruderal Dune & Bluff. These are shifting sand dunes and other sandy areas at sea level along the Pacific Coast dominated by nonnative beachgrasses and may have mixed areas of restoration -planted native dune grass, may include introduced low shrubs and other non-native species generally limited to those that can invade and thrive in the shifting sand and salt-spray environment. But, in some areas, that ruderal type may be very important to retain dunes during storm surge or may provide refuge for some dune-dependent species in that shifting environment. Ruderal ecosystems are generally not conservation priorities -we don't manage to a ruderal type -although they may support rare species or function as important landscape connectors or matrices in reserves. In landcover or GAP mapping, these ruderal types are important to map because of their large extent. But, the question for SWAP was whether associating these types to SGCN and HGCN would help drive good conservation decisions. The answer was "it depends" and we didn't really have the time to consider a more full critique or set of circumstances where it would be appropriate to say "yes" or "no" so that we could document that for the next revision. During SWAP SGCN and HGCN work, the focus was on native systems, although some ruderal types were associated.
Unfortunately, we didn't apply that uniformly, so it is possible that some ruderal associations were missed. We also did not discuss or frame the value of (or lack of value) in selecting these types. One criticism is that the spatial data is too coarse to define where restoration of a ruderal type would be beneficial at the site scale, so we should focus only on associating native habitat systems since that would be the restoration objective. We have more work to do here to clearly define how to handle ruderal associations in the SWAP.
Anthropogenic or Artificial Habitats. Sometimes, plants, fish, and wildlife can't be that choosy to only live in natural (or mostly natural) habitats and still persist. In some places, the human-modified environment may be the only place the species is retaining a toehold while ecologists, biologists, and restoration specialists figure out the best approach. Wetland shrub horticultural crops such as cranberries, managed monotypical forest plantations, grain crops, artificial shorelines, and large reservoirs are examples of these types which can still serve limited natural habitat function and provide connectivity better than suburban or urban areas, although there are often human-wildlife or humanplant conflicts. Some examples include elk eating the cranberry crop, sandhill cranes nesting when it's time to harvest the wheat, marine mammals drawn to artificial haulouts and being territorial, and reservoir management that may conflict with downstream aquatic life needs. SMEs considered whether human-influenced or human-constructed habitats are key for SGCN and HGCN; in some cases that last toehold is what's giving us the opportunity to make good decisions, find funding, create partnerships, and resolve potential conflicts to improve the success and durability of conservation investments of social capital, expertise, time, and money.

## Appendix C4. HGCN selection

Relatable to Required Element 2. Descriptions of locations and relative condition of key habitats and community types essential to conservation of SWAP species.
An "Important Habitats" framework was developed by a cross-Program WDFW team and shared widely during engagement for comment. The framework outlined categories for statewide and ecoregion habitat conservation strategies. As the concepts were honed, the name was changed to Habitats of Greatest Conservation Need (HGCN) to generally align with SWAP 2015.
HGCN occur at many scales -big to small, regional to sites. HGC support the sustainability of species and many support the ecosystem functions that also influence people's well-being and livelihoods. In conservation project planning, one scale may be the focus (such as restoring habitat for a speciesspecific site) and the relationship to other scales in the framework should be considered (e.g. the riparian relationship to an instream habitat restoration).
Many types of information identify the habitat categories in this framework, inform the conservation actions that people take in the statewide and ecoregion sections of the SWAP, and evolve over time with new information. WDFW and WA DNR NHP identified specific information sources to support the identification of HGCN. In conservation action planning and implementation, project teams can apply other sources to meet their specific purposes.
The purpose of including HGCN in the SWAP is to guide habitat and system conservation, research, and management in ecoregions to support fish, wildlife, plants, and human well-being. The HGCN framework
• shows relationships among WDFW's Priority Habitats, Washington Natural Heritage Plan (2025), important climate resiliency and habitat connectivity values, system and habitat conservation, and Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) habitats;
• includes conservation contributions in human spaces -parklands, urban riparian corridors, wildland-urban interfaces, agricultural and ranching lands;
• uses science-based resources and supporting documentation; and,
• communicates the importance of habitat conservation to a wide variety of audiences.
Three HGCN categories were proposed: Habitat Support, Strategic, and Local/Special.

## Habitat Support HGCN

Habitat Support HGCN support durable and resilient finer-scaled habitats for species and represent ecological concepts that can be applied at any scale from site to landscape, can be supported by data and/or modeled information, are not species-specific, and are important in part or whole to protect habitat function and ecosystem services. These places can be a matrix of various habitat types (e.g., riparian, wetlands, floodplain, and river together), successional stages, and quality to achieve the value(s) of biodiversity areas, corridors, and/or climate refugia.

## Biodiversity Areas

These can be large, intact, internally well-connected blocks of relatively high-quality habitat or vegetation communities where habitat function is not impaired by human activities (cores); areas identified as biologically diverse through a scientifically based assessment conducted over a landscape scale (e.g., statewide, ecoregion, county-or city-wide, watershed) (examples include WDFW Local Habitat Assessments, Pierce County Biodiversity Network, Priority Amphibian and Reptile Conservation Areas 221 ); or areas within a city or an urban growth area (UGA) that contains habitat that is valuable to fish or wildlife and is mostly comprised of native vegetation. 
222
 Biodiversity areas meet at least one of the following criteria based on the IUCN 2018 guidelines 223 :
• supports a high diversity of species, habitats or ecosystems, especially these of high conservation priority (listed in PHS and/or as Species of Greatest Conservation Need).
• is large enough to sustain genetically diverse, viable population(s) of highly threatened or rare species, ensuring long-term adaptability through sufficient birth and survival rates that counteract mortality and maintain genetic diversity.
• holds geographically restricted (e.g., endemic) populations of species or ecosystems.
• maintains a high degree of naturalness and ecological integrity, meaning its ecological processes and functions are largely intact and relatively undisturbed by human activities.
• serves as a crucial refuge for species or ecosystems, allowing them to persist during periods of environmental stress (e.g., drought, extreme temperatures), or providing a critical refuge from human impacts.
• is irreplaceable for the persistence of biodiversity across the larger landscape (e.g., a core habitat connecting other source populations). "Large" and "high quality" are relative to the conservation objectives and may differ by species/habitat relationships and spatial scale of the analysis or planning (statewide, regional, local). "Internally wellconnected" means there are relatively few or no human infrastructure barriers or adverse influences on wildlife movement and other essential activities within the area. The 'edge' of a biodiversity area is often a gradient -not a hard line or border -determined by influences on habitat quality (e.g. disruptions in canopy closure or loss of understory, light or noise penetration, non-native species invasion, edge intrusions by human activity) that may affect how the core does or does not support species' needs.

## Corridors

Corridors are well-connected habitats with relatively few or no internal barriers that link cores and support fish and wildlife movement (daily, seasonal, annual); facilitate access to food (e.g. winter/summer range for mule deer, foraging/nesting range for marbled murrelet) and shelter; foster genetic diversity (not isolating subpopulations for mating or pollination); and allow natural dispersal/colonization of new suitable habitat.

## Climate Refugia

Climate refugia are more stable in their functions and less impacted by habitat suitability-altering effects of climate change such as water and air temperature extremes, altered wildfire intensity or frequency, or significant changes in precipitation (drought, flood). These areas can protect some native species populations that cannot move or disperse readily under these challenges, potentially conserving genetic diversity and evolutionary potential.

## Strategic HGCN

Ecosystems are defined by their shared characteristics and 'Strategic Habitats' are specific ecosystems that are critical for biodiversity conservation in Washington, similar to the habitats of the same name identified in the Oregon Conservation Strategy. These habitats are more easily relatable to specific vegetation or aquatic systems (e.g. shrubsteppe, wetlands) and the ecoregions where they occur. Most can be associated to National Vegetation Classification (NVC) Group(s) or Global Ecosystem Typology (GET) Functional Group(s) which makes a direct connection to the framework for SGCN habitat associations in the SWAP. Strategic HGCN include
• WDFW Priority Habitats that are not 'features' and which can be related one-to-one or one-tomany to NVC Groups (habitats defined by their vegetation) or GET Functional Groups (aquatic and marine habitats not defined by vegetation); and Limited human-created habitat surrogates for native habitats were considered for inclusion in this section. Many have a strong connection to particular ecoregion SGCN (e.g., shrubsteppe Conservation Reserve Program lands in the Columbia Basin that are important to pygmy rabbits and greater sagegrouse) and are included in the ecoregion, rather than in this section. Other types, such as wellestablished or 'legacy' constructed wetlands or artificial reefs are also important, but we lacked the level of information that could be useful for this plan to identify conservation actions tied to these features. We will continue to explore this topic for another revision.
Local / Special HGCN include (these are all described in the SWAP section for these habitats):
• Bat foraging areas, roosts, maternity colonies 

## Adding Strategic HGCN

The intention in SWAP 2015 was sound -identifying and doing conservation for systems that support many SGCN 226 can create beneficial efficiency, bring conservation practitioners from different communities (e.g., species biologists, habitat restoration specialists, landscape ecologists, social scientists) together, and improve resiliency at the system level that also benefits other species that are not SGCN. Because SWAP 2025 includes PHS Priority Habitats as HGCN, some of that system relationship to multiple species is built in, and there is some overlap among PHS and NHP NVC Groups.
Example. Shrubsteppe is a PHS Priority Habitat and related to seven NVC Groups. Of those, five NVC Groups are also identified by NHP for conservation. The number of SGCN associated to those NVC Groups ranges from 1 to 33, with four of the systems associated with 18 or more SGCN.
However, a distinction driven by the SGCN numbers alone doesn't incorporate philosophical or policy values about what makes a habitat important for biodiversity even though that is one good indicator.
To evaluate if SWAP 2025 should add HGCN beyond the PHS and NHP systems, all NVC Groups and GET Functional Groups in Washington state that are not cultural types (human-created or ruderal) were sorted to determine if they were already included in a PHS or NHP Strategic Habitat. The WA Natural Heritage Program published the updated list of U.S. National Vegetation Classification Ecosystems for Washington State (November 2024) which shows the state conservation status (review, sensitive, threatened, endangered) of each US NVC Group and related associations. The Natural Heritage Program guide to identification for those systems will be updated in the coming year(s).
Systems that were not already HGCN were considered. Those with zero SGCN (18 habitats) or only one SGCN were not considered. 
227
 This resulted in 21 additional NVC Groups and GET Functional Groups that support more than one SGCN (Table 
9
); this shows several montane systems and a few ocean systems with many SGCN. Table Note. The following systems in this table are also of potential significance for First Food Sustainability (within Statewide Needs section) -huckleberry, in particular: G211, G218, G220, G241, G305, and G849.
Figure 
27
 highlights in pink the NVC systems from Table 
6
; these are not represented in PHS or NHP 
228
 . NVC Groups with ten or more SGCN identifies nearly every natural-type in the state (Figure 
28
) which provides no distinction: if everything is a priority, then nothing is a priority. Ocean ecosystems are experiencing significant threats due to changing temperatures, ocean acidification, and harmful algal blooms. 
229
 The ocean is a vast, interconnected environment. The GET systems in the table are not distinct waters or zones from the shoreline; these are pelagic layers from the surface where light penetrates the water (epipelagic) to pitch-black and freezing depths. 
230
 These are not systems that fit either PHS or NHP criteria or processes. Ocean waters are influenced by conservation strategies for the Pacific Ocean and Salish Sea at large, which must be national, multinational, and international to be effective. For these reasons, we did not add the ocean GET types to the HGCN list but acknowledge the conservation need.
Montane ecosystems are unique environments with varied temperature and moisture gradients across elevations from foothills to peaks, and often have specialized habitats, high biodiversity, and endemic species. Recent and anticipated changes such as increased temperatures, shifting precipitation patterns that influence snowpack development and persistence, soil moisture and retention, and forest health and wildfire vulnerability are posing severe threats to montane biodiversity at the system and finer habitat scales. Local temperature differences can abruptly shift montane biodiversity and ecosystem services along elevation gradients. 
231
 Montane ecosystem changes need urgent and comprehensive research that can be applied to influence management and conservation strategies in the face of these rapid changes. 
232
 A recent modeled evaluation 
233
 found that many of the montane systems in Washington state at a coarse scale are somewhat protected in areas defined by IUCN I-VI land protection status categories. 
234
 That work also indicated the US NVC expert collaboration could be helpful to advance a finer scale and current condition evaluation to inform conservation strategies within and adjacent to these protected areas.
Rather than create a cut-off number of SGCN (in SWAP 2015, it was 6 SGCN) that elevates a system to HGCN or create a complex third set of criteria outside of the WDFW and WA DNR vetted processes, and to promote the value of system conservation where many SGCN may benefit, SWAP 2025 recommends the following for the NVC systems in Table 
9
 in this appendix:
• be used by conservation community to find common ground in overlapping SGCN conservation need, consider First Foods values (e.g. huckleberry), and include these systems in planning considerations especially in biodiversity areas, protected areas, and any matrix lands that provide connectivity between/among those areas and cores; and
• evaluate using PHS and NHP processes in the next five years, document the findings in both WDFW and WA DNR databases, and include those findings in the next SWAP update to shape the future HGCN list. 
235
 This recommendation is also captured in SWAP Planning Conservation Recommendations section.

## Appendix C5. Threats and Actions

Required Element 3. Descriptions of problems which may adversely affect species identified in Element 1 or their habitats, and priority research and survey efforts needed to identify factors which may assist in restoration and improved conservation of these species and habitats; and
Required Element 4. Descriptions of conservation actions proposed to conserve the identified species and habitats and priorities for implementing such actions.
This section describes the resources and process we used to identify threats and actions for SGCN The following tables support the work described in this Appendix: 

## Standard Vocabulary

SWAP 2025 uses a standard set of threats/issues and actions vocabulary from the Conservation Standards to describe conservation needs for SGCN and HGCN; many SWAPs in the national network use the same set of issues and actions to describe the species and habitat conservation needs in their state.
Using the same framework helps us contribute to understanding national patterns of biodiversity impact and conservation actions that work. This standard vocabulary and set of definitions helps conservation practitioners speak the same language to communicate about the problems they are facing and solutions they could use.
The Conservation Standards threats and actions vocabulary was originally developed in 2008
foot_69
 , updated in 2016
foot_70
 to version 2.0, effectively skipped version 3.0, and just the threats vocabulary was updated to version 4.0 in 2024-2025. 
238
 Our SGCN and HGCN process was fully underway when Threats 4.0 came out and we could not pivot to use the new, revised information. We can explore that between SWAP revisions.
The 

## Selecting Threats and Actions for SGCN

"Threats" in the vocabulary list are not inherently "bad" (think about things like fire or recreation). They become an issue when they adversely affect the status or sustainability of a species or habitat. That's what people need to resolve that issue through conservation action. Conservation actions are how practitioners guide intervention to support a specific desired future condition or positive-progress strategy to address an issue. Conservation actions in the SWAP are not project-level planning; they are useful to identify, guide, and develop project plans, identify dependencies and relationships, pursue funding, and craft new or engage existing partnerships in the SWAP implementation. Issues and conservation actions identified in the SWAP should be well-supported, and actions should have a direct connection to the issue and the species/habitats affected by that issue.
We asked contributors to consider realistic and foreseeable direct threats that can be acted on based on what we know now, those that currently adversely affect or degrade the habitat, and are in most need of conservation attention. Conservation actions should directly relate to the threat to make progress toward resolving the issue and could include priority research or questions that need to be answered
foot_73
 , partnerships, structured decision-making, monitoring to inform effectiveness, and planning. We requested that contributors not include historic threats that are no longer actionable. The issues-toactions relationship could be one-to-one or one-to-many. There may be more than one action per threat.
Other considerations. Do our best with the information we have now to name the issues that pose the greatest risk to species and habitat sustainability. Think about actionable scales. Consider strategies and expertise that already exists to identify near-term actions with strong working groups and practices.
Who or what could help us define and approach 'gnarly problems' for which the action or approach is less clear and needs good thinking to move it forward? Issues should not be a long 'laundry list.' Consider lumping where threats are similar, conducted by the same actors, and strategies to address them could be same/similar.
Level 2 in the Threats vocabulary indicates direct threats. Direct threats limit population, habitat, or system sustainability, resiliency, redundancy, and/or representation. 
241
 Many contributors also identified indirect threats and stressors in comments to expose/briefly explain issues.
foot_75
Direct threats. Direct threats may be considered sources of stress: human actions (e.g., unmanaged or unsustainable fishing, large industrial solar development, pollution release, road construction, utilities operations/maintenance) that immediately affect/degrade a conservation target; natural phenomena altered by human activities (e.g., increase in water temperature caused by global warming) or natural phenomena impact that is increased by human activities (e.g., natural landslide due to human-caused land-clearing activities that threatens the only known location of a springs salamander).
Indirect Threat. A driver of or influence on direct threats that may help us understand the direct threat and where we can apply solutions. Sometimes called a root cause or underlying cause.
Indirect threat example. Clear-cutting or unsustainable logging may be the direct threat, whereas logging policies or rules may be the indirect threat (a different layer where solutions can be applied).
Stress. The impact of the direct threat on the conservation target; attributes of a conservation target's ecology that are impaired directly or indirectly. SMEs may want to identify the stress and related citation(s) in Threat comments explaining the threats.
Stress examples. Pygmy Rabbit (SGCN) -if direct threat is increased frequency, intensity, and extent of wildfire in shrubsteppe systems, then stresses to the rabbits would be significantly reduced forage and poor body condition leading to lower reproductive potential, isolated populations, reduced/limited population size, and increased predation from opportunistic native predators that take advantage of rabbit exposure.
Shrubsteppe (HGCN) -if direct threat is increased frequency, intensity, and extent of wildfire in shrubsteppe systems, then stresses to the habitat may be significantly reduced seed bank and soil moisture for natural recovery, reduced natural soil crust and bare soil exposure benefitting non-native cheatgrass invasion.
Comments for threats and actions in the SWAP are supported by good information and references (e.g. recovery outlines, Periodic Status Reviews, state or federal listing documents, NatureServe threat analysis, working group documentation, models, synthesis). References are most important in threats and actions if they provide different information from the status reference(s). If the most important conservation action is clearly stated in another product, we asked contributors to reference the product but not 'regurgitate' all of the pertinent information in the SWAP. In this way, the SWAP remains a nimble document that can point to evolving and relevant information in other plans that have active working groups or stakeholders or more frequent updates, and this is a good adaptive management strategy. 

## Coordination with Tribes

Twenty-nine (29) federally recognized tribes reside within Washington state of which 21 are treaty tribes and the additional are executive order tribes (or recognized by act of the U.S. Congress). There are several out-of-state tribes that have traditional and ceded lands in Washington, and at least 8 tribal groups that do not have federal recognition within the boundaries of Washington State. The Northwest Tribes and WDFW have an ongoing commitment of respect, unity, and alliance to collaboratively conserve and manage aquatic and terrestrial resources statewide and practice sound science to guide management decisions. Those relationships are documented by the Centennial Accord and re-affirmed with the Millenium Agreement to implement Treaties and achieve mutual goals. Conservation planning and action -such as the SWAP -benefit from the expertise and leadership that Tribes bring to this work.
Coordination with the Tribes through webinar briefings was intended to learn more about their interests and capacity to participate in plan development and improve the State Wildlife Action Plan with information and values from tribes. In the spring 2025 briefing, we heard that the SWAP should include First Foods as a conservation value and Northwest Indian Fish Commission Wildlife Committee reached out to help us start to develop some content.
In mid-September 2025, the small working group and SWAP Coordinator finished the draft content for First Foods Sustainability section in the Statewide and Transboundary section, identified black-tailed deer as an SGIN (need: better understand the effects of land use and land use planning on small localized herds that tribes rely on for subsistence), and highlighted the conservation need of huckleberry for HGCN.
Asking for feedback in briefings and following release of draft pieces did not generate much content. This is not a slight to Tribes, but a fact of limited capacity to respond to the mountain of requests that they receive to participate in natural resources related decision-making and planning. Surveys are not a good method to request information as many tribes work across their communities to discuss concerns and provide feedback, so a survey that goes out widely is not likely to represent a tribes' shared and official perspective. We also heard from Tribes that we could do much better in our SWAP coordination, if we all had greater capacity to collaborate on content development together. These constraints are real barriers to being able to workshop Plan content together in a regional way to better understand conservation questions and needs. We are going to explore how we could create better ecoregionally oriented collaboration and communication with Tribes *throughout* the Plan's ten-year period, to be more relevant, responsive, and inclusive in midstream updates.

## Agencies and Organizations Briefings

We've held several informational briefings and invited more than 200 local, state, and federal agencies and conservation and community organizations. In our last briefing, we shared draft pieces of the SWAP and asked for comments. Additionally, we have benefitted from the engagement of the WDFW Wildlife Diversity Advisory Council review of concepts and content; many of the members are directly associated with organizations that actively do conservation, research, and/or influence the conservation community. To try to get broad engagement, we shared these opportunities with the WDFW liaison network, a set of contacts that work closely with external organizations and agencies to share WDFW updates. WDFW Subject Matter Experts reached out to their communities of practice, conservation partners, and experts from other agencies and organizations to help develop content for the SWAP. For feedback that resulted from each briefing in the meetings and afterward, we encouraged conservation professionals, community and conservation organizations, and others to connect with contacts that they already have in conservation with WDFW so that the SWAP Coordinator wasn't a bottleneck or routearound those good connections. We also provided a SWAP inbox and received quite a lot of feedback through that avenue from plans and data to species-focused interest, and more generalized "please keep me informed" requests.

## Public Engagement

Beyond just the plan review, we asked the public to engage with us to scope during a kickoff briefing, share their connections to ecoregions and resources during the fall 2024 survey, and provide feedback in review of materials shared in spring 2025 and the final draft plan in fall 2025. And, because WDFW Commission committee and full meetings are open to the public, we sought that audience to boost the engagement opportunities. All public webinars were announced on the WDFW Events calendar 
https://wdfw.wa.gov/events
 and the PublicInput project page. Additionally, several individuals who signed up to be notified were sent an email announcing the spring public meeting opportunity.

## Overview of the 2024 Survey

In mid-September 2024 through mid-November 2024 following the summer briefings, WDFW held a survey open for two months to help with scoping. We could have been more focused in our questions, and the great response from participants was a broad cross-section of the state's geography and the public's interest in conservation of natural resources. Several agencies and organizations provided additional plans and information to consider. We were surprised by the amount of response (Figure 
29
) as most WDFW surveys do not receive that much attention.
The number of participants ( 
592
) is a bit misleading; the SWAP Coordinator neglected to clear internal survey testing responses before opening it to the public. Actual number of respondents was 581, which is still a good outcome for a WDFW survey. The way respondents self-identified told us there are many values and interests that all represent conservation engagement in our state (Figure 
30
). Respondents made a clear connection between where they live, work, and play, and the importance of the outdoors and conservation when asked about the ecoregions they are most familiar with and know the best. While we know this is a fraction of the people and communities that WDFW serves all across Washington, we greatly value the participation and gained good information from the comments that is peppered into the plan through species and habitat content, as well as the values we seek to represent.
Many of the habitats respondents mentioned are also WDFW Priority Habitats and several habitats mentioned valuable functions (like pollinator habitats). Nearly 200 conservation concerns were expressed, across all habitat types -shores to the mountains to the Columbia Basin -and at all scalesfrom mal-regulated septic systems to fragmentation of large corridors that connect habitats across political jurisdictions. Another survey summary slice:
• Species -about 110, many reflected in draft SWAP Species tables (April 2025)
• Habitats -about 108, all scales

[TABLE:  Washington state is approximately 43,352,831 acres. Of that approximately 38% is publicly owned and managed, and the Washington state Recreation and Conservation Office (RCO) maintains a Public Lands Inventory, last updated May 2025.40  Private lands with public easements and sovereign tribal lands are not part of this information.While state government in Washington provides stewardship of the SWAP through WDFW, Washington's conservation community includes a broader governmental network of tribes, state and federal resources agencies, and 39 county governments and communities that span from the Pacific Ocean across the Cascades through the Columbia Basin. These entities have natural resources management and stewardship roles in some way.Since time immemorial, Indigenous People have lived in the Pacific Northwest and hunted, fished, and gathered natural resources, traditional foods, and medicinal plants to support their diverse cultures.State and federal resources agencies create resource management plans, gather and curate resources data, synthesize and deliver information, plan and implement conservation actions and initiatives, create and enforce environmental rules and regulations, enhance state-federal cooperation that includes resources management on public lands, and engage and support private landowners in voluntary conservation work through technical guidance, information sharing, outreach, and on-theground support. Approximately 100 state and federal agencies were invited to participate in the SWAP revision (Appendix E). The Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies and the regional Western Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies provides connections to other states and national forums to initiatives, platforms, and communities of practice that are relevant to Washington state and which focus on conservation issues of our time: Wildlife Diversity (for non-game), SWAP, habitat connectivity, pollinators, invasive species, agricultural conservation and private lands, amphibian and reptile conservation, fish and bird conflict, enforcement, conservation outreach, ecotourism, education, and the National Fish Habitat Partnership.]

*• Element 1. SGCN were added and removed reflecting status, taxonomic changes, and emerging environmental threats. State-listed threatened and endangered plants are included as SGCN for the first time in the Washington SWAP.48  Two new SWAP species categories were added: Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN), to signal significant data gaps in necessary species information; and vulnerable concentrations including but not limited to WDFW Priority Species vulnerable concentrations. SGCN distribution is provided by HUC10 watersheds, rather than HUC 10 and HUC12 watersheds used in 2015. SGIN distribution is not provided (hence the need for more information). Vulnerable concentration distribution for WDFW PHS targets are provided at the county level and two others are described more generally. No change to this element in SWAP 2025. Monitoring SGCN and HGCN is conservation action and, where relevant, is identified in the action tables and descriptions meeting Element 4. Effectiveness monitoring is recommended as part of project-level planning. No change to this element in SWAP 2025. At a minimum, the SWAP will be reviewed and updated in ten years, or sooner if partnership, content, or conservation urgency requires it, and will follow contemporary USFWS Guidelines at the time of revision request. * SGCN distributions described in this section meet Required Element 1. This, paired with other information in this plan, guides where conservation actions could occur. WDFW and WA DNR do not have the capacity, resources, or consistent, compatible data across all SGCN to provide a species distribution model for each SGCN. Yet, it is important to provide some geographic boundaries for conservation action scoping and project development in implementation, and to meet SWAP requirements.Similar to SWAP 2015 Appendix B, watersheds are used to define SGCN distribution through range. U.S. Geologic Survey (USGS) and WA Department of Ecology use a national scale of nested water resources basins or watersheds called Hydrologic Unit Codes (HUCs, pronounced "hucks") from the National Watershed Boundary Dataset.59  The bigger HUC number means a smaller watershed (USGS standard).*

*Table 5 . Strategic HGCN listed NVC Groups(WA DNR NHP 2024)   Both PHS and NHP have robust and transparent vetting processes to determine which systems are included on each list. Washington NHP uses the NatureServe methodology to assess relative global and state rank among vegetation systems in the state which informs state conservation status: Endangered, Threatened, or Sensitive due to rarity and/or imperilment. Some of the PHS types were created from the ecological systems that were presented in SWAP 2015 and have a relationship to the NHP rare systems (Appendix C4). All PHS Priority Habitats have "unique or significant value to many species" assessed by several attributes.100  Each process contributes HGCN Strategic Habitats from different perspectives: PHS is focused on service and support for species conservation and NHP is focused on the representation, rarity, and integrity of the system itself. Both provide valuable conservation targets that support species and their habitats. There are no additional Strategic HGCN included just based on SGCN numbers (explanation in Appendix C4: Adding Strategic HGCN section).The following sections describe the SWAP Strategic HGCN that are not DNR NVC Groups. DNR provides a succinct published, well-organized resource for the 38 groups: Table1in this resource U.S. National Vegetation Classification Ecosystems of Washington State (November 2024) that should be used in conjunction with the 2025 State of Washington Natural Heritage Plan for more information. In each type described below, related NVC Groups are identified.*

[TABLE:  Habitats in the Pacific Coast nearshore are influenced by high wave energy, strong tidal exchange, freshwater discharge, and longshore sediment transport. Dynamic and seasonally variable currents drive upwelling in the summer to provide nutrient-rich cold water to nearshore areas (Skegwar and Pearson Geomorphic systems include barrier spits, estuarine deltas, dune systems, rocky headlands, and marine terraces(Skegwar and Pearson 2011, Andrews et al. 2015). Substrate characteristics, ranging from coarse cobble and bedrock to fine silts and sands, determine species composition and habitat suitability for spawning, foraging, and rearing for nearshore species(Partridge 2011).Structural features such as large woody debris, wrack lines, boulder fields, and overhanging vegetation provide habitat complexity and refuge (Skegwar and Pearson 2011). The Pacific coast nearshore supports priority species including razor clams, Dungeness crab, surf smelt, sand lance, juvenile salmon, rockfish, and marine mammals(Partridge 2007, Skegward and Perason 2011). It also hosts Priority Habitats and Species (PHS) vulnerable concentrations including forage fish breeding habitat and concentration areas; marine bivalve concentration areas; breeding concentrations of cormorants, stormpetrels, terns and alcids (Alcidae); and nonbreeding concentrations of shorebirds.Large estuaries within the Pacific Coast nearshore include Grays Harbor, Willapa Bay, and the mouth of the Columbia River (including waters west of the Astoria-Megler Bridge), with smaller estuaries lining the coast. Estuaries are semi-enclosed bodies of water that have free connection with the open ocean, and they provide high-productivity nursery habitat for juvenile salmonids, waterfowl, shorebirds, and shellfish. Policies at the county, state, and federal levels that prioritize invaisve and noxious weed management and provide resources for ongoing treatment and control should be promoted (WSRRI 2024). Growth Management Act, all cities and counties are rquired to adopt critical areas regulations to help preserve the natural environment and maintain fish and wildlife habitat (among a number of objectives). Promotion of integrated methods to replace non-native vegetation with native plant communities that are adapted to fire and can regenerate after fire, will provide better habitat for wildlife and enhance ecosystem resiliency (WSRRI 2024). 7.1 Fire & Fire Suppression WSRRI (2024) recommends improved inter-agency communication during fire response, as well as better communication with and engagement of the public in community fire planning. Broadly, effective outreach and education efforts to foster understanding and support for shrubsteppe landscape conservation, including threats, needed and oportunities, as well as community wildfire resiliency is needed. 7.1 Fire & Fire Suppression Improved planning and execution of fire response and mitigation and community fire protection can help limit impacts from future fires. Improvements in planning should include integration of habitat values into Potential Operational Delineations and Potential Control Lines, enhanced data collection on fires and impacts, and improved wildfire forcasting, communication, data sharing. 7.1 Fire & Fire Suppression WSRRI (2024) recommends development of policies within land management agencies with jurisidction over shrubsteppe landscape. Policy improvements that would benefit shrubsteppe in the context of wildfire include, among others, incorporation of fire management and assessments for restoration actions into Burned Area Emergency Responses assessments, Emergency Stabilization, and analagous state assessment and recovery processes, as well as delegations of authoritiy to incident management teams managing fires. 8.1 Invasive Non-Native / Alien Plants & Animals fragmentation of shrubsteppe. Westside prairie provides habitat for several Species of Greatest Conservation Need, including many state and federally listed species like Taylor's checkerspot (Euphydryas editha taylori), Mazama pocket gopher (Thomomys mazama), Oregon vesper sparrow (Pooecetes gramineus affinis), streaked horned lark (Eremophila alpestris), and Island marble (Euchloe ausonides insulanus; WDFW 2015). Ongoing restoration, prescribed fire, and invasive species control remain essential to protecting westside prairie for these and other prairie associated species.*

HGCN are finer-scale, difficult to map from satellite data because they are difficult to detect within larger habitat types in which they occur. They may be distributed broadly across Washington, but locally uncommon. Usually, these types have specialized function(s), environment, or conditions; may host a suite of rare or endemic species; and are very important to specific SGCN and/or animal concentrations. [TABLE:  ). The report and spatial data released in summer 2025 provide the best platform to view, understand, and act on habitat connectivity needs at this scale. Connectivity conservation actions in the plan are geared towards land use planning, voluntary conservation incentives, transportation projects, public lands, and indigenousled stewardship. Both the report and the spatial data can be accessed here https://wdfw.wa.gov/species-habitats/habitat-recovery/connectivity/action-plan. Terrestrial connectivity at finer scales can be achieved using this product, stepping down the spatial data and analyses to see patterns, accounting for local biodiversity areas and cores, and using other available habitat connectivity resources specific to the area (e.g. Washington Transboundary Habitat Connectivity Scoping Report and the Okanogan -Kettle Subregion Connectivity Assessment 111 , Washington Shrubsteppe Restoration and Resiliency Long-term Strategy 112 ). Vulnerable ungulate concentrations (elk, mule deer) in this SWAP rely on this connectivity to move between summer and winter range across the landscape in regular migratory patterns.]
