September 2025.Drut and Buchanan 2000  neotropical migratory bird conservation concerns, and Partners In Flight Avian Conservation Assessment database. Twelve of the 13 are SWAP Species 117 :• SGCN -Black Scoter, Surf Scoter, Flammulated Owl, Long-billed Curlew, Long-tailed Duck, Red Knot, Sage Thrasher, Sagebrush Sparrow, Short-tailed Albatross; and• SGIN -Black Swift, Olive-sided Flycatcher, and Rufous Hummingbird.Migratory birds are a vulnerable concentration in this SWAP, and many PHS migratory vulnerable concentrations also rely on breeding, stopover, and over-wintering habitats in Washington: Eastern Washington breeding phalaropes, stilts, and avocets; breeding and non-breeding concentrations of waterfowl; Western Washington breeding -Cormorants, Storm-petrels, Terns, and Alcids; Western Washington non-breeding -Loons, Grebes, Cormorants, Fulmar, Shearwaters, Storm-petrels, and Alcids; Western Washington nonbreeding -Shorebird Concentrations. SGCN waterfowl, sandhill cranes, shorebirds, and warblers pause to replenish themselves in Washington during fall and spring migrations, and some groups may overwinter.Certain critical habitats are important in a large landscape-scale way because they are in a sense contiguous for birds (shallow marine, coastal shoreline) and many of the habitat-focused issues are shared across political boundaries (wetland conservation 118 , oak woodland 119 , riparian forests and shrublands). Tidal and freshwater wetlands within coastal lowlands throughout the northern Pacific Flyway have been a conservation priority for more than a decade, and provide critical breeding, migration, and non-breeding habitat for millions of migratory birds each year. Habitat loss has been driven by agricultural conversion, commercial development, forestry, and urban expansion; more recently and into the future, sea level rise and increased flooding and storm surge will dramatically alter coastlines -and how people and wildlife use coastal lands and waters. The Pacific Birds Habitat Joint Venture and Intermountain West Joint Venture both highlight habitats that are also HGCN in the SWAP and important for migratory birds: estuaries, freshwater wetlands, shallow marine habitats, riparian forests, and oak woodlands, to name a few. River corridors such as the Columbia, Skagit, and Snake Rivers provide migration pathways and habitat through a combination of open water, sandbars, and riparian zones. High-elevation wetlands, subalpine lakes, and meadows in the Cascade and Olympic Mountains are also important stopovers for songbirds, shorebirds, and waterfowl migrating through mountainous flyways.]

Land uses, conversion, and human interactions change wildlife's ability to use habitat. Not only related to habitat suitability and connectivity, sometimes wildlife conflict leads to lethal removal of a conflict animal when other deterrence like hazing doesn't work (e.g. elk in cranberry bogs, deer in orchards, black bear in tree farms). While such removals are considered in game population management and season-setting processes that engage Tribes and stakeholders (Game Management Plan process and status and trend reports), other conflicts are related to more localized subpopulation conservation, social tolerance, and land use decisions.]

*Implement and continue to update the 2025 -2030 WISC Statewide Strategy. This multi-partner, multijurisdiction strategy is the state's best collaboration to improve coordination, leverage resources, and advance best practices for this important issue. Extending the Council's purpose and work at least to 2032 is critical for continuity, accountability, and adaptive management. The updated strategy emphasizes integration with watershed and species recovery planning, forest health, and resilience efforts. The strategy acknowledges the need for species-and regionally specific actions, continuing to build explicit connections with regional structures such as WRIAs, salmon recovery regions, and other existing partnerships. Council members also play a role in carrying recommendations back into their organizations, extending impact beyond the limited Council staff's work. Lead regulatory agencies update priority species lists, identify monitoring gaps, and ensure monitoring is empowered and supported. The Council can add value by convening partners, aligning priorities, and promoting tools that improve monitoring efficiency. More community engagement in detection and reporting, such as through organized community science, can feed institutional systems, with a focus on the WISC -Washington Invasive Species Council prioritization matrix, WDFW Aquatic Invasive Species program, and DNR Natural Heritage Program Washington Invasive Ranking System (WIRS) for ecologically impactful invasive plants.Develop a cross-agency process to review those observations and research-grade iNaturalist detections for targeted invasives. To help make outreach more effective and targeted, identify ecoregions and particular HUC10 watersheds in the state where SGCN, HGCN, and any of the 50 high-priority WISC-identified invasives occur and use that mapping effort to target invasive species local jurisdiction and public outreach. * amount of forested land in Washington state has has remained steady over the last decade: approximately 52 to 53% or about 23 million acres.192  Over half the land area of Washington is forested, east and west of the Cascade crest: coastal forests along the outer Pacific-facing foothills and slopes of the Coast Range ecoregion, lowland forests and hills in the Puget Lowland ecoregion, mountain forests of the Cascade and Olympic ranges in the West Cascades and North Cascades ecoregions (North Cascades includes the ecologically similar Olympics), and eastside forests of the East Cascades Slopes and Foothills, Northern Rockies, and Blue Mountains ecoregions.193    Forest management for commercial harvest or ecological conservation influences fish and wildlife habitat suitability, integrity of native forested systems, HGCN representation and function, and sustainability of commercially managed and harvested forests. While some of Washington's forested landscapes are managed for conservation, environmental education, and to protect outstanding scenic and recreational values (e.g. WDFW wildlife areas, WA DNR Natural Resources Conservation Areas and Natural Area Preserves, federal Wilderness Areas and National Parks), much of Washington's forests are managed for timber harvest and wood or biofuel products. Changes in forestry ownership (e.g. residential, commercial, financial asset), tax laws, population growth and trends, harvest technology, markets (e.g. global, local housing), and other uses on these lands (e.g. leases, easements, or sales for hunting, recreation, mineral rights, development rights) all influence timber production, long-term forest ownership and retention of forested working lands, and potential effects on ecological values.Forest management and harvest on federal lands (e.g. US Forest Service National Forests, BLM forested lands) are regulated by the Northwest Forest Plan, which covers 24.5 million acres of federal forest lands in Washington, Oregon, and northern California.194  The NWFP is undergoing amendment 195 and a Draft Environmental Impact Statement was released in March 2025. The Umpqua National Forest is coordinating the effort, with a Record of Decision anticipated in March 2026. From 2023 until March 2025, a federal advisory committee of Tribal leaders, environmental advocates, timber representatives and local government officials met to create a set of recommendations for the amendment to protect endangered species and the habitats they depend on and apply ecologically appropriate forest health and sustainability measures to support First Foods sustainability (e.g., huckleberry) and address issues like wildfire, pests and invasive species, and diseases. They delivered a first set of 192 recommendations in July 2024 which address critical issues such as ensuring Tribes are included in land management planning and implementation, conservation and recruitment of old growth forest conditions and habitat for species that depend on old growth ecosystems and regional biodiversity, sustainable harvest opportunities are provided for rural communities, and fire resiliency is a key component of forestmanagement. 196    Management and harvest of timber, salvaging standing and down wood, constructing forest roads, installing and replacing forest road stream crossings, applying forest chemicals from an aircraft, and other forest management-related activities on non-federal land in Washington, both public and private, are regulated by the state Forest Practices Rules. Ownerships range from individuals and families who own small forest parcels to large holdings owned and/or managed by private corporations and public agencies. In Washington, the average ownership size for non-industrial private landowners (NIPF) is 115 acres, representing nearly 30,000 landowners managing about half of the private forestland in the state.197  The FP Rules are established by the Forest Practices Board and implemented by WA DNR to protect public resources on non-federal forestland.198  The Board has several considerations that lead to Rules:• best available science in aquatic resources and upland wildlife conservation for state and federal listed species and the habitats on which they depend • the Forests & Fish Agreement and the Forest Practices Habitat Conservation Plan (HCP) 199 • integration with Fish Protection Standards (WDFW Hydraulic Code Rules and permitting), and • compliance monitoring and adaptive management requirements Additionally, forest management and other activities on state trust lands managed by WA DNR comply with the State Trust Lands HCP,. This HCP covers northern spotted owls, marbled murrelets, ripariandependent species, habitat for other federal and state listed species, unlisted plants or animals that could be declining in the future, some of the PHS habitats and features (e.g. talus fields, caves, cliffs, oak*

[TABLE: Mountains Ecoregion Figure 15. Blue Mountains Ecoregion ]
[TABLE:  Located in the southeastern-most corner of Washington, the Blue Mountains ecoregion has a large core of public lands, centered around the Wenaha -Tucannon Wilderness with surrounding public lands held by US Forest Service, WA DNR, WDFW (Asotin Creek, Chief Joseph, and W.T. Wooten which together have 10 units), and BLM. The Snake River and Grande Ronde Rivers and their contributing waters are in rugged incised canyons and several stretches in Washington, Oregon, and Idaho are designated Wild and/or Scenic. The ecoregion has a rugged, mostly natural feel, with diverse recreation opportunities (e.g. hiking, hunting, fishing, river rafting, birdwatching). Most of the human population is around the ecoregion edges, and most private landholdings in this ecoregion are in ranching, dryland farming, and orchards. Economic drivers are varied, and most jobs in the region are government (university, state agency), with manufacturing, health care, retail, hotel and food service, and agriculture, forestry, fishing and hunting following. Job growth is highest in utilities (236% in the last five years) with wind power development driving most of that growth.Intergovernmental collaboration is very important for fish and wildlife conservation. The Blue Mountains ecoregion crosses adjacent state borders. It is the largest ecoregion in Oregon (ODFW 2016) and a significant ecological region slicing through the middle of Idaho (Middle Rocky Mountain Steppe -Coniferous Forest -Alpine Meadow Province, IDFG 2024). Additional significant conservation relationships in this ecoregion include several tribes (e.g. Yakama, Nez Perce, Umatilla, Cayuse, Walla Walla) that hunt, fish, forage, and collect natural resources for First Foods and culturally significant purposes. These tribes are connected to a much larger community of Sahaptin-speaking peoples and regionally known as part of the Northwest Plateau tribes from inland areas of the Columbia and Fraser River basins of the U.S. and Canada.]

[TABLE: Species of Greatest Information Need Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN) are also 'Expert Nominations' and a separate table, highlighting needs for basic status information: new/urgent survey, inventory, research, and assessment to help us better understand these species' needs, context, and habitat relationships.The SGIN table may guide external researchers and conservation organizations to generate data and information for WDFW institutional systems that biologists and ecologists should need and be expected to use. The only conservation action available in this framework for SGIN will be "Research and Monitoring: Basic Research and Status Monitoring." 216 SGIN are nominated because we know something of the threats or risks to their habitats or context, but we don't have much/enough data to understand species status or conservation needs. We could not identify all species-related Required Elements for SGIN. Species nominated in this category indicate that a key piece of information is needed to craft more effective management and conservation action in the places where those species occur. This is not a bin for 'nice to have' or 'cool to know.' Conservation practitioners should be expected to use this information in conservation project planning if the effort is made to survey or research these species.SGIN can be considered for State Wildlife Grant funding to determine if they should be an SGCN, but if information is collected, WDFW should be able to identify how we intend to use that information in a conservation effort.The results of this process are in TableD3. Species of Greatest Information Need ListVulnerable ConcentrationsSingle-SGCN aggregations (e.g., western toad spawning/emergence, DPS-specific salmon staging/spawning, single-species bat hibernacula or maternity colonies, pacific lamprey migration/spawning, cliff swallow nesting colonies) are addressed in species-specific threats and actions. This category is for important or vulnerable concentrations that]
[TABLE: may or may not have a specific SGCN relationship and are important because their shared ecological contribution is greater than the individual parts; we may have gaps in single-species information but have strong information about the conservation value of and threats to the collective; and conservation actions for the broader group may be more efficient and impactful than what we could do for the individual species in that assemblage.]
[TABLE: SWAP vulnerable concentrations (or vulnerable animal groups) are WDFW PHS vulnerable concentrations, with three additions: pollinators, migratory birds including seasonal raptor gatherings, and freshwater mussel beds . PHS defines vulnerable concentrations or groups as species or groups of animals susceptible to significant population declines, within a specific area or statewide, by virtue of their inclination to aggregate. Examples include heron rookeries, seabird concentrations, marine mammal haulouts, shellfish beds, and fish spawning and rearing areas. These are described and mapped (Priority Habitats and Species List | Washington Department of Fish & Wildlife and Priority Habitats and Species on the Web). The three additional groups are based on SME recommendations, shared conservation priorities among SWAPs, and deep and wide engagement by the conservation community: pollinators, migratory birds including hawk concentrations, and freshwater mussel beds.The results of this process are in TableD4. SWAP Vulnerable Concentrations]

[TABLE:  Conservation Standards are open-source (Creative Commons license), describe ideal practices for good conservation work, and are used globally for conservation planning, tackling tough issues, and building stakeholder confidence in conservation approaches and outcomes.239  These and many other resources in the Conservation Toolkit have been developed and revised by a community of conservation practitioners over time, supported by international and national conservation networks, and used in many State Wildlife Actions Plans. While we have only chosen to use the threats and actions portion of the Standards, this helps Washington meet another SWAP Best Practice, relate to many other SWAPs across the network, and slice or roll up threats or actions by shared themes across SGCN. In future SWAP iterations, we have good opportunities to use other Standards and Toolkit practices and can explore that between revisions.The Washington SWAP uses the Level 2 Issues (Threats) and Actions from the vocabulary (Tables C5a and C5b linked in this section) to define issues and recommended actions for conservation targets: SGCN, vulnerable concentrations, the habitats that they rely on, and/or the HGCN. No threats are identified for SGIN (not enough information) and only one action is available to choose for SGIN: 8.1 "Research and Monitoring: Basic Research and Status Monitoring."]

Scientific integrity is the adherence to professional practices, ethical behavior and the principles of honesty and objectivity when conducting, managing, using the results of and communicating about science and scientific activities. Not all state wildlife action plans include rare or sensitive plant species because State Wildlife Grants, at this time, do not directly fund plant-specific conservation action. Plant-related conservation actions can be funded by SWG if they are related to invasives that threaten an SGCN, specific vegetation as an important component of a wildlife or fish SGCN conservation action, or if SGCN-related threats could cause significant impacts to the composition, structure, and function of their habitat. Plant SGCN conservation must be funded under different mechanisms.
The "Three R's" framework is usually applied to species, but the concepts can also be considered at habitat and system scales: can the habitat or system adapt to changing conditions, can they rebound from stochastic disturbance without significant intervention, are there other places on the landscape a system occurs that could support habitats and species following a catastrophic event? This is not a plan to catch all of the interesting "nice to know" projects. Conservation actions in the SWAP should be about the most important things we need to do to move the needle for biodiversity.
The "Three R's" framework is usually applied to species, but the concepts can also be considered at habitat and system scales: can the habitat or system adapt to changing conditions, can they rebound from stochastic disturbance without significant intervention, are there other places on the landscape a system occurs that could support habitats and species following a catastrophic event? ## Conservation by Geography

Conservation targets, issues, and actions are also discussed and presented in ten geographic sections: statewide and transboundary, and nine ecoregions. 
107
 Conservation actions are at many scales to benefit conservation targets -HGCN, vulnerable concentrations, and SGCN -and the systems on which they depend.
The statewide and transboundary section addresses key issues and efforts that are working or needed at that larger scale, across the state and with other governments (e.g. 

## Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs

The most important part of the SWAP are the actions that we recommend at many scales to benefit the conservation targets: HGCN, vulnerable concentrations, SGCN and the ecosystems on which they depend. Actions are proposed to address threats or issues that affect the sustainability of the conservation targets. This section identifies statewide and transboundary (i.e. across many ecoregions, statewide, many states, or international) actions to support the durability and effectiveness of the actions taken at finer scales. An example would be development of a riparian conservation policy for the state, which would increase riparian protection and restoration and benefit multiple riparian-dependent SGCN in the ecoregions or watersheds where they occur. The priority statewide and transboundary actions presented here may also be considerations in more localized SGCN and HGCN conservation implementation to improve the effectiveness and durability of site-and habitat-level actions.

## Forest Health Conservation Recommendations

Support the State Forest Action Plan Priority Goals for Wildlife and Salmon Recovery:
• Restore and protect ecosystem health to support fish and wildlife habitat and biodiversity prioritize or recognize to be useful in their work. We have selected the EPA Level III ecoregions as they are widely recognized and used, although not all conservation work in Washington uses this system. The ecoregions that WA DNR Natural Heritage Program uses in the Natural Heritage Plan (2025, page 14) are close to the ones used in the SWAP, but not exactly the same. A few differences stand out but are not too significant for the purposes of organizing our shared conservation information and priorities.
Ecoregion descriptions are not provided in this document. The official original descriptions of US EPA Level III Ecoregions are in the Commission for Environmental Cooperation's North American Terrestrial Ecoregions-Level III (2011) documentation, prepared by the U.S., Canada, and Mexico as a background paper for the spatial metadata. 
205
 Additional good descriptions are provided in the WA DNR Natural Heritage Plan (2025) Appendix J. 
206
 For brief overviews and videos, explore each of Washington's ecoregions and their relationships to iconic SGCN at WDFW's Biodiversity webpage.
Appendix C2 has more information about sorting species information into ecoregions. Briefly, each species was related to a watershed and then watersheds were related to ecoregions. Note, marine species have been manually removed from inland ecoregion SGCN lists for the reasons outlined in that appendix (i.e., some HUCs touch marine waters and extend across lowland ecoregions into mountainous ecoregions -it is an issue we will address in the next revision through better data selection options; see SWAP Planning Recommendations). Ecoregion -HGCN relationships are noted in SWAP Habitats: HGCN section and pulled into each ecoregion section.
Note to Reviewers. We intend to provide ecoregion summaries of some conservation action themes: ideas provided in the threats and actions of this SWAP revision that indicate multiple species in an ecoregion could benefit from similar actions. Given information and time constraints, this will be pursued after delivery in December, inform SWAP implementation and engagement opportunities in the next few years. Following the review period and any comments on this topic, we will update the Reporting Progress and Update Opportunities section before delivery to the RRT.
Also note, there are ecoregional tables mentioned in each section. These are 'slices' of the existing tables that are available for review -D1b SGCN Vertebrates and Invertebrates by ecoregion, D8a NVC Groups by ecoregion, and other HGCN information presented in the document. These summary table slices may change with comments we receive, so only one example is provided for review -the Blue Mountains ecoregion. The other tables are noted for future inclusion.

## Appendix C. SWAP Conservation Targets Processes and Resources

DRAFT online tables will be available during the review period, then final draft tables will be available online on the WDFW SWAP website for the RRT review in December 2025. Tables will be labeled FINAL when the RRT has finished their review.
This appendix contains the criteria, processes, and resources that WDFW used to select/identify
• SWAP species, including Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN), Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN), and Vulnerable Concentrations
• SGCN Distribution (HUC 10 watersheds within species' range of conservation opportunity)
• SGCN habitat association relationships to the US National Vegetation Classification (USNVC) 'Groups' and the Global Ecosystem Typology (GET) 'Functional Groups'
• Habitats of Greatest Conservation Need (HGCN), and • Threats (or issues) and Conservation Actions.
Species-related SWAP content was framed by an internal cross-Program (Wildlife, Fish, Habitat) technical team, then developed by Subject Matter Experts (SMEs) in WDFW and WA DNR, consulting data, information, literature, and their expert and peer networks. The habitat content was also framed by an internal cross-Program team, with some crossover with the species team. The teams valued transparency, repeatability, support by citable information, and having an institutional database for this information that could be referenced, queried, and updated more easily when new information is available.
During scoping, we heard that the difference between SWAP SGCN / HGCN and WDFW's Priority Habitat and Species was somewhat confusing. We aimed to remedy as much of that as possible in the time we had and in line with the purposes of each product:
• SWAP highlights species, habitat, and system conservation actions and partnerships, including data and planning needs; while
• PHS guides land use decisions and many local jurisdictions adopt PHS whole or in part into comprehensive plans and regulations, focused on existing / known species and habitat information to guide avoidance, minimization and mitigation during zoning, infrastructure planning, and other development.
Both are useful to communicate which species and habitats need our conservation attention.
Appendix D provides links to resulting tables separately so that all tables can be accessed from one piece of the SWAP. Additionally, when the plan is approved, all tables will be made available on the WDFW SWAP website. As the agency approves the data behind the SWAP, it will also be made available either on request or through an online viewing tool of some sort. More information on that availability will be posted to the WDFW SWAP website as soon as it is available.

## Appendix C1. SWAP Species Selection

Required Element 1. Information on the distribution and abundance of species of wildlife, including low and declining populations as the State fish and wildlife agency deems appropriate, that are indicative of the diversity and health of the State's wildlife. This section describes how we approached the "abundance" portion of Required Element 1, using status and the criteria behind status as a surrogate.
No species has a "sum total count of individuals" in Washington state, however through surveys, monitoring, synthesis, research, and modeling some species distribution and/or habitat, WDFW and other conservation professionals have good status information for many species. Status is welldocumented, supported by information, and a good surrogate that meets the intention of Element 1.
A WDFW technical team convened to review the 2015 SGCN process, SWAP best practices since 2015, and available information sources many of which had been updated. They developed criteria, processes and tools that a network of SMEs could use to select SWAP species that meet the USFWS and AFWA 2017 SWAP Revision Guidance and Eight Required Elements to " … identify and be focused on the 'species in greatest need of conservation' and address the 'full array of wildlife' and wildlife-related issues." Three SWAP species categories emerged: SGCN, SGIN, and vulnerable concentrations.
Native species and subspecies that are considered taxonomically valid are eligible to be SWAP species. Taxonomic validity 211 means that suspected new species, subspecies, and variants need to be confirmed using the formal scientific process prior to inclusion in a SWAP species Because each species has varying degrees of information and voluntary conservation actions could be beneficial in areas where we don't know if the species occurs on private property but habitat availability is possible, we chose to represent distribution by a geography of conservation opportunity: HUC 10 range, which can be related to the distribution of systems that we used for habitat (Element 2) and species occurrence data. This allows us to also roll up or 'slice' the information by ecoregions and larger watershed scales (such as HUC 8 which is somewhat analogous to WRIAs). This is a repeatable, Plants. WA DNR maintains occurrence and distribution data for SGCN plants and the communities of Washington state in the Natural Heritage Program and uses the NatureServe system for this curation. They provided HUC lists for SGCN plants that closely align with their data sources. They also provide additional resources for SGCN Plant distribution: Online Field Guide to the Rare Plants of Washington | Department of Natural Resources which also provides range comments, a coarse scale map organized around counties, a link to their ecoregion structure, and additional identification information. An example: 
https://fieldguide.mt.gov/wa/?species=pellaea%20breweri
The vertebrate and invertebrate species ranges will be made available visually on the SWAP website when the RRT has completed review.

There is no D5 table online. The tabulated data relating every SGCN to each HUC is a LOT of information, available on request (there are 14,536 closely associated HUC records for all but 1 SGCN; this will be remedied in the next update; there are more than 260,000 records in this data set overall).
WDFW is working on a mechanism to be able to show that information in a graphic format or web service similar to the range figures provided in the SGCN Distribution section. For example, here is an excerpt (not all columns of data) for one species so that plan reviewers can see the level of effort behind the HUC selections. This information will be available in a draft map view service in December 2025 to support RRT review, then finalized when the plan is approved, with the caveats from this plan that a HUC10 selected for a species does not mean the species occurs throughout the watershed. Species are limited by habitat suitability and other factors within that watershed.
For more information about how HUC10s were selected and the limits to that information, refer to Appendix C2.
